## W: W–Wendy's

### W
  
abbreviation. a WC, a toilet  
[NOTES: ca1940 at least traditional children's rhyme version of Old King Cole Old King Cole was a merry old soul/ And a merry old soul was he;/ He called for a light in the middle of the night/ To go to the W.C../ The wind blew round the W. door/ The candle had a fit;/ Old King Cole fell down the hole/ And came up covered in [shit] sweet violets; violets for ladies: Covered all over from head to toe, Covered all over with/ Sweet violets.]  
> **MANSFIELD** `You've only got one w. at your place,' said Miriam scornfully. `We've got two at ours. One for men and one for ladies. The one for men hasn't got a seat.' *(1916,Aloe, The,31,(1982 O'Sullivan))*

### WAAC
  
abbreviation. Women's Auxiliary Army Corps  
> Let me hearken back to that Christmas Eve of 1941 when "WAAC's" were only "Wassies." *(Dec 20, 1943,NZEF Times,III/130 : 7)*  
> <i>W.A.A.C.S</i>. - Ah! Now you're asking! *(1918,New Zealand At the Front,33)*  
> **LATHAM** Changes in Unit Designation did not necessarily correspond with similar changes in New Zealand so that it was near the end of 1942 before the Club girls became NZWAAC (Welfare Division) in company with the VAs who were then NZWAAC (Medical Division). The next year, in September 1943, they changed to NZWAAC (General Division). *(1986,WAAC Story, The,28)*  
> **SAVAGE** The men, whether dressed or in their ordinary clothing, carry a waddy, suspended by a thong from the wrist. The waddy is in figure somewhat resembling a large battledore, and is usually formed of hard black stone, but some are made of bone taken from the head of the whale. This instrument is the sword of New Zealand ... *(1807,New Zealand (various authors/titles),52)*

### WAAF
  
abbreviation. Women's Auxiliary Air Force

### WACC
  
abbreviation. weighted average cost of capital  
> In practice, business uses the "weighted average cost of capital" (WACC, pronounced "whack") as their internal discount rate. *(Sep 6, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),36)*

### wackied out
  
adjective.  
> After <i>Outwaves</i> the lesbians had half an hour to themselves, but by then I was wackied out and went to the Lava Bar. *(Aug 21, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),64)*

### wack-job loner
  
phrase. ??  
> ...she finds herself swiftly cast as a "wack-job loner" by the bitchy women in her lodgings. *(Oct 6, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),62)*

### wacko
  
exclamation.  
See: whacko

### wad
  
noun.  
> <i>wad n.</i> sl. a drink of spirits, esp. whisky; a roll of banknotes. *(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,12)*

### waddy
  
noun. a switch or whip used to move reluctant stock or to protect oneself against a threatening animal [made of supplejack, leather, a thin branch or, recently, a length of alkathene pipe.]  
See: flogger, supplejack  
> MASTERS Back Country Tales 38 The dogs, seeing me with the waddy, and thinking probably I intended using it on them, let the pig go. *(1960)*  
> NZ Farmer Dec 31. 14 .. the length and weight of the blood-stained, butt-end and lead-loaded 'fall' of an old stockwhip .. 'Do you belt the bull with that?' I asked, jerking my thumb at the said waddy. *(1964)*  
> SHARPE Fiordland Muster 75 It was a cudgel, a waddy, call it what you like ... *(1966)*

### waddy
  
noun.  
> HOCKEY   Members of clubs are polishing up their waddies, getting out shin-guards and gloves for the fray of the coming season. *(Mar 22, 1911,Hawera & Normanby Star,2)*  
> This has always been a popular form of sport in Hawera, and records will show what fine teams have played here in the years since first it was initiated, when, it may be added, the fine spring sticks available now were unobtainable, and many of the first players used home-made "waddies" from the bush. *(Apr 19, 1924,Hawera & Normanby Star,4)*

### waddy
  
noun.  
[NOTES: a patu, or carved mere - bone, stone, wood]  
> The waddy, or patoo patoo. *(1837,Description of a View of the Bay of Islands,10)*  
> The Hau Hau Chief has sent into Napier, demanding ... the right thigh bone of Mr. Colenso, for a waddy. *(1868,Punch or the Wellington Charivari,58)*  
> [quoting Wellington Punch] The Hauhau chief has sent in to Napier, demanding ... the right thighbone of Mr. Colenso for a Waddy. *(Sep 26, 1868,Marlborough Express,6)*  
> THE remote forefathers of the Auckland luxury lovers, who struck while they were earning ten times more wages than their immediate forefathers, wore suits of blue paint and carried waddies torn from trees or laboriously fashioned from chunks of rock. *(Nov 22, 1913,Observer (Auckland),2)*

### waddy
  
verb.  
> <i>waddy, waddie n.</i> a native wooden club or walking-sticks; -v.t. to stike with a waddy. [Abor.] *(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,12)*

### waddy
  
noun. (informal) a club or bludgeon  
> <i>Wad.dy</i> (w&obreve.d.d&ybreve.), <i>n.; pl.</i> WADDIES (-d&ibreve.z). [Aboriginal. Thought by some to be a corruption of Eng. <i>wood</i>.] 1. An aboriginal war club. <i>R. Brough Smyth. </i> 2. A piece of wood; a stick; a peg. <i>Vogan. </i> *(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2033)*

### waddy
  
noun.

### waddy
  
noun. a small club; a baton  
> **MEREDITH** [The horse] was given a mile, at full speed ..., with a Maori boy on his back armed with a stout supple-jack waddy, which was vigorously applied from the jump. *(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,138,1935)*  
> **PFAFF** We saw where they buried [the murdered man], so we got good waddies, and every man we met that day we took to be Burgess or Sullivan [the murderers]. *(1914,Diggers' Story, The,113)*  
> `Take that waddy, Mark, and if they don't start off together fetch the slowest [horse] a good clip.' *(1924,Me and Gus,53,1977)* [NOTES: c1924 Anthony Me and Gus (1977 Gus Tomlins ed. Sturm) 53]  
> The [Moriori] men to do the [bird] killing, killing waddy - a short club 18 inches long and 2 inches in diameter - strapped to the waist, and an inflated kelp bag lashed beneath the chin, leap into the sea ... *(1928,BP Bishop Memoirs,Vol. IX No. 5 pp. 343-382) 364)* [NOTES: 1928 Baucke Manuscript in Skinner and Baucke The Morioris (Bishop Museum Memoirs]  
> <i> waddy, waddie n.</i> a native wooden club or walking-sticks; -v.t. to stike with a waddy. [Abor.] *(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,12)*  
> **VAILE** [glossary] <i>Waddy </i>Short, heavy stick: a small club. *(1939,Pioneering the Pumice,299)*  
> **DAVIS** ... possums drop down in hundreds ... and me running about with a bit of a manuku [sic] waddy clobbering anything that moved. *(1964,Watersiders,112)*  
> <i>waddy ... n</i>. abo. war club; walking stick; any thick stick. *(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),21)*  
> **SEDDON** There was a scuffle outside the kitchen door and then a man, a non-Seddonite, banged open the door and fled, an irate Seddonite armed with a waddy chasing him. *(1968,Seddons, The,174)*  
> **HENDERSON** Calling the faithful to prep or parades, he'd belt [the iron strip] with a huge nut-and-bolt waddie two feet long. *(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,152)*  
> **McGILL** <i> waddy </i>piece of wood; Baker suggests possibly pidgin Maori for `wood' as well as pidgin Aboriginal for `club'. *(1999,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,120)*  
> **NEWTON** Each man selected and cut a long [manuka] pole and armed with these `waddies' ... *(1949,High Country Days,93)*  
> **MASTERS** I picked up a good stout waddy, and sailed in with the idea of stunning the animal, ... The dogs, seeing me with the waddy, ... , let the pig go. *(1960,Back-Country Tales,38)*  
> **SHARPE** In other districts I had copied my companions in distress when hill cattle had to be drafted, by arming myself with a stout stick about three feet long. It was a cudgel, a waddy - call it what you like - but it could be used quickly and effectively in an emergency. *(1966,Fiordland Muster,75)*  
> **MOORE** ... to protect himself against a beast that charged, ... he carried what he called his `dream tablet' a large heavy `waddie' which he did not hesitate to use on the head of a dangerous beast. *(1969,From Forest to Farm,106)*  
> Let all have boots with iron plates/Tipped faithfully and well, and waddies/With loaded heads, wherewith to beat/To lifeless pulp each other's bodies. *(Jul 27, 1889,Observer (Auckland),17)*  
> The people jumped into the mass of rabbits so thickly crowded together that it was impossible to move without treading upon them, and made war upon them with waddies. *(Sep 11, 1890,Ashburton Guardian,3)*  
> But say, 'ere, doctors don't creep round back yards with waddies like this up their sleeve. *(Mar 30, 1901,Observer (Auckland),19)*  
> At one ex-railway worKs he was told they had made 10,000 waddies.   When he asked their use, he was told that at one time there were not firearms for all, and they were to hit the foe with.   They were lengths of boiler pipe, with a wicked looking nut at one end. *(Aug 22, 1944,Ellesmere Guardian,2)*

### waddy
  
noun.

### waddy
  
noun. a fisherman's stick or club for stunning fish  
[NOTES: incl battering of birds (penguins), eels, seals]  
> **HISLOP** Sure enough, there was his old waddy [a sharpened willow-stick plunged into the eel] growing out of the back of his Loch Ness eel, . *(1943,Pure Gold,139)*  
> **SHARPE** Full equipment for this sort of fishing [sc. for eels] includes a strong waddy, a butcher's knife, a small sharp axe, and a sack. *(1962,Country Occasions,185)*  
> **SUTHERLAND** You have to stun [the eels] with the waddy and put them in the bag. *(1978,Elver, The,62)*  
> Usually two penguin hunters stood by, one on each side of the cul de sac, holding belaying pins or waddies.   The penguins were knocked on the head, and their bodies were placed in heaps. *(Oct 24, 1900,Wanganui Chronicle,2)* [NOTES: Year is incorrect.]  
> [The seal] was fully eight feet in length and should have been worth capturing, but after inspecting him they procured waddies and "shooed" him into his native element. *(Aug 8, 1907,Taranaki Herald,8)*

### Wadestown wadical
  
noun.  
> **McGILL** <i>Wadestown wadical</i> Yuppie (young urban professional) inhabitant of inner Wellington hill suburb of Wadestown, supposedly representing the Labour Party's trendy lefties (left wingers), slighting in suggestion of lisp. *(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,120)*

### wadie
  
noun.  
> **TAYLOR** We called upon a Mr Wade who has a neat garden attached to his cottage, he introduced mice ... here which after him the natives call <i>wadies. </i> *(Mar 15, 1839,Journal,2/74)*

### wading stick
  
noun.  
> I was carring a wading stick and wearing felt-soled wading boots that enabled me to progress over the stones with nimble impunity. *(Dec 13, 2003,Dominion Post,E10)*

### wae
  
noun.  
See: whai

### waekahu
  
noun.  
> **HOOKER** Waekahu, <i>Lindsay. Lycopodium volubile.</i> *(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),769)*

### waekura
  
noun.  
> **WILSON** <i>Sticherus cunninghamii</i> UMBRELLA FERN ... Tapuwae-kootuku ... Waekura ...Fronds very distinctive, umbrella-shaped on erect brown stalks, sometimes producing an extra one or two umbrellas above the first ... *(1982,Stewart Island Plants,436)*

### waerenga
  
noun. a clearing for cultivation  
[NOTES: aka wairenga, wailenga]  
> **LEE & KENDALL** Wai |enga or Wair|enga: A place cleared of wood and rubbish for a farm; *(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,222)*  
> **BULLER** ... in years gone by occupied by Maori plantations. A new growth covered the long-abandoned `wairengas'. *(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/143)*  
> **GUTHRIE-SMITH**  *(1926,Tutira,92)*  
>  *(1928,Where the White Man Treads,222)* [NOTES: [?in 1905ed]]  
> We remember that monster gathering and great Maori feast at Remuera, while it was yet a Maori wairenga, or cultivation, with the stumps in the ground, now so exquisitely beautiful as the favourite suburban retreat of your merchant princes. *(Jun 15, 1867,Daily Southern Cross,5)*

### waeroa
  
noun. a mosquito  
> Turned out by waeroas about 4 a.m. ... Eat kumara, ti & kuharu. [fn.] The root of the ti - rather like licorice root. *(Dec 20, 1860,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/670,1960)* [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]  
> **WEKEY** Of insects the waeroa, or mosquito, I have seen in my room ... the <i>namu</i>, or black sand-fly, is troublesome in summer ... *(1862,Otago As It Is,67)*

### waewae
  
noun. a skate  
> <i>Raja nasuta</i> Muller and Henle. SKATE, WAEWAE, OR AKAURA *(1930,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,11/103)* [NOTES: (Phillipps)]

### waewaekaka
  
noun. ? a variation on umbrella fern, with the fern rising above the surroundings in total appearance like an umbrella or parasol;  
> **HOOKER** Wea-wae-kaka, <i>Col[enso]. Gleichenia semi-vestita. </i> *(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),769)*  
> **CHEESEMAN** <i> [Gleichenia circinata, Swartz.] Waewaekaka; Waewaematuku. </i> *(1906,Manual NZ Flora,1018)*  
> **WILSON** <i>Gleichenia microphylla</i> PARASOL FERN ... Waewaekaakaa ... Very similar to [tangle fern] ... *(1982,Stewart Island Plants,432)*

### waewaekoukou
  
noun.  
[NOTES: 'owl's foot' aka waiwaikoko, but note that waewaekaka is different being 'parrot's foot']  
> **HOOKER** Wae-wae-koukou, Col[enso]. <i>Lycopodium volubile. </i> *(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),769)*  
> Elegant female head-dresses were formed of ... the graceful Waewaekoukou (<i>Lycopodium volubile</i>). *(1869,TrNZI,1/III/37)* [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]  
> Waewaekoukou. Lycopodium volubile, Forst *(1869,TrNZI,1/III/53)* [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago).]  
> The kumara ... were piled radially around the sides on a bed of soft fern or <i>Lycopodium</i> (waewaekoukou) ... *(1903,TrNZI,35/21)*  
> **SATCHELL** [poss. waewaekaka] The boys on the [Northland] station were getting the big shed ready for a dance ... another young man ... was engaged in looping up garlands of `waiwaikoko' or owl's-foot moss, together with branches of Christmas tree, aflame with blood-red flowers. *(1905,Toll of the Bush,236)*  
> <i>L[ycopodium] volubile</i> Forst., the `waewaekoukou' of the Maori, is probably the most widespread species we have, and the most beautiful. *(1915,TrNZI,47/88)*  
> There is a triumphal arch of pungas at the end of the wharf where the word "WELCOME" is painted in large blue letters, and stretching from this are festoons of waiwaikoko, a delicate kind of fern of a bright green colour. *(May 13, 1876,Daily Southern Cross,3)*  
> Other clubmosses, such as waewaekoukou (<i>Lycopodium volubile</i>), spread along the ground or scramnble through the scrub. <i>Ibid.</i> 77 ...the springy stems and leaves of both waewaekoukou (<i>Lycopodium volubile</i>) and mangemange (<i>Lygodium articulatum</i>) form a good bush mattress. *(2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/72)*

### waewaematuku
  
noun.  
See: waewaekaka  
> **HOOKER** Wae-wae-matuku, <i>Col[enso]. Gleichenia semi-vestita.</i> *(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),769)*

### waewaetaha
  
noun. [Maori] ??  
> Group Maori in the Ministry of Education is seeking to recruit a Waewaetaha for the Atawhaingia Te Pa Harakeke (ATPH) development contract. *(Nov 11, 2006,Dominion Post,H6)*

### Wafer Charlotte
  
noun.  
[NOTES: I haven't come across anything like this anywhere! [hwo]]  
> WAFER CHARLOTTE ... Madeira cake, cut out centre. One white of egg (beaten) ... with icing sugar; put round outside ... put on pink wafer biscuits, tie round with tape; fill centre with fruit ... jelly ... beat cream and flavour, pile on top; take off tape and tie with a ribbon to match your flowers. *(1932,St Andrew's Cookery Book,139)*

### waft
  
verb.  
[NOTES: (whalers')]  
> went out with 4 boats and the Captain Got a large whale wafted and anchored him. *(Jun 2, 1836,Log of Mary Mitchell)* [NOTES: 1836 June 2 Capt. Joy Mary Mitchell log in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 444]

### wag
  
verb. [informal] (also wag it) to play truant or be absent from (school etc.)  
> Up at Wanganui a `kid' has been unearthed who has not missed school for a single half day during the last 10 years. What a splendid record! Fancy not having once `wagged it.' *(Jun 7, 1913,NZ Truth,415/1/7)*  
> **SLATTER** ... he never wagged lectures to go on a pub crawl, ... he never made an exhibition of himself on Capping Day. *(1968,Pagan Game, The,15)*  
> <i> to wag:</i> to cut school *(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use)* [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]  
> <i>wag ... 2. sl.</i> play truant *(1985,Collins Compact Dictionary,539)* [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; nf. 1986 NZPOD]]  
> **GILDERDALE** <i>wag </i>Play truant. M. Alexander's<i> Children's Tales, Written in New Zealand for Little  New  Zealanders</i> (1900): `They played the wag' (page 27). The English slang `play hookey' is still used in P. Garrard's <i>Hilda at School</i> (1929). *(1982,Sea Change,242)*

### wage round
  
noun. the annual period of bargaining for the renewal of industrial and salary awards  
> The two issues - tax reform in an election year and the need for a low wage round - have now become inextricably linked. *(Jun 2, 1981,Evening Post,4)*  
> **BOSTON** [Hdg] The Talks on a Wage-Tax Deal and the 1981-82 Wage Round. <i>Ibid.</i> 196 The main problem during the 1977-78 round occurred in the freezing industry.<i>Ibid.</i> 197 The 1977-78 wage round settled at a moderate level.<i>Ibid.</i> 219 For the first time since 1969 there was no G.W.O. [General Wage Order] during or after the 1981-82 wage round. *(1984,Incomes Policy in NZ,216)*

### wages
  
noun.  
[NOTES: to make wages, meaning extended from goldmining domain to include quarrying, railway work, unspecified labour.]  
> The manager reports that the contractors have ceased work, as the country is so hard in the face that they could not make wages. *(Jul 23, 1912,Thames Star,1)*  
> The principal grievance to-night, before the Mayor, was the impossibility of making wages at the stone breaking provided by the Corporation, the men saying that owing to the hardness of the stone they could not make more than 3s a day. *(Aug 3, 1886,North Otago Times,2)*  
> The co-operative workmen complained of the management of the Woodville-Eketahuna railway works, in that the price had been cut down so that they could not make wages. *(Oct 31, 1894,Feilding Star,2)*

### wages
  
noun. gold roughly equivalent in value to the ruling rate of labourers' hire (cf TUCKER 1; WAGES GROUND)  
[NOTES: (goldminers') esp. in phrase make wages]  
> `Well, they were first comers, and they are on Gabriel's Gully, and making wages.' *(Sep 20, 1861,NZ Goldfields,15,1976)* [NOTES: 1861 Sep 20 in NZ Goldfields 1861 (1976) 15]  
> **HODDER** The claim may not yield sufficient `wages' to pay for lost time. [fn] The term `making wages' is employed to indicate that the proceeds of a day's labour is equal to the amount paid in the labour market [sc. at that time, eight to ten shillings a day]. *(1862,Memories of NZ Life,223)*  
> ... make what, in diggers' parlance, is styled `wages'. *(1865,Evening Post,vol1/no.116/2)*  
> **G.M.WILSON** At last we see the gold in the bottom of the sluicing box and they tell us after about a month's work they have only got three ounces ... - not wages, as they express it. *(Jan 30, 1873,Diary)* [NOTES: 1873 Jan 30 G.M.Wilson Diary in 1973 Wierzbicka Wilson Family 158]  
> **AYSON** Thirty shillings a day was regarded as `tucker' and sixty shillings as wages. *(1937,Thomas,29)*  
> The majority of the diggers are doing well, some exceedingly well, and we do not hear of any who do not make wages. *(Aug 19, 1857,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,2)*

### wages claim
  
noun. [Sport]  
[NOTES: goldmining]  
> **REID** At this rush a `wages' claim usually bore the interpretation of [pounds]8 to [pounds]10 per man per week. *(1884,Rambles on the Golden Coast,68)*  
> **HEINZ** <i>CLAIMS </i>Worthless claims or ground were called schicers or duffers. Those yielding up to [pounds]3 per week per man were known as `tucker' claims; up to [pounds]8 per week were known as `wages' claims; above [pounds]8 were known as `pilers'; the richest of all were known as `Home-ward-bounders.' *(1974,Bright Fine Gold,200)* [NOTES: Glossary of Mining Terms]  
> **MAY** It was a matter of nice judgement. The digger recognized four classes of payable ground: `tucker ground' kept him in food with nothing to spare; a `wages claim' gave him a pound or two above living expenses; a `riser' was a rich claim; and a `piler' or `homeward bounder' the richest of all. Probably most of the `pilers' were beach-claims, where the leads were shallow and rich, the outlay small and provisions cheap because of easy access. *(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,244,1962)*  
> The workings which but the other day were swarming with life and industry now by comparison appear almost deserted;  small wages claims are scornfully rejected, and even shareholders in the rich ground, in the last stage of the disease, have engaged wages men to work during their absence, and started with the rest for the Southern Dorado. *(Aug 12, 1865,West Coast Times,2)*  
> It was well known from the beginning that the lead was only payable, or at best the claims, would be what are known as wages claims but from the magnitude of the preparations made by the holders of some of the claims, strangers were lead [sic] to believe that the fortunate possessors of them had untold wealth at their command. *(Aug 2, 1873,Grey River Argus,2)*  
> At a meeting of creditors of the Hauraki North G.M. Company held yesterday it was agreed that four months time should be given the Company to enable calls to be made and collected, wages claims to be treated as preferential, and general creditors to be paid in instalments pro rata as the money comes to hand. *(May 28, 1898,Auckland  Star,2)*  
> These causes of delay might be borne with more patience if the diggings were somewhat richer than any of those hitherto opened in this part of the country, but on tucker, or small wages ground, people do not bear such disappointments with a very remarkable degree of christian [sic] fortitude. *(Oct 4, 1864,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,3)*

### wages ground
  
noun.  
See: cf payable; tucker-ground; wages  
[NOTES: goldmining]  
> What is termed `wages ground' is always to be found *(1870,AJHR,D-40/6)*  
> **SCANDRETT** Each of these [mining] localities contains what is known as `wages ground'; men can always earn on an average fifteen shillings or more a day. *(1883,Southland and Its Resources,7)*  
> These causes of delay might be borne with more patience if the diggings were somewhat richer than any of those hitherto opened in this part of the country, but on tucker, or small wages ground, people do not bear such disappointments with a very remarkable degree of christian [sic] fortitude. *(Oct 4, 1864,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,3)*  
> The engineer estimates that when the adit is finished it will drain wages ground enough for over 100 men to a depth of over 100ft. *(Sep 29, 1898,Otago Witness,18)*

### wages man
  
noun.  
[NOTES: contrast contract worker cf OED <i>wagesman</i>: Austr.: a man who works for wages 1888 (goldfields quots)]  
> **COWAN** In 1880 a traveller reports [that] One claim was still so rich that `a wages man would be quite satisfied if he was allowed a shovelful and a half of wash dirt for his day's work'. *(1948,Down the Years in the Maniototo,39)* [NOTES: 1880 in1948 Cowan Down the Years in the Maniototo 39]  
> **BELL** `... you would be the right man for the [12] bush country,' McKeown said, `... Flash Harry, who has a contract ... was saying only this morning that he would take on a couple of wages men at bushfalling - so there's a job at hand ...' *(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,11)*  
> They are chiefly wages men, no claim owners abandoning their chances here. *(Jan 7, 1863,Daily Southern Cross,3)*  
> Claims by the wages men were afterwards made in the Warden's Court. *(Aug 5, 1870,Colonist (Nelson),3)*  
> Very few wages men were at present employed in the Thames mines. *(May 9, 1901,Auckland  Star,3)*  
> In this appeal the Chairman said the appellant's partner had been granted a sine die adjournment and thought it might be arranged for one of the two to go and a wages man to be put on the claim. *(Nov 1, 1917,Grey River Argus,4)*  
> The New Zealand miners want to be wages men on less working-time, which would mean less coal. *(May 13, 1921,Evening Post,6)*  
> There were everywhere the first clear signs of inflation, to be paid for by the wages men, pensioners, and those receiving benefits. *(Aug 16, 1939,Evening Post,19)*  
> The State miners had unanimously agreed to a proposal submitted by himself that the whole of the wages men should be organised on a contract basis. *(Dec 16, 1941,Evening Post,8)*

### wagga
  
noun.  
See: also wagga

### wagga
  
noun.  
[NOTES: aka wogga]  
> **CARR** Sheets are not fashionable, while three sacks sewn together make what is termed a `New Zealand Possum Rug' or `Wogga'. *(1913,Country Work,6)*  
> We've got a lot of blankets, and rippin' Wagga rugs - sacks, you know, all sewn up together. *(Sep 23, 1905,Evening Post,10)*  
> **OWEN 7 PERKINS** Another important part of the swaggy's equipment was his bedroll. Generally it was called a `wagga'. Now the old wagga was popular in the wintertime - a bit heavy to carry, but it came into its own in the winter. The wagga rug was made out of the old one-striper sacks... The old one-striper was the fair-dinkum chaff sack, because of course at that time oats and chaff were still used a lot for horsefeed... The old heavy one-striper made a wonderful wagga.The wagga originated over on the Aussie side, and generally to improve it you opened up two sacks, and this made a wonderful rug. *(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,72)*

### waggle stick
  
noun. a stick to frighten whitebait towards the shore  
> **McDOWALL** More common, and also illegal, is the "waggle stick" - just a long slender stick, often with a white cloth flag at the end, which is used to frighten the shoals towards the river bank. Because the law prohibits their use, waggle sticks are usually hidden in the bush or secreted among the timber of the stand. *(1984,NZ Whitebait Book, The,153)*  
> Another piece of equipment you might find useful is a swaggle stick, not that it is legal. Whitebait are pretty stupid, but occasionally they get it in their tiny minds to swim around your net instead of into it. This is when you need a swaggle stick. A pole, five yards long, with a shiny tin lid attached to the end. You stick it in the water and try to scare the recalcitrant whitebait back on course. *(Oct 7, 2002,NZ Woman's Weekly,3)*

### waggon
  
noun. a four-wheeled, horse-drawn, all purpose vehicle for farm transport especially in NZ for conveying milk to a dairy factory as well as feeding out hay or other supplementary food to stock in winter  
> **ANTHONY** Anything you see that you think you'd like, slip into your pockets. If it's too heavy, come back with a waggon later on. *(1936,Follow the Call,99)*

### waggon
  
verb. to drive bullocks or horses in a waggon team.  
> NEWTON Big Country of the South Island 81 He was waggoning at Akitio in Hawkes Bay in 1911. He was also waggoning at Molesworth in 1938 when the Crown took over ... *(1973)*

### waggoning
  
noun.  
> **MASTERS** Mr Ted Bradley, a waggoning proprietor, who had the Puketitiri Hotel at the time, then took over the postal and stage coach business, ... [1906] *(1959,Tales of the Mails,33)*

### WAgMag
  
noun. a newsletter for women in agriculture.  
> NZ Countrywoman Sept 1 WAgMag is the newsletter that grew out of correspondence with women all over the country and now serves as a link between regions. *(1985)*  
> WAgMag 37 Spring 1 Welcome to the 'new look' WAgMag .. Thanks to those readers who answered the questionnaire from the last WagMag. *(1990)*  
> NZ Farmer March 27. 5 WAgMag is a quarterly newsletter for women in agriculture... The Ministry of Women's Affairs is responsible for arranging the production and distribution of WAgMag. *(1991)*

### wagon
  
noun. a vehicle; express wagon so-called was used in NZ (e.g. on grocery deliveries) until replaced by motor trucks c1920s  
> **HENDERSON** WAGGON: Four-wheeled heavy vehicle, having no sides, often used by sawmills. *(1961,Friends in Chains,190)* [NOTES: c1900-1920 A Teamster's Glossary in 1961 Henderson Friends In Chains 190]

### wagon wheel
  
noun. a kind of round unleavened bread  
[NOTES: (transf.)]  
> **HILLIARD** <i>takakau</i> hard bread (`wagon-wheels') *(1960,Maori Girl,48)*  
> **HILLIARD** Takakau, also called WAGON WHEELS a flour-and-water dough, no leaven, rolled out flat and baked in the oven; a sort of oven-damper. A good quick Maori dish to feed a crowd. *(Sep 17, 1988,Personal Communication)*

### wahaika
  
noun.  
> Their war implements of wood were made both of Manuka and Rimu; the curious halbert-shaped Wahaika, the broad Meremere (or hand club), for close quarters ... were made of the former ... Notes (Colenso Maori races) *(1869,TrNZI,1/III/15)*

### waharoa
  
noun. [Maori] an entranceway  
[NOTES: [Ma. <b>waharoa</b> gateway to pa] cf. P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 286.]  
> Widely recognised as one of the most significant historic sites in New Zealand, [Ruapekapeka Pa] has been given a new breath of life with an upgraded track, some core site interpretation and a new waharoa (entranceway) .. the curved waharoa signifies a gateway to the sharing of stories from all sides of the battle, allowing people to enter the pa site in the right spirit, giving it the respect it deserves. *(Jul 2004,Conservation Action,17)*  
> .. Whakatane's Te Whare Wananga O Awanuiarangi are carving a unique Maori gateway (waharoa) that will be auctioned on Sunday at 1pm. *(Jul 20, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,3)*  
> THE reserve price has already been reached and a gateway, or waharoa, carved by Awanuiarangi visual arts students in Whakatane will be sold to the highest bidder on Friday. *(Jul 27, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,1)*

### waharoa
  
noun. [Maori; Marine] <i>Atrina zelandica</i>, horse mussel  
[NOTES: [Ma. <b>waharoa</b> horse mussel <i>atrina zelandica</i>] cf. P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 286.]  
> **LEE & KENDALL** W|ah r|oa; A shell-fish so called. *(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,221)*

### waharoa
  
noun. [Marine] a smelt  
[NOTES: [Ma. <b>waharoa</b> smelt<i> r. retropinna</i>] cf. P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 286.]  
> **BEATTIE** Waharoa is said to be a big kind of paraki, and larger than the minnow. *(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,63)* [NOTES: c1880 South Island Maori Lists `Wahi Mahika Kai' in 1945 Beattie Maori Place-names of Canterbury 63]  
> **GRAHAM** ? paraki = <i>Retropinna</i> sp. a smelt *(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,28)* [NOTES: 1955 Phillipps 28]

### waheene
  
noun.  
See: wahine

### waheine
  
noun.  
See: wahine

### wahi tapu
  
noun.  
[NOTES: also attrib; aka wahitapu, wahi-tapu]  
> A wahi tapu is defined as "a place sacred to Maori in the traditional, religious, ritual, or mythological sense" and a wahi tapu area as "an area of land containing one or more wahi tapu." *(Feb 2000,NZ Historic Places,76/20)*  
> "Wahi tapu permeates all settlements made to date and wahi tapu is included in all claims under negotiation.  [new para]  "People like recreational fishers spokesman Alain Jorion and his followers have no appreciation of wahi tapu and its importance to Maori culture, hence their arrogant display of prejudice to a culture other than their own. .." *(Feb 4, 2009,Gisborne Herald)* [NOTES: http://www.gisborneherald.co.nz/Default.aspx?s=3&s1=2&id=8777]  
> The central North Island tribes of Tainui are also involved in discussions with Ngati Whatua when it comes to Maungakiekie, and all these tribes recognise the place as wahi tapu, or sacred. *(Jan 14, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),36)*  
> The heads will be brought direct to Te Papa, where they will be stored in a wahi tapu (sacred vault). *(Jul 2, 1998,Dominion,1)*  
> Wahi tapu, or Maori covenant, is registered over private land in Tauranga. *(Dec 3, 2002,Dominion Post,B5)*  
> It's now a wahi tapu, it's got the essence of the man there. *(Aug 14, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),33)*  
> **KEENE** <i>wahitapu</i>: sacred burial ground. *(1963,Pioneer Recollections,195)*  
> **ELDER** `In selecting a portion of land for a settlement, it would be advisable to take care that it be as clear as possible of what the natives call the wahhe tabboo (<i>wahi tapu</i>). Wherever a person has breathed his last, or his bones have been laid for a time, there is always a piece of timber set up, if there is no tree already growing, to perpetuate his memory. This wahhe taboo is not suffered to be molested, and is held sacred both by friends and strangers. *(1934,Marsden's Lieutenants,140)* [NOTES: 1817 Jul 25 Kendall in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 140]  
> **LEE & KENDALL** W|ahi t|apu; A sacred place. *(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,221)*  
> **WILLIAMS** Upon enquiring it appeared that they were on a <i>Wahitapu</i> and consequently could not be driven home . *(Jun 22, 1828,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,136,1961)*  
> **WILLIAMS** About 10 at night Tohitapu very vociferous on account of one his [sic] <i>Wahitapu</i> (sacred spots) having been invaded by some of the lads belonging to the Settlement. *(Dec 10, 1832,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,267)*  
> **POLACK** .. he informed me that both fire and water was tapued by the tohunga, or priest, who was busy at preparing an incantation near the Wai-tapu [a2-acute], situated among a grove of cabbage palm-trees without the fence. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/137 We passed an extensive grove containing a Wai-tapu [a2-acute]. In this place was deposited the bones of a male and female chief ... The house which enclosed these remains of mortality was built of old canoes, ... [138] It was surmounted with a <i>maihi</i>, or frontispiece, which was decorated with feathers. *(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/130)*  
> **POLACK** The Waitapu of a native village is generally selected amid a cluster of trees, among which the <i>karaka</i>[a3-acute] (a laurus) fruit-tree, is generally found; of course the fruit is prohibited from being eaten. *(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/120)*  
> **DIEFFENBACH** All these places, wahi-tapu (sacred places), as they are called generally, or papa tupapakau (a coffin for the corpse), if it is a monument, are strictly sacred; ... *(1843,Travels in NZ,2/65)*  
> **SUTHERLAND** For similar reasons, a slave or other person not sacred would not enter a `wahi tapu' or sacred place, without having first stripped off his clothes; ... *(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,293)*  
> ... the `Wahi tapu,' or `sacred place,' has been desecrated; ... *(Aug 15, 1857,Lyttelton Times,3)*  
> **MANING** Then half the country claimed payments ... on account of a certain <i>wahi tapu</i>, or ancient burying-ground, which was on the land, ... *(1863,Old New Zealand,78)*  
> **ANGAS** The clothes and property of a deceased chief are usually buried with him; or they are collected together, and placed in a `wahi tapu,' or sacred enclosure, surrounded with a fence, where they rot away, exposed to the winds and the weather. Hiriwenna, having been buried only six weeks, the ornaments of the `wahi-tapu,' or sacred place, as these erections are termed, remained fresh and uninjured by the weather. *(1866,Polynesia,150)*  
> **TAYLOR** Near the same place was a <i>Wahi tapu</i>, sacred grove, into which formerly they had been accustomed to draw the spirits of those who died, by uttering some of their powerful [60] spells, so that they might not wander about and injure the living; ... *(1868,Past and Present of NZ,59)* [NOTES: [cf also Polack NZ 1838]]  
> **BAINES** The particular spot of ground where the canoe was beached has been a Wahi Tapu (sacred place) ever since ... *(1874,Edward Crewe,27)*  
> **TREGEAR** Any fragments left by the chief were carefully placed in the basket and deposited in the sacred place (<i>wahitapu</i>). <i>Ibid.</i>198  Each village had a piece of ground (<i>wahitapu</i>) reserved for placing thereon <i>tapu</i> property, such as scraps of a chief's food, clothing, etc. <i>Ibid.</i> 283 The sacred portion (<i>wahi-tapu</i>) of a village was a place set apart from common uses, generally just outside the <i>pa</i> and usually fenced off. ... In more modern times the wahitapu has become a cemetery. *(1904,Maori Race, The,112)*  
> **COWAN** ... the other or seaward side was bounded by a little knoll of a cape, the <i>wahi-tapu</i>, or holy place of the <i>pa</i>; ... *(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/117)*  
> **LYSNAR** The remains of the dead and all connected therewith, were, and are, extrememly tapu, and certain places, such as [88] wahi tapu (burial grounds) are similarly sacred. *(1924,New Zealand (various authors/titles),87)*  
> **MIKAERE** A god called Karutahi (One Eye) had revealed to Te Ito that the reason why so many Maori were dying was that the people no longer knew where the wahi tapu (sacred places) were *(1988,Te Maiharoa,39)*  
> Under the proposed legislation, the Marine and Coastal Areas Bill, iwi will have the ability to prohibit of restrict access to wahi tapu areas. *(Oct 21, 2010,NZ Herald,A9)*  
> The action was taken by a group of Ngati Awa who did not want any development on the land, arguing that it was wahi tapu and part of the ancient Opihi Urupa. *(Nov 2, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,4)*  
> The springs are waahi tapu. A sacred place. We see them as the source of wairoa, the life force associated with water. *(May 15, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),33)*  
> Real estate developments have been built throughout New Zealand on waahi tapu or sacred ground. *(Jul 24, 2000,Evening Post,TV Week : 15)*  
> The Mahia Heritage Group claims that ancient pa and wahi tapu (sacred sites) are being destroyed... The site has special significance for Maori, too, but without Cook let's assume that a few wahi tapu wpould not have been enough to carry the day. *(Aug 3, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24)*  
> District and regional councils... can identify and protect significance landscapes, heritage and cultural sites (waahi tapu). *(Jul 20, 2002,Weekend Herald,B7)*  
> The funeral is to take place to-morrow, and the body buried at Wahi Tapu at Taupiri. *(Dec 31, 1863,Daily Southern Cross,5)*  
> I was standing in the wahi tapu or sacred grove to receive the offerings of the warriors. *(Feb 7, 1882,Auckland  Star,1)*  
> Maori Wahi Tapu (burial place) at Puketoua, Bay of Islands. *(Jun 3, 1891,Evening Post,3)*  
> Rising picturesquely in conical form in the centre of this sheet of 35 acres of water is a small island, which once was wahi tapu, or native burial ground, sacred to the remains of native chieftains. *(Mar 23, 1914,Evening Post,6)*  
> **HULME & MORRISON** The graveyards - urupa, wahi-tapu - are numerous. *(1989,Homeplaces,65)*  
> The remains are expected to be returned within 12 months and will be stored in a wahi tapu area of Te Papa while their iwi links are researched. *(Jun 26, 2004,Dominion Post,A9)*  
> Under the proposed legislation, the Marine and Coastal Areas Bill, iwi will have the ability to prohibit or restrict access to wahi tapu areas. *(Oct 21, 2010,NZ Herald,A9)*  
> In the meantime, local Maori claimed the entrance to be an ancestral burial cave and declared it wahi tapu, effectively off-limits to everyone else. *(Apr 1997,NZ Geographic,No. 34/46)*

### wahid
  
noun.  
> We are sitting in a bar thinking about having wahid for the sharia when suddenly my friend becomes very distracted. *(Aug 30, 1943,NZEF Times,III/114 : 10)*

### wahine
  
noun. a Maori woman or wife  
[NOTES: (often preceded by the aj pl usu. in -s <i>Maori,</i>) 1960 17C M B8 Wanganui Girls C.I 28 WAHINE (USED BY BOTH MAORI AND PAKEHA)]  
> Northern Southern Otahite a Woman Wahine Wahine Wahine Come here Horomai Horomai Harromai Fish Heica Heica Eyca a lobster Kooura Kooura Tooura Sweet potatoes Cumala Cumala Cumala *(Mar 1770,Banks Journal,2/35-36,1`962)*  
> <i>English New Zeland South-sea Islands</i> A Chief Eareete Earee A Man Taata Taata A Woman Ivahino Ivahine *(Mar 31, 1770,Cook's Journals,1/286,1955)*  
> **McNAB** Their Whinies (or women) are not regular featured in general as the men, tho' some of them were fine jolly girls. *(1908,Historical Records,2/204)* [NOTES: 1773 Apr 12 W. Bayly's Journal (Cook's Second Voyage) in 1908 McNab Historical Records 2/204]  
> **FORSTER** A Comparative Table of the Various Languages in the Isles of the South-Sea ... [English] <i>A Woman</i> ... [New-Zeeland] Waheine [a-macron, e1-grave] *(1778,Observations Made During a Voyage,284)*  
> **COLLINS** Wha-hei-ne[acute], <i>A woman. </i> *(1804,English Colony in NSW,562)*  
> **SAVAGE** Wyeena .. A woman. *(1807,New Zealand (various authors/titles),74)*  
> **NICHOLAS** A woman Whyeenee *(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/341)*  
> **McCRAE** Pomarre[acute] .. .. Rungatyeeda no te Kaaingha E Wharre[acute] .. .. Whaheene[acute] no Pomarre[acute]. Poaka .. .. Tamyte[acute] no Pomarre[acute]. ...  E Po .. .. Koeteedo no Pomarre Whaheene no Tenana. *(1920,Journal,27,1928)*  
> **LEE & KENDALL** Wah|ine; A woman; a female partner. *(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,221)*  
> woman - wa|heine [wahine] *(1835,Boultbee Journal,109,1986)* [NOTES: SEE ALSO 1826-28 Boultbee Vocabulary in 1979 Begg 282]  
> **MARKHAM** [The Chief] asked me if I had a Waheinee [<i>wahine</i>] ? No. Would I like one? Certainly. Then take my daughter. Which I did in New Zealand fashion. *(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),41,1963)*  
> **BAILEY & ROTH** Farewell to Tonga - Mowries and wyenas also,/ *(1836,Shanties,19,1967)*  
> ... wienas or wives. <.I>|Ibid.</i> 2/32/94 ... winena ... *(1841,NZ Journal,2/32/92)*  
> **BIDWILL** At this village I saw a woman so very light that she had a perceptible colour in her cheeks. I intended to say to her, jokingly, that whe was a white woman (Whaiheinie Pakiha), not a New Zealander (Waihini Mowrie): however, ehe understood me to ask her whether she would be my waihini, <i>i.e</i>., my wife, and immediately came over to the mouth of the tent where I was sitting, and seated herself beside me - ... *(1841,Rambles in NZ,72,1952)*  
> Give to Richards Whaheen 10 regatta shirts, ... and 5 pairs of lambs-wool hose to get daubed. *(Jul 14, 1842,Piraki Log,139,(ed. Anson 1911))*  
> **WAKEFIELD**  *(1845,Adventure in NZ,vol. i. p. 29)*  
> They inquired for the <i>waihina</i> and I led the party up to the house to introduce them to my wife, . *(1940,Establishment of New Plymouth Settlement)* [NOTES: c1846 Weekes My Island in 1940 Establishment of New Plymouth 119]  
> **McKILLOP** The men do not shew the same desire to intermarry with white women, as the white men do with the maori whihienes (women), who make the working classes far better wives ... than any Europeans, ... *(1849,Reminiscences,252)*  
> **POWER** In the interior of the Pa the Wahines[e-acute], or matrons, are busy weaving flax-mats, cleaning potatoes or fish, or engaged in the superintendence of a Maori oven, or a huge gipsy-looking cauldron, called a `go-ashore', and can only afford to greet the visitor with a whining `tena koe pakeha', sighing as though they were very much to be pitied. *(1849,Sketches in NZ,160)*  
> **MUNDY** A group of whyenees and piccaninnies .. *(1852,Our Antipodes,289,1855)*  
> **ROCHFORT** The wahines (women) came out and greeted us with a shout of welcome. The old chief led me into his whare, where I was received by his wife, and the petty chiefs and women of the tribe. *(1853,Adventures of a Surveyor,35)*  
> **BAINES** The New Zealand `Wahini' has a pattern of her own ... *(1874,Edward Crewe,19)*  
> **PRATT** Maori waihine or native woman ... *(1877,Colonial Experiences,2)*  
> **MORRIS** <i>Wahine, n. </i>Maori word for a woman. The<i> i</i> is long. *(1898,Austral English,493)*  
> At present 12 <i>wahines </i>are employed at this work. *(1903,AJHR,G1/4)* [NOTES: (Lands Report)]  
> In vain did the auctioneer solicit bids but `two bob' from a Maori wahine was all that was forthcoming. *(Feb 10, 1906,NZ Truth,34/5/8)*  
> **MANSFIELD** ... met a most fascinating Maori ... he took Mrs Webber and me to see his `wahine' - and child *(1907,Urewera Notebook,44,(1978 Gordon))*  
> It [The WCTU] runs a costly mission amongst the Maori women of the Dominion, cadges funds for the purpose, and sends talkative members to disturb the simple wahines who are content to live the natural existence of their primitive ancestors. <i>Ibid.</i>. 244/4/5 Maori women, as a class, don't drink ... The law protects the wahine more effectually than the W.C.T.U. can ever hope to do, as it prosecutes any person who supplies her with beer, ... *(Feb 26, 1910,NZ Truth,244/4/3)*  
> **PFAFF** Tainui's wahine was a splendid fisher. *(1914,Diggers' Story, The,42)*  
> **COWAN** We had a couple of pig dogs with us, Maori kuris, which Hursthouse had borrowed from an old wahine at Te Kuiti ... *(1916,Bush Explorers,19)*  
> Incidentally, the wahines and `Picanninis' invariably joined in the free-for-all Donnybrook with which [the Maori football match] ended. *(Jun 10, 1920,Quick March,41)*  
> **GUTHRIE** This Maori boy's <i>wahine</i> called upon the wife of her husband's pakeha to beg ... European clothes. *(1930,NZ Memories,49)*  
> **PINFOLD** A Maori wahine (woman) observed a Pakeha housewife carefully starching some linen cloths. as a general expression for a woman... *(1930,Fifty Years In Maoriland,31)*  
> **BEATTIE** An American whaler who had two sons by a Maori woman got tired of her ...When he was dying he was greatly troubled about the way he had treated the wahine and her two halfcaste sons ... *(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,24)*  
> <i>wahine</i>, woman; wife. *(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/160)* [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]  
> <i>wahine: woman. </i> *(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,176)*  
> **SCRYMGEOUR** The Maori buck did the shearing, with machines, boys penned, and did pick-up work. Wahines ... did the sorting, and Maori girls did the sweeping on the shearing board. *(1960,Memories of Maoriland,110)*  
> <i>wahine n -s [Maori & Hawaiian] Hawaii & NewZeal </i>: WOMAN, WIFE, SWEETHEART, MISTRESS *(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,2569)*  
> **KEENE** <i>wahine:</i> woman. *(1963,O Te Raki)*  
> **BRAITHWAITE** WAHINE Wife, woman. *(1967,Evil Day,382)*  
> **MASON** HINGAWARU ... When Rangi chooses his wahine, someone else pays, ne? RANGI: When I choose, Dad, I'll choose a <i>girl</i>, not a wahine! Hate that word! Makes me see fat slobbery old tarts waddling along the beach. *(1974,Hand on the Rail, The,20,1981)*  
> <i>wahine n. 1. N.Z.</i> a Maori woman. 2. a Polynesian woman. [< Maori & Hawaiian] *(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,1322)* [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 867; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 539]]  
> I was often afterwards navigated across it on the back of a Maori wahine when none of the male sex were at hand. *(1881,Poenamo,Bk2/1/48)*  
> **BAUCKE** When we came to a native encampment, / Enlivened by wahine chatter, / Preparing the bounteous haangi. *(1905,Where the White Man Treads,104)*  
> The annual evening devoted to the entertainment of their wahines by our local  Savages  is always a successful function. *(Aug 7, 1935,NZ Free Lance,6)*  
> The Natives generally - King's as well as Queen's men - have throughout our protracted stay been very courteous to every one of the British visitors;  and hardly a day has passed without one or other thoughtful wahine bringing a well baked loaf or kit of potatoes, c., to some one of the guests. *(Jun 19, 1860,Wellington Independent,3)*  
> Sandra Lee is in line for a job-for-the-wahine as New Zealand's representative on the International Whaling Commission. *(Jul 28, 2002,Sunday Star Times,A11)*  
> One elderly wahine said to me: "Maori unhappy." *(Jun 8, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,7)*

### wahine
  
noun. a pakeha woman, wife  
[NOTES: (in English contexts, occas. jocular)]  
> **GODLEY** ...  he would ask the <i>wahina</i> (mneaning me) how much she would do them for, and so I undertook them at twopence a shirt *(1951,Letters from Early NZ,00)* [NOTES: 1852 quote - check page number]  
> **MAY** In fact a small Christchurch and the night that I arrived there was a grand Ball given on the opening of a fine large Billiard room where you might have seen whienas [wahines] both Maori and Pakeha in crinolines and satin boots. *(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,71,1962)* [NOTES: 1856 James Hammet in 1967 May West Coast Gold Rushes (1962) 71]  
> **ASKEW** I was told ... that the `wyeenas' (women) ... frequently fed the sickly ones . *(1857,Voyage to Australia & NZ,348)*  
> It is not fit that the daughter of the great tribe should be the slave-wife of the white wahine. *(Dec 21, 1893,Otago Witness,11/5)* [NOTES: (Morris)]  
> Warriors, Wahines, Kuri, Poaka, Tamariki, Kumeras, Karos, Interpreters (1st, 2nd and 3rd grade), Poets Laureate, Poi Dancers, Guides, &c. on application to the Registrar. ...WAIPIRO - - An acursed drink ... TAIHOA - - The Police have the matter in hand POAKA - -a pig PUKEKO A bokakker out of season KORERO A long talk UTU - - The spike HAPU - - A Tribe KUMERA - - A sweet Spud *(1913,Auckland University Carnival Book,27)*  
> **PARTRIDGE** <i>Wahine. </i>A woman: New Zealand coll.: late C. 19-20. Direct ex Maori. Cf. <i>lubra. </i> *(1937,Dictionary of Slang,935)*  
> **BAKER** We have long since adopted wahine *(1941,NZ Slang,56)*  
> **SOLJAK** New Zealand colloquialisms which are of Maori origin include; ... <i>wahine: </i>woman *(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),117)*  
> <i>wahine n.</i> a woman [Maori]. *(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),628)*  
> <i>wahine n. </i>a woman. [maori] *(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,132)*  
> <i>wahine n. </i>a woman. [maori] *(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,132)*  
> wahine The Maori word for a woman. *(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),43)*  
> <i>wahine ... n.</i> woman [Maori]. *(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),21)*  
> **WILSON** Skiting to everyone about his groovy wahine, then won't speak to me when we get home. <i>Ibid.</i> 51 `I want you to meet a friend of mine, Cynthia Black. Meet Joff Smith, and my wahine, Martha Whitwell.' *(1984,South Pacific Street,48)*  
> **McGILL** <i>wahine</i> woman, often desired or seen as girlfriend or wife; from Maori, colloquialism late C19. *(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,120)*  
> Some one told them that this was "wahine's" luggage, and they therefore left it;  by no means from gallantry, but from hopelessness of fire-arms. *(Sep 13, 1862,Taranaki Herald,3)*  
> ".. All the same the Pakeha Wahine." *(Dec 11, 1903,Observer (Auckland),5)*  
> He would, he assured the wahines, which he carefully explained meant ladies, that he would have been only too pleased to explain the mysteries that the Chief [of the Savage Club] desired to have so jealously guarded. *(Aug 15, 1913,Poverty Bay Herald,7)*  
> **EDMONDS** Molly sitting by the fire like an old wahine, reading and chewing dates. *(1989,Hot October,161)*

### Wahine Day
  
noun. 10 April 1968, when the interisland ferry Wahine foundered in Wellington Harbour  
> For those of us old enough to remember, the very words "Wahine Day" prompt strong and disturbing memories. *(Aug 3, 2001,Evening Post,9)*  
> In New Zealand, "Wahine Day" emergedspontaneously, just like that, on April10, 1968. *(May 9, 1993,NZ Tablet,17)*  
> I spent most of my childhood wishing that we would have a storm bigger than Wahine day. *(Jun 11, 2002,Evening Post,6)*

### Wahine disaster
  
noun.  
See: Wahine Day  
> At the hospital, the extent of the Wahine tragedy in which 51 people lost their lives was soon apparent when the survivors began arriving - at first in a trickle and then a flood. *(Aug 15, 2002,Dominion Post,B6)*  
> The Historical Medal Society of Australiaand New Zealand is to strike a specialmedal to commemorate the Wahine disasterin Wellington Harbour on April 10. *(Aug 9, 1968,Evening Post,10)*

### wahine Pakeha
  
noun. [Maori] a European woman  
> **J.C. BIDWILL** At this village I saw a woman so very light that she had a perceptible colour in her cheeks. I intended to say to her, jokingly, that she was a white woman (Whaiheinie Pakiha), not a New Zealander (Waihini Mowrie): however, she understood me to ask her whether she would be my waihini, i.e., my wife, and immediately came over to the mouth of the tent where I was sitting, and seated herself beside me - ... *(1841,Rambles in NZ,72,1952)*

### Wahine scale
  
noun.  
> The old Beaufort scale of wind force hasbeen unofficially replaced in Wellingtonby the Wahine scale. Gales in Wellingtonare met by comments such as "It's not asbad as the Wahine storm" or "We haven'thad a day like this since the Wahine." *(Apr 10, 1993,Sunday Times (NZ),8)*

### Wahine storm
  
noun.  
> The front verandah was blown off during the Wahine storm. *(1987,Alfredton,220)*  
> **KNOX** "It would have to be Wahine-storm weather for them not to clear the slip off in the morning,' Hannah said. *(1987,After Z-Hour,66)*  
> Then came the <i>Wahine</i> storm of 1968, and the building began to fall apart. *(Dec 4, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32)*  
> [Black swan] numbers in Lake Wairarapa and in Lake Ellesmere declined dramatically after the Wahine storm in 1968, which was so violent it destroyed the weedbeds on which the swans fed and their numbers have never really recovered. *(Aug 9, 2003,Dominion Post,F8)*  
> The old Beaufort scale of wind force hasbeen unofficially replaced in Wellingtonby the Wahine scale. Gales in Wellingtonare met by comments such as "It's not asbad as the Wahine storm" or "We haven'thad a day like this since the Wahine." *(Apr 10, 1993,Sunday Times (NZ),8)*  
> [Caption] In 1968 disaster struck thecollege in the shape of the Wahine storm,and many of the fine old trees in thegrounds were blown down. *(1981,St Patrick's College, Silverstream 1931-1980,40)*

### Wahine weather
  
noun. a bad storm  
> [Hdg] Wahine weather. A storm of near Wahine proportions has brought the lower North Island to a virtual standstill. *(Aug 19, 2004,Dominion Post,A1)*

### wahu
  
noun. sundew

### wai
  
noun. water (widely understood by English speakers but rare in English contexts other than placenames)  
> Pricey undies, fraudulent CVs, and dodgy human resources are mere wai under the bridge. *(May 2, 2004,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),29)*  
> **PARKINSON** A Vocabulary of the Language of New Zealand. [...]  Egooree, <i>A dog</i>. Teyka,<i> Fish. </i> Hewhai[a-grave], <i>A skate</i>. Eraperape, <i>A fish called Chimaera. .</i>.. Hewai, <i>Water.</i> [...] Tochee,<i> A hatchet, or adze. </i> Hecacahoo, <i>A garment. </i> *(1770,Journal,126)*  
> **FORSTER** A Comparative Table of the Various Languages in the Isles of the South-Sea ... [English] <i>Water </i>... [New-Zeeland] Evai [a-macron grave] *(1778,Observations Made During a Voyage,284)*  
> **SAVAGE** Whey .. Water. *(1807,New Zealand (various authors/titles),77)* [NOTES: (Maori Vocabulary)]  
> **LEE & KENDALL** Wai, s. water; *(1920,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,221)*  
> water - wa'i [wai] *(1826,Boultbee Journal,111,1986)* [NOTES: ALSO IN 1979 Begg 284]

### wai
  
noun.  
See: whai

### waiariki
  
noun. [Maori] a warm thermal spring or pool  
> The word Puia is especially used in the Taupo country, to designate the intermittent, geyser-like fountains of Tokanu ... and of Whakarewarewa on Lake Rotorua. Puia has ... the more general meaning of crater or volcano, and is applied to active as well as extinct volcanoes. ... Ngawhas [is] a term specially used for non-intermittent springs, for the solfataras and sulphurous hot-springs on the Rotomahana, Rotorua, and Rotoiti. Finally, the springs suited to bathing purposes, the water of which never reaches the boiling point, and all naturally warm baths are called `Waiariki,' corresponding to the Laugar of Iceland. *(1870,TrNZI,2/171)*  
> **BULLER** Hochstetter divides the steam jets into three kinds:  1. <i>Puias</i>, which are geysers, continuously or intermittently active.  2. <i>Ngawas</i>, which are inactive <i>Puias</i> - emitting steam, but not throwing up columns of water.  3. <i>Waiarikis</i>, which mean any sort of cistern of hot water suitable for bathing. *(1878,Forty Years in NZ,11)*

### waiata
  
noun. [Maori] a (Maori) song  
> Through the years she became an authority on weaving, and was also respected for her familiarity with classical waiata that recounted Arawa and Mataatua history. *(Dec 1998,Mana,7)*  
> **SAVAGE** Wyata .. Singing. *(1807,New Zealand (various authors/titles),75)*  
> **LEE & KENDALL**  *(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,221)*  
> **LEE & KENDALL**  *(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,221)*  
> ... for so accommodating are their tunes and metres that they now set the Catechism to them and thus, when they join us, still find a substitute for their ancient <i>waiata. </i> *(Jul 30, 1839,Report)* [NOTES: 1839 July 30 report in Wily and Maunsell Robert Maunsell (1938) 54]  
> **DIEFFENBACH** E' Waiata is a song of a joyful nature; E' Haka, one accompanied by gestures or mimics; E' Karakia is a prayer or an incantation, used on certain occasions, - ... *(1843,Travels in NZ,2/57)*  
> **TAYLOR** In the afternoon we had a long conversation with Heuheu, his acquaintance with their ancient traditions, waiatas, karakias and the number of them is surprising. *(Nov 15, 1845,Journal,3/172)*  
> **POWER** A column ... will be devoted to ... music and the fine arts ... to substitute other songs and dances for the discordant and barbarous `hakas' and `waiatas' at present in vogue. *(1849,Sketches in NZ,184)*  
> **POWER** One or two little Anacreontic anecdotes, preserved in the `Waiatas', or love-songs, are recorded of the [polynesian] immigrants, ... *(1853,Recollections (various authors/titles),356)*  
> **SHORTLAND** If a woman is forsaken by her lover, she gives vent to her feelings in a <i>waiata. </i> *(1856,Traditions & Superstitions,177)*  
> **HAAST** We looked in vain for some of the whares. They were gone, and amidst the ruins ... stood Katarina ... singing a most doleful waiata, in which she described the flood, ... finishing with the words `Awe, awe, kua riro te mira,' (Alas, alas, the mill is lost) ... *(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,42)*  
> **T.S. GRACE** They all embellished what they had to say with waiatas (songs), but one old man attracted my attention more than all the rest. *(Jan 4, 1868,Journal)* [NOTES: 1868 Jan 4 T.S.Grace Journal in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 177]  
> **HAWTHORNE** In September, 1867, a waiata or poem was composed by Te Kooti, the burden of which was to the effect that Te Kooti was inspired ... *(1869,Dark Chapter from NZ History,12)*  
> **MEREDITH** Occasionally they [sc Maoris] travel at night in a party; but they always sing <i>waitas</i> [sic] (native dirges) to keep the <i>taipo</i> (devil) away. *(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,72,1935)*  
> **BULLER** ... he did so by ... using a well-known waiata, or poem, which wrought them into a frenzy. *(1878,Forty Years in NZ,85)*  
> ... so many of the Maori <i>waiatas</i> or songs are in honour of their ships ... *(1879,TrNZI,11/72)*  
> The following Maori <i>waiata</i>, or song, has a somewhat interesting history ... *(1893,TrNZI,25/426)*  
> **BAUCKE** Then Puke, leaping up like a geyser, howled a last despairing howl, sang a waiata .. *(1905,Where the White Man Treads,88)*  
> One can cover four and almost five generations, if one can reach back to the Rapata Wahawaha of the eighties and watch the environment now shaping the lives of one's own mokopuna. They, poor things, may be denied all opportunities to hear the old waiata, to take part in the bowdlerised haka or to look with understanding on ceremonial. *(May 15, 1931,Na To Hoa Aroha,2/149)* [NOTES: 1931 May 15 Na To Hoa Aroha (1987) (Ngata-Buck Correspondence) 2/149 (Ngata)]  
> **HYDE** A New Zealand captain, marching at the head of the Maori Pioneer Corps, started them off singing the Maori <i>waiatas </i>- the very sweet, very plaintive tribal songs that from one generation to [141] another have been handed down among the people of the Maori race. *(1936,Passport to Hell,140)*  
> **SILJACK** Some of their [sc. Maoris'] more modern waiata, like many contemporary Hawaian melodies, are based on missionary hymn tunes, though syncopated variation frequently conceals their origin. *(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),28)*  
> <i>Waiata</i>, song, a general term, different varieties having different names; but waiata is generally known to Pakeha. *(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/160)* [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words]  
> <i>waiata</i>: song *(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,176)*  
> **VON HAAST** [They found] Katarina, one of the two Maori women ... singing a most doleful <i>waiata</i> in which she described the flood ... *(1948,Julius Von Haast,93)*  
> **BAXTER** (Treading with polished shoes on the face of the dead,/ The willow, the evergreen waiata). <i>Ibid.</i> 487 ... perhaps with a Maori friend/ To sing a waiata,.. *(1979,Collected Poems,251,1962)*  
> **KEENE** <i>waiata:</i> song, sing. *(1963,O Te Raki,195)*  
> **MASON** HINGAWARU ... But I remember my grandfather. Spoke not a word of English, never, till he died. Full of waiata and karakia; taught me the whakapapa right back to Hoturoa and the Tainui. *(1974,Hand on the Rail, The,(1981 TS))*  
> <i>waiata n.</i> NZ Maori song. [M] *(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,867)* [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]  
> **Michael King** Ngata himself undertook a study of Maori waiata which was eventually published in four volumes...as <i>Nga Moteatea</i>. *(2003,Penguin History of New Zealand,293)*  
> That night, after roast pork, over red wine around a well-stoked fire, laughter and waiata can be heard drifting across the river from the Tieke Marae, preparing for the next day's ceremonies. *(Oct 2, 2000,Evening Post,13)*  
> There was heard.. the characteristic waiata (song of lament). *(1900,Auckland Weekly News,Xmas No. / 12)*

### waihena
  
noun.

### Waihi disease
  
noun. an osteo-malacia disease in cattle, caused by nutritional deficiency.  
> This condition has acuired the name of 'Waihi disease' on account of the fact that the first-noted cases occurred in the districts of Waihi, North Taranaki. There is good reason to believe that Waihi disease is allied to osteo-malacia. In the case of Waihi disease the condition had arisen as a result of the depletion of the soil of its nutritive elements. Rib bones, showing bulbous-like enlargements characteristic of Waihi disease, which attacks dairy cattle grazing upon certain kinds of second-class light land. *(Sep 1913,NZ Farmer,1158)*  
> **BELSHAW** Epidemics of 'Waihi disease' occur in exceedingly droughty seasons in many parts of the North Island indicating a shortage of phosphoric acid in the herbage. *(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,82)*  
> `Waihi disease', or bone mal-nutrition trouble, so called because it first appeared in cattle at Waihi, in Taranaki, is often very prevalent in many parts of the North island ... the cure of the ruminant animal acutely suffering from a phosphorus deficiency ... *(1929,TrNZI,60/43)*  
> There are, however, certain areas in which a decided malnutrition disease develops in stock, mostly in dairy cattle, in which the deficiency is phosphoric acid, since the symptoms are those of `Waihi disease', a bone-malnutrition trouble manifested by lameness, swollen joints, and curable by dosing the subjects with phosphates as a lick or medicinal drench ... *(1930,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,12/304)*  
> The known deficiency diseases in New Zealand apparently mainly due to the lack of some element in the food of farm animals are:- `Bush Sickness' or [mistakenly] Iron deficiency. `Dopiness' or Calcium deficiency. `Waihi Disease' or Phosphate deficiency. `Goitre' or Iodine deficiency. <i>Ibid.</i> 82 `Waihi disease' in cattle gives as its most prominent symptom extreme lameness in milking cows. Epidemics of `Waihi disease' occur in exceedingly droughty seasons in many parts of the North Island indicating a shortage of phosphoric acid in the herbage. *(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,81)*  
> Dr Reakes also describes the so-called "Waihi disease," Taranaki, Waikato, and Hawke's Bay.   "It has practically always been found amongst dairy cattle grazing on light land and usually in old pastures which had not been manured at any time ..." *(Jun 18, 1914,Marlborough Express,3)*

Of all these woods there are abundant quantities. *(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/400)*

### Waikikamukau
  
noun. an imaginary New Zealand placename, or town, parodying the form of Maori placenames; see also (cf. WAIWHARE, WAIWORRI)  
[NOTES: Fictitious Maori name (jocular)]  
> Many of us remember free milk at school, working a first job on a milk run, eating milk-bottle lollies or hearing tall tales about mythical Waikikamukau, a town so remote it made Eketahuna seem like a metropolis. *(Mar 2003,NZ Geographic,62/52)*  
> "What was yours? [sc. reunion]". "The Waikikamukau Knitting and Sewing Circle?" *(Jul 26, 1943,NZEF Times,III/109 : 6)*  
> A special meeting of the Waikikamookau rate payers was called today ...on the subject of Evolution. *(1928,Acid Test, The,17)* [NOTES: 1928 Minhinnick (Auckland Sun) in 1981 Mclaucklan Acid Test 17]  
> **BRUNO** ... the suburbs of Waikikamookau. <i>Ibid.</i> 58 A stringy private, as long as a wet weekend in the suburbs of Waikikamookau, snarled ... *(1946,Maleesh George,41)*  
> **LELAND** <i>Whykickamoocow:</i> An imaginary town locatedin the back of beyond; HICKSVILLE, NEW ZEALAND. *(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,112)*  
> (Labelmaker] Make `test' id 2 / 01 Smith*A. B. / Any Place / Newtown / 12 Jones & Co. / P.O. Box 27 / Waikikamukau / List `test' *(Sep 1982,Victoria University Computing Services Centre,Bulletin 124)*  
> **McGILL** <i>Waikikamukau </i>imaginary archetypal Kiwi back-of-beyond village, with variant spellings, including <i>Waikikamookau</i> and <i>Waikukumukau,</i> former used by Minhinnick in 1920s humorous newspaper articles, the latter used by English writer Austin Mitchell in his 1972 <i>The Half-Gallon, Quarter-Acre Pavlova Paradise</i>. Becoming unacceptable in recent years, though school playgrounds may yet ask `Waikikamukau?' and answer, `Because it kicked me.' *(1982,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,120)*  
> From 'Hastings' the [model] train runs past 'Windsor'.. passing on the way an unnamed siding that the owner would call 'Waikikimookau' but for the fact that the station sign would have to be curved to go part way round the bend. *(Nov 25, 1935,NZ Free Lance,21)*  
> **Owen Marshall** Budgie...always said he'd played halfback for his first fifteen, though he was vague about the school. Michael and Rik had a private joke that it was probably Waikikamukau District High. *(2005,Watch of Gryphons,71)*  
> No,  the 7.30 fromWaikikamukau is running an hour late *(Jun 1936,NZ Railways Magazine,13)*  
> Of course the idea's not a new one, they've been doing it for years in the central North Island town of Waikikamokau. *(Dec 14, 2004,Independent Herald (Wellington),p10)*  
> Anyone who can get the Colossi of Mannon into his sea-kit and present them to the Waikikamukau Borough Council as an ornament for the park, should receive substantial recognition. *(Jan 19, 1942,NZEF Times,I/30 : 6)*
