### Waingaro schist
  
noun. Geol. definition  
> Thus there is a general similarity in colour and symbol for the Waingaro Schist and Devil River Vocanics (hd and hw), the Karamea Granite (ko) anad the Onehau Granite (so). *(Jun 1972,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,32/27)*

### Wainui siltstone
  
noun. Geol. definition  
> Wainui Siltstone, sometimes considered the oldest Waitemata sediments in the area, has late Oligene (early-mid Lw) and early Milcene (late Lw) ages and is sedimentologically and structurally better placed with "Onerahi" sediments. *(Oct 1982,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,58/13)*

### Wainuiomartian
  
noun. resident of Wainuiomata  
> It's been long overdue, but as they say all good things take time! Ynui Technology would like to announce that Wainuiomartians now have a place on the net to share our thoughts and ideas, and help to promote Wainuiomata. If you would like to take advantage of the Forums and Chat Room, users will need to register for a free account. *(Jun 13, 2010,Internet - see Notes)* [NOTES: http://wainuiomata.com/news/?cat=4]  
> I began life as a Wainuiomartian; that is, I was raised in Wainui-o-mata, a working class, dormitory suburb of New Zealand's Capital City, Wellington. *(2004,Internet - see Notes)* [NOTES: http://www.phildarkins.com/biography/index.asp]  
> He then recounted three separate Wainuiomartian invasions when his establishment had been systematically ransacked by Nappy Valley neanderthals. *(Apr 2010,Internet - see Notes,00)* [NOTES: http://johnansell.wordpress.com/2010/04/04/hanging-in-palmerston-north/]  
> Maybe rose-tinted spectacles from a nearing-40 ex-wainuiomartian, but it all used to be so much better! *(May 2, 2010,Internet - see Notes)* [NOTES: http://www.robyngallagher.com/2010/04/14/beyond-the-valley-of-the-suburbs/]  
> The community pulls together, all the clubs help each other, and everyone is proud to be a Wainuiomartian. *(Feb 10, 2010,Dominion Post,A13)*

### Waiohine gravel
  
noun. Geol. classification  
> A large part of the Waiohine gravel in Wairarapa is shown as Holocene even though it had been attributed to the Pleistocene (Last Glaciation) in a previous publication. *(Dec 1967,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,24/19)*

### waiora
  
noun. [Maori] health, soundness  
> **HOWE** Waiora - this refers to the health and total well-being of a person or other forms of life. It links with the idea of `wairua' below. *(1985,Towards Taha Maori,23)*

### Waioriki poisoning
  
noun. an effect suffered by stock from eating the Waioriki buttercup.  
> EDGAR NZ Farmers' Vet. Guide 245 Waioriki poisoning is by the most poisonous of all the buttercup species [Ranunculus rivularis]. *(1959)*

### Waiouru blonde
  
noun. [Rural] a sheep.  
> **MCGILL** Waiouru blonde - sheep near army training quarters. *(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,121)*  
> **HUGHES** Take care to ensure that the only blondes within a bull's roar are 'Waiouru blondes,' otherwise known as sheep. *(1999,Shooting from the Lip,8)*  
> .. Canterbury duster, Waiouru blonde, cotty, decoy, glue pot and scrubdasher. *(Nov 22, 2002,Dominion Post,C6)*

### Waiouru blonde
  
noun. a sheep (near army training quarters)  
See: shepherd's grummet  
> **HUGHES** Take care to ensure that the only blondes within a bull's roar are 'Waiouru blondes', otherwise known as sheep. *(1999,Shooting from the Lip,8)*  
> **McGILL** `I miss that underground mutton.' `Eh?' `Rabbit. You are back in 10 minutes with your  supper.' ... We need an Eric Partridge to record these Kiwi colloquialisms like underground mutton, Rangitoto Yankees, leather ladies (possums hit by trains in tunnels, where the flies don't get to them and they petrify) and the Waiouru blondes (sheep). *(1985,G'day Country,171)*  
> **McGILL** <i>Waiouru blonde</i> sheep near army training quarters. *(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,121)*

### Waiouru limp
  
noun.  
> **McGILL** <i>Waiouru limp</i> a limp allegedly acquired in pursuit of preceding, [[Waiouru blonde]] allegedly for sexual purposes. *(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,121)*

### waipera
  
noun.  
See: waipiro

### waiperou
  
noun.  
See: waipiro

### Waipipian
  
adjective. rock classification  
> The Waipipian - Waitotarian (Late Pliocene?) fluctuation is inferred from approximate data, and the amplitude given for it above may be considerably in error. *(Dec 1961,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,11/16)*  
> Thesis studies completed . . . R. F. Whitten (M.Sc) Waipipian stratigraphy and palaeoecology [sic], South Taranaki Coast. *(Aug 1973,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,35/15)*  
> In most areas sheets separated by thick mudstone and sandstone units occur in Opoitian, Waipipian and Mangapanian rocks. *(Nov 1978,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,46/3)*

### waipirau
  
noun.  
See: waipiro

### waipiro
  
noun. [Maori] alcohol, from Maori 'stinking water'; of common use in shearing and mustering.  
> SILK History of Puhoi 50 The Maori tasted the waipirau and loved it. *(1923)*  
> MACFARLANE Farming: Facts, Faults & Fancies 34 He is full of anecdotes and kind to children, dogs lick his hands and at afternoon tea, or over a waipiro it is a pleasure to meet him. *(1931)*  
> BEATTIE Early Runholding 54 The Maori shearer of the early 'sixties ... proved a ready convert to the custom of drinking waipiro and 'doing in' his cheque at the nearest grog shanty. [waipirau] *(1947)*

### waipiro
  
noun. liquor, esp. spirits; grog  
> He had previously been known as a greatconsumer of waipirau, a wife-hitter,loafer, and general ne'er-do-weel. *(Jan 29, 1910,NZ Free Lance,13)*  
> Allasame te waipiro. Te waipiro te werry good t'ing for te pakeha. *(Dec 1912,NZ Free Lance,Xmas Annual : 31)*  
> Many of the early race meetings were run on a free-and-easy style, with much squabbling and fighting, and not a little "waipiro" or other strong drinks. *(1957,Racing in Rangitikei,7)*  
> **HUTT** I am not coming with waipiro - corrupted, smelling water, although I have learnt some of you like that; but I am bringing you waireka - sweet water. I am bringing you wairoa - the living water, I am bringing you rongoa - medicine - for your bodies; and rongoa - medicine - for your souls. *(1999,Maori & Alcohol,00)* [NOTES: From sermon by Rev. Gideon Smales at Porirua, 1843, quoted in NZ Herald, 1 April, 1893.]  
> **SCYMGEOUR** Joe Gillies presented the youthful visitor with a flowing pannikin of "Waipero". *(1960,Memories of Maoriland,133)*  
> The Natives of the Bay ... have long been fond of the `wai perou' (smelling water) or rum, but until very lately they would never <i>purchase </i> any; ... *(Aug 9, 1834,NZ Independent,31,1979)*  
> He went down to the Pah and drank Wypiero (Spirits) until he was beastly drunk. *(1840,Journal of Ensign Best,230,1966)*  
> **TAYLOR** ... they [Maori party] said they had Waipirau also with them I told them if they would give me that I would accept it to pour out upon the ground as it was he wai waka mate. <i>Ibid.</i>Mar 16<i>IV/3 ... my boys had received about half a bottle of rum ... I asked them what they intended doing with the Waipirau, they said sell it to the pakehas. *(Feb 16, 1846,Journal,3/218)*  
> Maoris' salutary idea of the noxious qualities of waipiro. *(Sep 12, 1857,Chambers Edinburgh Journal,174)* [NOTES: `Social Progress at the Antipodes']  
> **MANING** `Was not little Jackey-<i>poto</i>, the sailor, drowned by the Taniwha? He <i>would </i>go on shore, in spite of every warning, to get some water to mix with his <i>waipiro </i>, ... *(1863,Old New Zealand,(xi) 166)*  
> **SHORTLAND** Yielding a little to the seductions of Waipera [Waipiro] they [Maoris] indulge in occasional playful though frantic gestures. *(Mar 12, 1868,Town by Night,31,1978)*  
> [It] overturned, sending Maoris, Waipiro, and all the Christmas commodities to the bottom. *(Jan 11, 1871,Evening Post,2)*  
> **TINNE** ... a crowd of drunken natives ... ready to sponge ... for another glass of their beloved waipero (stinking water), the curse of their race here as in America. *(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,25)*  
> **CAMPBELL** ... he must not be a <i>tongata riri </i> [87] - a man of bad temper - or fond of <i>waiperou </i> - stinking-water - <i>alias</i> spirits!. Such was the term into which they translated rum, which was the prevailing article consumed. <i>Ibid.</i> Bk 4/11/341 But though we had the inevitable percentage of indulgers in <i>`waipirau' </i>we had not got the length of having thieves, ... *(1881,Poenamo,Bk3/2/141)*  
> **BATHGATE** Gilbert ... got the shepherd to show him the canoe ... It was really a canoe of native manufacture, and was simply a log of Totara, which had been hollowed out by some patient Maori, assisted by ... a stone adze, in the days before the advent of the `Pakeha' with his deadly civilisation and exterminating `Waipouru' (alcoholic liquor - literally, `stinking water'). *(1881,Waitaruna,74)*  
> **MORRIS** <i>Waipiro, n. </i>Maori name for spirits - literally, stinking water, from <i> piro </i>, stinking, and <i>wai </i>, water. In New Zealand geography, the word <i>Wai </i> is very common as the first part of many names of harbours, lakes, etc. Compare North-American Indian <i>Fire-water. </i> *(1898,Australian & NZ Gazette,493)*  
> **BAUCKE** Look around you to-day : when it [Government] asked for permission to take a road through ... it covenanted that it would neither buy land, interfere with our customers, nor bring waipiro within its boundaries. *(1905,Where the White Man Treads,272)*  
> **GUTHRIE** At Otaki our party saw ... Rangihaeata who boldly asked for <i>waipero </i> or grog ... *(1930,NZ Memories,75)*  
> **REISCHEK** They amused themselves with singing and dancing, and were drinking some horrible concoction which had been sold them as waipiro (spirits). *(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,60,1933)*  
> **FINLAYSON** You strike the money there, boy! Some good waipiro too perhaps if we're lucky. *(1938,Brown Man's Burden,25,1973)*  
> **6** <i>waipiro </i>, introduced strong drink; literally stinking water (wai, water; piro, rotten or stinking). *(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/160)* [NOTES: J.C.Andersen]  
> **BEATTIE** The Maori shearer of the early sixties ... unfortunately ... proved a ready convert to the custom of drinking waipiro and `doing-in' his cheque at the nearest grog shanty. *(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,54)*  
> On the contrary, [Maoris] showed such an aversion [to spirits] that they gave them the name of <i>wai-piro </i>(stinking water) ... *(1908,TrNZI,40/160)*  
> "It is a blot on the character of the white man that the Maori was ever introduced to waipiro - fire water or stinking water - which facilitated orgies of drunkenness and debauchery ever since". *(Mar 15, 1992,Dominion,2)*  
> **MARKHAM** ... was done, then I would give the Natives, a dram of Rum to wash it down. It used to be called Why Pirah [<i>waipiro </i>] or Stinking Water. Now they call it Why Pie [pai] or good Water. They like now to get Showrangy [<i>haurangi </i>], or drunk if they can. The Young Ladies learn to like it on board the Whalers' in the Bay of Islands. *(1834,New Zealand (Heaphy),45)* [NOTES: 1963 edition]  
> **HAY** With the advent of 'waipiro' it no longer became necessary to make tu-tu wine. *(1915,Reminiscences of Earliest Canterbury,19)*  
> The Maori.. is too glad of the chance to get the waipiro on reasonable terms. *(Jun 11, 1904,NZ Observer,5)*  
> The Chancellor was introduced as Paapi Waipiro. *(Jun 14, 1913,NZ Observer,16)*  
> Very often at the close of the performance, many were greatly under the influence of waipiro. *(Mar 26, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,8)* [NOTES: check page]  
> Jimmy had an unquenchable thirst for the "waipero". *(Sep 11, 1890,Otago Witness,36)*  
> **HUTT** Ernest Tinne in 1872 observed.. at a Taupo hostelry  "a  crowd of drunken natives were squatting before the door, ready to sponge on the newest arrival for another glass of their beloved waipero [<i>sic</i>](stinking water), the curse of their race here as in America." *(1999,Maori & Alcohol,33)*  
> He said, "Too much waipiro." *(Jul 5, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,15)*  
> **OWEN & PERKINS** But she [sc. an elderly chief's wife] said now, with the coming of the Pakeha,, there was the waipiro - the drink - and tobacco, and all the evils the Pakeha had brought them. *(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,111)*  
> **ROBERTS** There was nothing radically wrong with these worthies, their distinguishing characteristics being an unlimited capacity for beer, or as the Maoris called it, waipero. *(1890,History of Oamaru & North Otago,63)*  
> How much better, we say, would have beenthat glad day  If we only had got more"waipira" (sic). *(Jan 17, 1917,Chronicles NZEF,I/10 : 227)*

### waipiro
  
noun. liquor, grog  
[NOTES: incl attrib; ca1952 Wellington Hotel Try the old waipiro - can't beat it. ca1951 H.S. Gajadhar, Wellington p.c. Plenty of waipiro there, no need to take any]  
> Waipera `liquor' ... *(1869,Punch or the Auckland Charivari,147)*  
> **ROBERTS** ... beer, or as the Maoris called it, Waipero. *(1890,History of Oamaru & North Otago,63)*  
> Still Mrs. Green had told her that she didn't take enough gone over the bar: that she didn't sell enough waipiro, and that Miss Fox's till showed almost double the amount, and that things wouldn't do. *(Sep 21, 1907,NZ Truth,118/5/2)* [NOTES: [check sense???]]  
> **OWEN** Try the rum ... very good <i>waipiro.</i> [fn] Strong drink; almost slang, and in common use among Colonials. *(1909,Philip Loveluck,85)*  
> But the everlasting korero about waipiro by members of the women's cant-talkers union [sc. the WCTU] is calculated to arouse curiosity that has been the leading characteristic of the sex since the days of apple-eating Eve, ... *(Feb 26, 1910,NZ Truth,244/4/5)*  
> There are also a good number of Maoris shearing here [Martinborough] and they have more use for a pint of waipero [sic] than a paper. *(Nov 24, 1911,Maoriland Worker,38/15/1)*  
> **SCOTT** The spree was at the Pahi pub/ ... Te kai, kapai, waipiro plenty,/ Advice to you who bottle empty/ When full of ink, to walk unable,/ Don't hesitate but try the stable. *(1987,Seven Lives on Salt River,55)* [NOTES: 1912 poem in 1987 Scott Seven Lives on Salt River 55]  
> Warriors, Wahines, Kuri, Poaka, Tamariki, Kumeras, Karos, Interpreters (1st, 2nd and 3rd grade), Poets Laureate, Poi Dancers, Guides, &c. on application to the Registrar. ...WAIPIRO - - An acursed drink ... TAIHOA - - The Police have the matter in hand POAKA - -a pig PUKEKO A bokakker out of season KORERO A long talk UTU - - The spike HAPU - - A Tribe KUMERA - - A sweet Spud *(1913,Auckland University Carnival Book,27)*  
> <i>~ n.</i> alcoholic drinks in general. [Maori, wai, water and piro, stinking.] *(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,12)*  
> ~ The Maori word for whisky or other spirits from `wai', water, and `piro', stinking. *(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),437)*  
> ~ The Maori word for whisky or other spirits from `wai', water, and `piro', stinking. *(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),437)*  
> **PARTRIDGE** [The] following [was] employed by those who served in the [Great] War ...  waipiro, intoxicating liquor, from the Maori; *(1935,Slang Today,286)*  
> **PARTRIDGE** <i>Waipiro.</i> Intoxicating liquor:; id. Straight from Maori. *(1937,Dictionary of Slang,935)*  
> <i>waipiro </i>spirits, liquor *(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,176)*  
> <i>waipiro n.</i> spirits, alcoholic drink, grog, [Maori]. *(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),628)*  
> **ELLIOT & ADSHEAD** These men [barmen] toiled like galley slaves, pushing out the `waipiro' (Maori for stinking water) and at closing time they fairly collapsed over the barrels. *(1967,Cowshed to Dog Collar,119)*  
> <i>waipiro ... n.</i> spirits, alcoholic drink, grog [Maori]. *(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),21)*  
> **McGILL** <i>waipiro </i>strong alcohol; Maori `wai', water, and `piro', stinking. *(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,120)*  
> We will put it to the waipiro mob, and let them answer in a sober moment, and we are certain they will speak in favor of direct taxes. *(Sep 28, 1844,Daily Southern Cross,2)*  
> Afterwards came spirituous liquors (Waipiro). *(Nov 26, 1859,Nelson Examiner,2)*

### waipiro-laden
  
adjective.  
> ... the slumbering form of the sleuth [King Country] policeman who set out to keep night-watch in the fern for a motor-load of illicit grog, and who was found ... with a `dead marine' tied to his foot bearing a joyful note from the waipiro-laden gauntlet-runners. *(Jul 11, 1921,Quick March,25)*

### waiporou
  
noun.  
See: waipiro

### Wairakeite
  
noun. [Geology]  
[NOTES: misspelling (in source or not) of Wairakite q.v. [yes, as in source. JB]]  
> He tells the story of Alfred Steiner (Petrology) meeting the Minister of Science during his visit to NZGS in 1956 or early 1957, and showing the Minister a phial of the newly discovered mineral Wairakeite, which he had in his pocket. *(Jul 1997,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,113/30)*

### Wairakite
  
noun. zeolite mineral found in the Wairakei area  
[NOTES: listed in OED with attrib. to Steiner]  
> A New Zealand mineralogist seeing the book is naturally interested in the minerals first described from this country, namely akatoreite, (Read and Reay), awaruite (Skey), huttonite (Palst), hydrogrossular (Hutton), motukoreaite (Rodgers, Chisholm, Davis and Nelson), taranakite (Hector and Skey), tuhualite (Marshall), wairakite (Steiner and Coombs),and wairauite (Challis and Long). *(Oct 1982,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,58/71)*  
> Mr Steiner gained and international reputation for his work in connection with the Wairakei field, especially from his demonstration and description of pronounced mineral zoning with depth and from his discovery of the new zeolite mineral wairakite. *(Apr 1967,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,23/2)*

### wairangi
  
noun. [Maori] a New Zealand-bred cultivar of dwarf rape, a brassica forage crop.  
> Grasslands Range of Forage and Conservation Plants 72 Wairangi dwarf forage rape - excellent drought tolerance. *(1995)*

### wairangi
  
adjective. [Maori] foolish, witless  
[NOTES: Williams sense 2]  
> [poem] .. My kuia whose name I carry, Said they were wairangi .. *(Jun 16, 2006,Whakatane Beacon,2)*

### wairangi
  
noun.  
> The woods used are totara burr, wairangi, kauri, lancewood, puriri and kohutuhutu. *(Apr 23, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,18)*

### Wairarapa death
  
noun.  
See: cf. New Zealand death  
> **MANSON** At Tom's insistence, a call was made at a sheep station called Kahumingi; the owners, the Vallances, were good friends of his. Mrs Vallance's first husband, Donald Drummond, had suffered what was called the `Wairarapa Death'- drowning; and until she remarried, his widow had managed the big property herself. *(1981,Widow of Thorndon Quay,127)*

### wairau
  
noun. [Maori; Rural] a New Zealand-bred cultivar of lucerne (Medicago sativa).  
> Yields of Wairau in these trials were not affected by wilt, and Saranac yielded as much as Wairau. Where wilt seriously reduces the productive yield of Wairau, Saranac can be expected to remain productive for perhaps twice as long. Saranac is a coarser lucerne than Wairau and not so good for producing high-quality hay. *(Oct 1974,NZ Journal of Agriculture,47)*  
> Figures for the 1982-83 season show it yielded the same from four cuts as the other home-grown varieties, Wairau and Rere. *(Nov 1986,NZ Journal of Agriculture,22)*  
> Wairau - standard New Zealand cultivar, well adapted to both cutting and grazing. *(1995,Grasslands Range of Forage and Conservation Plants,10)*

### Wairau affair
  
noun.  
> **McGILL** It was known to the Wellington settlers as the Wairau Massacre. Later it was called the Wairau Affray. Today it is simply the Wairau Affair. *(1985,G'day Country,120)*  
> In 1843 his loyalty saved that city from destruction after the Wairau affair. *(Jan 28, 1862,Colonist (Nelson))*  
> Governor Fitzroy, soon after his arrival in New Zealand, saw Rauparaha, and heard his explanation of the Wairau affair. *(Mar 17, 1898,Otago Witness,4)*  
> They had both taken part in the Wairau affair, and were very obnoxious, therefore, to all the English of the Wellington and Nelson settlements. *(Jul 21, 1928,Evening Post,17)*  
> All the Maories [sic] in Nelson left on the arrival of the news of the Wairau affair, fully expecting indiscriminate vengeance to be taken by the whites, in accordance with their own customs. *(Dec 23, 1843,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,4)*  
> ".. That the claims of the following persons should not be entertained:- William Morrison - he having been a Wellington immigrant, and his sufferings at the Wairau affair not having been such as to entitle him to speci[a]l consider[a]tion ..." *(May 21, 1859,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,3)*

### Wairau affray
  
noun.  
[NOTES: ?a contemporary `missionary' term]  
> **SELWYN** On the other side of the [Tuamarina] river, along which the path to the Wairau lies, the ground itself explained the circumstances of the affray. *(Aug 30, 1848,New Zealand (Selwyn),74)* [NOTES: 1848 Aug 30 Selwyn in 1851 NZ Part V (Church in the Colonies XX) 74]  
> **SELWYN** On the other side of the [Tuamarina] river, along which the path to the Wairau lies, the ground itself explained the circumstances of the affray. *(Aug 30, 1848,New Zealand (Selwyn),74)* [NOTES: 1848 Aug 30 Selwyn in 1851 NZ Part V (Church in the Colonies XX) 74]  
> **WRIGHT-ST CLAIR** In June 1843 the settlement had suffered its first major disaster in the Wairau affray. *(1971,Man of the World,58)*  
> **McGILL** It was known to the Wellington settlers as the Wairau Massacre. Later it was called the Wairau Affray. Today it is simply the Wairau Affair. *(1985,G'day Country,120)*  
> **BELICH** One cause of trouble was the hasty New Zealand Company land purchasing which had also contributed to the Wairau Affray ... *(1986,NZ Wars, The,73)*  
> Living, as I do, amongst the natives, having some knowledge of the Maori language, and for years being on intimate terms with most of the natives connected with that tragical event .. I believe that interested parties have concealed from the public, the real character of the Wairau affray ... *(Sep 16, 1843,Daily Southern Cross,3)*  
> ".. That the claimants who suffered by losing relatives or otherwise, in consequence of the Wairau affray, should receive compensation to the amount of aGBP50 in land;  and that Christopher and Elizabeth Maling should receive aGBP75 between them ..." *(May 21, 1859,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,3)*  
> Amony [sic] the latter class may be mentioned some now rusty relics of the deplorable Wairau affray, which occasioned a thrill of horror being sent through the colony in June 1843, when the massacre took place of Captain Wakefield, R.N., the agent at Nelson of the New Zealand Company, and other Europeans who had surrendered, by Rangiparata. *(Jan 23, 1890,Otago Witness,17)* [NOTES: Among . . .]  
> The Governor told them that his mind was dark when he encountered at Sydney the tidings of the Wairau affray. *(Jan 5, 1907,NZ Truth,7)*  
> The only weakness in that particular link of the huge chain of evidence there is on the Wairau affray is a statement made several years later by Mr. Frederick Tuckett, wherein he put on record that a chief of Wairau, Rawiri Kings Puaha, "denied the authenticity of both the alleged sales, and explained that the Wairau did not appear in the original deeds, but had been inserted, on an erasure, or by interlineation." *(Jun 28, 1929,Evening Post,8)*

### Wairau massacre
  
noun.  
> This was [the] so-called "Wairau Massacre" - a turning point in the relations betwen the tangata whenua and the early colonial settlers of the Nelson-Marlborough region. *(Jun 1998,Cuisine,No. 22: 21)*  
> First the clash was called the Wairau massacre, then incident, the affray. *(Nov 19, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),29)*  
> **J.B.WILLIAMS** [Kororareka] A second Wairoo [Wairau] Massacre is staring them in the face. *(1844,NZ Journal,81,1956)*  
> **McKILLOP** He [Rangihaeatea] is ... much feared and hated by the Europeans - partly from the prominent part he took in the Wairou massacre, ... *(1849,Reminiscences,40)*  
> **ANSON** Scene, also, of the so-called `Wairau Massacre' of the `pakehas' by [Rauparaha]. *(1911,Piraki Log,195)*  
> **GUTHRIE** But a little later news of the Wairau Massacre filtered through from the north, brought down by a whaler. *(1930,NZ Memories,92)*  
> **COAD** Last but not least came the conflicts with <i>The Wairauillassore</i> [sic], the Maoris. *(1931,Such Is Life,38)*  
> **HALL-JONES** The date of his arrival is not fixed, but he returned north on hearing of the Wairau massacre (17th June, 1843) ... *(1945,Historical Southland,78)*  
> **REED** Arising out of a land dispute, a conflict had taken place between Maoris ... and a party of well-meaning but ill-advised settlers. It was the Wairau `massacre', in which more than twenty pakehas lost their lives. *(1947,Story of Canterbury, The,52)*  
> **GORE** In the same year, following the news of the Wairau Massacres, he was a member of the special Committee of Public Safety set up to advise on defence measures for Wellington. *(1956,Levins,5)*  
> **SINCLAIR** Two years after the second Company settlement, Nelson, was established in 1841, there was a serious incident (termed by the settlers the `Wairau massacre') when Captain Arthur Wakefield and several other Europeans were killed while attempting to arrest two turbulent chiefs, Te Rauparaha and Te Rangihaeata, ostensibly for burning surveyors, huts, but in reality for resisting the survey of land which the Maoris denied having sold. *(1959,History of New Zealand,75)*  
> **McGILL** The cannon behind still looks in trim, but in fact there was little use for it after the Wairau Massacre, when the Maoris scarpered, in expectation of utu. *(1980,Ghost Towns of NZ,5)*  
> **KING** Settlers were horrified by the incident, which quickly came to be called `the Wairau Massacre'. *(1981,New Zealanders at War,29)*  
> **McGILL** It was known to the Wellington settlers as the Wairau Massacre. Later it was called the Wairau Affray. Today it is simply the Wairau Affair. *(1985,G'day Country,120)*  
> It was the bartering of that identical old cannon which indirectly led to the "Wairau Massacre". *(Dec 23, 1890,Otago Witness,18)*  
> That the Committee differed from the view taken by the Government in its proclamations and in Mr. Shortland's letter to the Mayor, of the late Wairau Massacre, and instead of regarding it as the result of a contest between armed settlers from Nelson and Natives, as the Government did, they considered it as the result of an attempt of the Police Magistrate of Nelson to execute her Majesty's warrant and support her authority over her subjects. *(Jul 29, 1843,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,2)*  
> Anniversary Days .. Frid. 17 Wairau Massacre, 1843. *(Jun 11, 1853,Otago Witness,2)*  
> Scene of the Wairau Massacre, June 14 [sic], 1843. *(Nov 3, 1869,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,2)*  
> He commanded three companies of H.M.'s 58th Regiment during the operations after the Wairau massacre, and behaved gallantly in command of troops in the Hutt Valley. *(Jun 9, 1879,Auckland  Star,2)*  
> He was the oldest member of the Legislative Council and took a prominent part in the administration of the Government in the Cook Strait settlements, after the Wairau massacre. *(Mar 7, 1887,Hawera & Normanby Star,2)*  
> The monument cost aGBP200, and the hill on which it is placed, at Tuamarina, also has the Wairau Massacre monument upon it. *(Jun 4, 1904,Colonist, The,4)*  
> A notable addition to the New Zealand national historical collection is a letter of condolence written on the occasion of the Wairau massacre, 17th June, 1843. *(Jan 28, 1920,Colonist, The,1)*  
> ...there is nothing here that would upset Maori sympathisers (the term Wairau "massacre" is disparaged)... *(Jun 11, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),66)*

### waireka
  
noun.  
> **TINNE** .. we were soon luxuriating in the delights of a `waireka' (hot tank), ... *(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,12)*

### wairenga
  
noun.  
See: waerenga

### wairepo
  
noun.  
See: whai repo

### wairua
  
noun. spirit or spirituality  
> They may not be sure how to get it, because a team's wairua is not tangible, it can't be bullied or suckered or teased or injected or rhetoricised or seminared into existence. *(Jun 13, 2004,Sunday Star Times,B13)*  
> That is our conscience, our wairua - that's first and foremost why we're there. *(Jul 29, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56)*  
> **STEAD** 'Why's she got his hat?''It's got his wairua.''What's his wairua?''I dunno. Bits of his hair.' *(1999,Talking about O'Dwyer,47)*  
> The language is beautiful and thethoughts are clear. It has wairua! *(Sep 28, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),53)*  
> It's a way of respecting the wairua of those homes. *(Dec 1998,Mana,68)*  
> This island holds too much wairua, too much spirit. *(Aug 14, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),33)*  
> Our only concern should be that our leaders are poeple with wairua, leadership skills and that they are fine role models. *(Feb 23, 2006,Dominion Post,B8)*  
> **MARSDEN** I answered in the affirmative; he replied, `That is the whydua <i>(wairua)</i> or spirit of Shunghee's father.' *(1819,Letters & Journals,183,1932)*  
> **LEE & KENDALL** Waid|ua, s. A spirit. *(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,222)*  
> **POLACK** On my advancing near the Wai-tapu [a2-acute], the natives, in a piteous tone, begged me not to go near, as the spirits (wairua) of the place would kill them, ... *(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/202)*  
> **POLACK** At such burials most heart-rending barbarities prevailed, slaves being deliberately murdered to serve the spirit (<i>wairua)</i> of the warrior in the next world. *(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/116)*  
> **DIEFFENBACH** The dead bodies are `tapu' until the tohunga has taken a part of the flesh, and ... has hanged it up on a tree ..., as an offering to the Atuas, or to the wairua of him to revenge whom the war was undertaken. *(1843,Travels in NZ,2/129)*  
> All were alike firmly believed to be remembrances of what they had seen in the <i>reinga </i>, or unseen world (or place of the departed,) whither the spirit (<i>Wairua </i>) was supposed to have been during the sleep of the body.  <i>Ibid.</i> 1/III/42... this haunting spirit, or phantom, (<i>Kehua </i>,) which haunted its former place of residence, when in the body, ... differed widely from the sensible intellectual spirit (<i>Wairua </i>), which had departed to the <i>reinga </i> and which was not feared. *(1869,TrNZI,1/III/41)* [NOTES: (Colenso Maori races)]  
> **DOMETT** The `<i>Wairua </i>', Spirits of the myriad dead, - *(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/150)*  
> **HENDERSON** The fellowship of friends and relations is most necessary to a Maori at such times when the <i>wairua</i> of the dead is still not far away and perhaps present in the house. *(1948,Taina,181)*  
> **KEENE** <i>wairua </i>: spirit. *(1963,O Te Raki,195)*  
> **JENKIN** To the Maori ...the soul or <i>wairua </i> of a man was believed to depart to the spirit world on the death of the body ...sometimes the spirit did return, and these ghosts or <i>kehua </i> were greatly loved. *(1970,NZ Mysteries,96)*  
> **BAXTER** wairua <i>spirit; soul </i> *(1982,Selected Poems,190)*  
> **HOWARD** Wairua -this word refers to the spirituality of life (it does not mean religion) Maoris have a strong belief in the spiritual nature of the world. Children were taught this spiritual world from the beginning of their lives (by karakia), and hence this increased their wairua. *(1985,Towards Taha Maori,24)*  
> **IHIMAERE** What I'm trying to say ... is that the conflict was not only over the land, the tinana, but also over the spirit, the wairua. *(1986,Matriarch, The,69)*  
> Today it echoes with screams and shouts of excitement, and the tears that fall and mix with the wairua (spirit) of the river are tears of laughter. *(Mar 19, 2001,Evening Post,17)*  
> Many Maoris are deeply affected by the event, and treat the casket as though the bishop [sc. Pompallier] had just died and his wairua was still hovering around. *(Mar 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 56/10)*  
> **Ihimaera ed.** Or perhaps they'll bury me in the ground, in the Pakeha way. It won't matter to me: my wairua will have ascended to heaven by then. *(2007,Get on the Waka,137)* [NOTES: (Paula Morris)]  
> "REO WAIRUA," MEANING "SPIRIT VOICE." *(May 21, 1928,Evening Post,10)*  
> A constant motif is the koru which symbolises the wairua and mauri of the artist. *(Mar 25, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),49)*  
> **EDWARDS** Dad was a very wairua person, veryhard working. *(1992,Mihipeka,3)*  
> "Live, we want to capture the integrity of the music on the album, the wairua," says Gooch. *(May 22, 2002,Capital Times,11)*  
> "The inlet is an integral part of the Manukau Harbour," wrote presiding Environment Court Judge Gordon Whiting."If the water is interfered with, the wairua [spirit] of its water will decay and when the gates are opened the resulting decay will affect the wairua of the greater Manukau Harbour." *(Jul 29, 2004,NZ Herald,A1)*

### wairua tautoko
  
phrase. [Maori]  
> Mikara is also conscious of carrying the wairua tautoko of the people who wanted to walk the hikoi, but for various reasons couldn't. *(May 3, 2004,Salient,52)*

### wairuru
  
noun.  
> **BURTON** Many types of fungus were eaten by the Maori. These were known by various names such as hawai, wairuru, tiki-tehetehe, maiheru, tawaka (a species of <i> Agaricus </i> ) and hakeka (<i> Himeola <I/> ). *(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,8)*

### wait for a death
  
phrase. [obsolete] (of shearers) to wait for a man to be sacked so as to get his job  
See: -  
[NOTES: also as waiting (verbal noun) for a death]  
> In contract sheds only fast men are welcomed by the `boss'. Some men who are what we term `guns,' are kept going all the season, and in many cases good honest shearers are cast off to make room for these `guns.' Of course, the `gun' shearer who comes along does not know that this has happened, as the `gun' shearers are as a rule good unionists, and would not demean themselves by `waiting for a death.' At the same time, the `boss' knows when the gun is cutting out, perhaps 200 miles away, and he knows from the other boss which way the `gun' is going, and everything is in readiness when he arrives at the shed. *(Oct 6, 1911,Maoriland Worker,31/17/1)*  
> bullet: An old word for `the sack' when one is dismissed from the job. The old custom of unemployed shearers waiting round in case someone got `the bullet' was called `waiting for a death'. *(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/22)* [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]

### wait for it
  
exclamation. rugby commentator Winston McCarthy's cry in anticipation of a kick succeeding  
> **McGEE** There were times of closeness, father and son, brother and weary brother, waking very early on cold mornings, huddling together under a blanket in front of a wireless waiting for it ... wait for it, wait for it! -  and for a whole generation god was only twice as high as the posts. *(1981,Foreskin's Lament,93)*  
> Winston McCarthy, a popular commentator, was famous for his expression, "Wait for it. ... It's a goal!" *(Jun 2004,NZ Memories,No. 48/6)*

### wait gang
  
noun. [informal] the waitstaff at a restaurant  
See: -  
> They [birds] don't look as menacing, but they're there in every nook and cranny, emblazoned on every wine lass and even printed on the wait staff's backs... The young, well-presented wait gang prove "pro", but with enough informality to make you feel at home and in a country pub. *(Apr 10, 2004,Dominion Post,E6)*

### wait on
  
verb. (as command) wait, hang on  
[NOTES: [CC Ransom notes: I find wait on as a substitute for wait for at least as common here as in the US.]  
> **BENNETT** The verb <i>to wait</i> has acquired an otiose preposition in ordinary use; one is told to `wait on' rather than to `wait', but if any prepositional sense was ever felt in on - as in the American `wait on me' - it has now been lost. *(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,91)*  
> **WINFIELD PETCH** My father came from ... Dorset in 1880, one thing we used to say always amused him, and that was `Wait on' for `Wait for me'. I suppose I picked it up at school, as we certainly did not hear it at home. *(Dec 1, 1957,Letter)* [NOTES: 1957 Dec 1 Winfield Petch letter (Papakura)]

### wait woman
  
noun. politically correct name for a waitress

> We are instantly touched by the gorgeous attentions of our wait woman. *(Mar 16, 2003,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),14)*

### wait-a-bit
  
noun. Rubus cissioides  (formerly <i> R. australis </i>), bush lawyer  
[NOTES: cf. WAIT-A-WHILE: Morris <i> wait-a-while <I/> , an Australian thicket tree. cf. stay-a-bit]  
> Below lay the thickest part of the town [sc Dunedin], and even that was so irregular that scrubby higher ground, covered with a bright green-leaved thick-growing bramble, called `wait-a-bit', entirely removed the sameness of colour, ... *(Sep 23, 1861,NZ Goldfields,21,1976)*  
> The bush ... was composed principally of `bush-lawyer' or `wait-a-bit'. *(1890,Zealandia,1/441)*  
> <i>wait </i>v.i.-wait'-a-bit, -a-minute,-awhile n. the bush-lawyer; an acacia that forms tangled masses of vegetation. *(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,12)*  
> **MATTHEWS** This was his first introduction to the New Zealand bush but, though interested, he had little time for observation of nature, being hard put to it to keep the pace set by his guide. There was no path, part of the way was very steep, and the thorny vines of the `bush lawyer' or, as Pene called it, <i>Taihoa <I/>  (Wait-a-bit) made travelling difficult. *(1940,Matthews of Kaitaia,17)*  
> <i> Wait.-a-bit</i> . (w&amacr.t.&adot.-b&ibreve.t.),<i>  n. (Bot.)<I/>  the bush lawyer of New Zealand; - called also <i> wait-awhile <I/> . See LAWYER, <i> n., 3. [Local, N. Z.]. <I/<i> > *(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2033)*  
> **BURDON** Wait a bit! Good term for some of the vines and creepers of the northern forest, and Mangemange is one of them. *(1996,Hunting for DOC,86)*

### Waitaha
  
noun. [Maori] supposed pre-Maori inhabitants of NZ  
[NOTES: also the Maori name for Canterbury]  
> By failing to take such claims to pieces, and those of the Waitaha proponent Brailsford, Howe ensures that their supporters will continue to regard them as undemolished and therefore enduringly valid. *(Mar 15, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),55)*  
> Twenty-five years on, the Fell mantle has passed in a sense to Christchurch historian Barry Brailsford,.. who in several books has related how a Waitaha nation consisting of many tribes came here at least 1750 years ago and "covered all the islands of Aotearoa" before being decimated by the Maori. *(Apr 8, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),26)*  
> It is the belief that a pre-Maori people called Waitaha settled the island - indeed, settled the whole of New Zealand - at least 1000 years before the arrival of Maori. <i>Ibid.</i> 46 There is every sign that the myth of the pre-Maori Waitaha is in for as long and as fashionable a vogue as the myth it descends from: that of a pre-Maori Moriori people. *(Oct 10, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),45)*

### Waitaha/Waitaha penguin
  
noun. [Maori] a yellow-eyed penguin, extinct since about 1300-1500AD  
[NOTES: <i>Megadyptes waitaha</i>]  
> The yellow-eyed penguin filled a niche after the Waitaha became extinct after the arrival of Polynesians between 1300 and 1500AD .. The Waitaha penguin is believed to have been closely related to the yellow-eyed penguin, but smaller. It was named after the Waitaha iwi, an early South Island people, lost through marriage and conquest. Waitaha penguin bones were found from Nelson to Southland. *(Nov 20, 2008,Dominion Post,A11)*

### Waitakian
  
adjective. Geol. classification  
> It was at high tide level in a low cliff of massive Te Akatea Siltstone (the highest member of the Te Kuiti Group here and of Waitakian age) some 20 ft below the base of the Waitemata Gorge. *(Dec 1970,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,29/1)*  
> Early Odontoceti, especially the Squalodontoidea or shark-toothed dolphins, are well represented - two forms are known from the Whaingaroan, while others occur in the Duntroonian and Waitakian. *(Apr 1977,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,43/21)*  
> The outcrops had formerly been determined as being Landon or Waitakian in age and had been correlated with the Oligocene, Mahurangi facies argillaceous limestones of the Northland Allochthon. *(Sep 1992,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,97/38)*  
> These clearly show regional emplacement of "Onerahi" rocks in the Waitakian and possibly early Otaian, soon after rapid subsidence and formation of the proto-Waitemata Basin in this area. *(Aug 1989,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,85/23)*

### Waitangi Day
  
noun. 6 February, a public holiday commemorating the signing of the Treaty of Waitangi in 1840  
> Waitangi Day without protesters is a little like Christmas without Santa. *(Mar 4, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94)*  
> They discussed her Waitangi Day tears. *(Jun 12, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),71)*  
> In the meantime, our embassies in foreign lands were formally celebrating Waitangi Day as New Zealand Day in their host countries before it was given statutory recognition at home by the Waitangi Day Act 1960. *(Aug 16, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),95)*  
> As for the Waitangi Day celebrations: `That's a holiday, yes?' *(Dec 26, 1989,Evening Post,4)*  
> An Act to provide for the commemoration of the signing of the Treaty of Waitangi... 2. Waitangi Day - The sixth day of February in every year shall be known as Waitangi Day and shall be observed throughout New Zealand as a national day of thanksgiving in commemoration of the signing of the Treaty of Waitangi. *(1960,NZ Statutes,45/343)*  
> WAITANGI DAY .. Tribute to the foresight and generosity of Lord and Lady Bledisloe in presenting Waitangi House to the people of New Zealand was paid by Mr. Cheviot Bell, president of the New Zealand Founders' Society, at a reception in the Majestic Cabaret last night.   The function, given to celebrate the 100th anniversary of the Treaty of Waitangi, was attended by the Mayor, Mr. T. C. A. Hislop, and Mrs. Hislop, and by Mr. R. A. Boulter, British Trade Commissioner. *(Feb 7, 1940,Evening Post,12)*  
> Waitangi Day is the anniversary of the signing of a disputed document, the meaning and legitimacy of which is hugely contested by the descendants of the original signatories, both brown and white. *(Oct 17, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),5)*

### Waitangi industry
  
noun. = Treaty industry  
> This was back before the Wiatangi [sic] industry got underway and Maoris and whites treated each other as equals and got along as great mates. *(2001,Good Bastards: the larrikin's guide to success,103)*  
> That issue involves all of us, not just the Waitangi industry's vested interests. *(Sep 14, 2004,Dominion Post,B4)*

### Waitangi Treaty
  
noun.

### Waitangi Tribunal
  
noun.  
> The Labour Government elected in 1984 passed legislation to enable the formerly impotent Waitangi Tribunal (established by Matiu Rata in 1975 when he was Labour Minister of Maori Affairs) to investigate land claims and treaty grievances dating back to the treaty's signing in 1840, rather than the 1975 fixed previously. *(Nov 1999,North & South,60)*  
> Ironically, National came to power that same year and was responsible for establishing the Waitangi Tribunal in 1976. *(Aug 16, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),95)*  
> Unfortunately, genuine land settlements get delayed, as the Waitangi Tribunal uses up time and resources in dealing with such claims. *(Feb 17, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),6)*  
> The case ties with the Waitangi Tribunal's 2003 report on the Wellington District. The tribunal's job is to investigate alleged breaches of the Treaty of Waitangi and to make recommendations to redress wrongdoing. *(Nov 19, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24)*  
> In 1999 the Waitangi Tribunal found that under Article Two of the Treaty the [Whanganui] river was a taonga (treasure) and was possessed by iwi... *(2008,NZ Geographic,94/112)*  
> There is apprehension - by Pakeha - about the recommendations of the Waitangi Tribunal. *(Jul 2, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11)*
