### waiwai-roa
  
noun.  
> **POLACK** It is the scourge of sand-flies (<i>namu</i>) and mosquitoes, (<i>waiwai-roa</i>). *(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/126 fn)*

### waiwaka
  
noun. [=?] not kaiwaka ?? nf Williams ho;  
> Waiwaka .. *(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,15)*  
> **KNIGHT** We sometimes played with the children. The boys good naturedly climbed white pine trees and also the waiwakas that grew with them, ... *(1975,Poyntzfield,26)* [NOTES: c1920s Eliza McKenzie in <i>Outlook</i> in 1975 Knight Poyntzfield 26 [Porirua 1850s]]

### waiwaka
  
noun. [Ma.] a native timber used as blocks for woodchopping contests.  
> NZ Farmer Aug 16. 63 The working bee was called to get waiwaka blocks from the bush and cart them to the sports paddock...strewn all along the way like pieces of paper in a paper chase, were his waiwaka blocks. *(1951)*

### waiwatua
  
noun.  
See: waiuatua  
> Waiwatua. Euphorbia glauca, Forst. *(1869,TrNZI,1/III/48)* [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

### Waiwhare
  
noun. as an apocryphal name cf Waikikamukau

### Waiworri
  
noun. as an apocryphal name cf Waikikamukau  
> **MACKAY** This chapter concerns the old Waiworri swept by fire a matter of 30 years ago. *(1961,Puborama,68)*

### waka
  
noun. [Maori] metaphorical usage, a vehicle  
> New works recently choreographed by members of all-male Black Grace Dance Company make a sizzling programme that powers along like a waka in overdrive. *(Oct 6, 2001,Evening Post,4)*  
> At the beginning I said to thefacilitators, 'Don't get on this waka ifyou can't last the distance'. *(Mar 9, 1996,Evening Post,14)* [NOTES: metoph.]  
> **BAKER** "Our waka had hooves and tails and our medicines came from the forest." *(2000,Kokopu Dreams,66)* [NOTES: metoph.]  
> The celebrity callously abandons ship, kicks his waka to the kerb, but IT'S THE WRONG KERB, which is a piece of geography lost on the celebrity because he uses corporate cabs and taxis so much he probably assumes he's got a share in the taxi rank. *(May 12, 2001,Evening Post,2)*  
> It came after the legality of that parking ticket in Wellington was challenged on the basis that your standard Maori car is, well, a waka. *(Mar 29, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7)*  
> As an art event, Venice Biennale is what our Biennale commissioner, Jenny Gibbs, described as a peculiar animal... <i>Ibid</i> 51 But the question still lingers - has the waka tied up to the gondola too late? *(Apr 14, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),50)*  
> Abraham Lincoln famously said that a house divided againast itself could not stand. To translate that into New Zealand terms, we are all in the same waka and should be paddling together. *(Jul 17, 2012,Dominion Post,B5)*  
> Guy :"For the first time we're in the same waka." Chris : "Up a creek without a paddle between us." *(Oct 30, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),59)* [NOTES: metoph.]  
> Get yourself down to Aotearoa's first urban marae and help push the waka of Maori Literature Week on its way. *(Sep 22, 2001,Evening Post,2)*

### waka
  
noun.  
See: weka

### waka
  
noun. [Maori] a canoe  
[NOTES: in fairly frequent English use in 19 century; less so in early and middle 20th century but revived in late 20th century after sesquicentennial celebrations - with pl often <i> wakas </i>, and in the collocation <i> waka maori </i>; infrequent in 20th cent. early spg <i> e'wakka, walker, wauka </i>]  
> A Camborne resident called rescue services about 4 pm yesterday after seeing two waka and one single-person waka in trouble. *(Jan 7, 2000,Evening Post,1)*  
> **PARKINSON** A Vocabulary of the Language of New Zealand. [...] Egooree, <i> A dog.</i> Teyka,<i> Fish </i>. Hewhai[a-grave], <i> A skate </i>. Eraperape,<i> A fish called Chimaera. </i> Hepaooa, <i> Ear-shells </i>. Hekohooa[a-grave], <i> Small ear-shells </i>. [...]  Heanoohe[e1-grave], <i> Fern root </i>. [...]  Hewai, <i> Water </i>. [...] Tochee, <i> A hatchet, or adze </i>.  Hecacahoo, <i> A garment. </i> Opoonamoo, <i> A green ear-ring.</i> Potai, <i> A feather ornament on their head.</i>  Emaho, <i> Tataow </i>. Hewaca, A canoe. [...]  Orero, <i> To speak, or a speech. </i>[...] Ekeeno[e2-grave], <i> Bad. </i> *(1770,Journal,126)*  
> <i> waka n </i>. a Maori canoe. *(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),628)*  
> <i> waka ... n </i>. Maori canoe *(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),21)*  
> <i> waka n.</i> NZ traditional Maori canoe; group of Maori tribes claiming descent from the occupants of one of the traditional canoes. [M] *(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,867)* [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]  
> Skills were developed for selecting, felling, transporting and hollowing totara trunks to make waka (canoes). *(Nov 22, 1988,Dominion,10)*  
> Waka has special meaning [headline] ... For Tane Nathan ... being a kaihoe (crew member) on Wellington's waka ... has special significance. *(Mar 2, 1990,Contact (Wgtn),3)*  
> The traditional waka is a canoe; today's waka could be the rusty Honda Civic in which the poet has transported his whanau over the years, and which has now gone to the wrecker's yard; while tomorrow's waka could be a space-ship "rocketing to another orb / singing waiata to the spheres." *(Aug 28, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),47)*  
> Three sausages split down the middle like waka. *(Feb 10, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),44)* [NOTES: metoph.]  
> A couple of "Kai wheros" rushed down to the beach; the "waka" was brought alongside; out jumped the crew. *(Mar 8, 1873,Auckland Weekly News,14)*  
> Waka were a big success. There's something raw and sensual about a huge bunch of guys paddling, and it's very New Zealand too. Let's put them in every scene. We could have Interisland waka. X-wing waka, for an inspiring war scene in space. Whitbread waka. Sedan and convertible waka in five-speed and automatic. Any time the pace flags, throw in some waka. *(Jan 28, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),72)*  
> [Hdg] Tamihere not impartial says lawyer...[He] said Mr Tamihere had clearly been "paddling Dover's waka" during the saga. *(Sep 8, 2000,Dominion,2)*  
> The highlight of the day was the arrival of 15 waka that had journeyed from Okahu Bay to the Viaduct Basin... First was a twin-hulled waka haurua (migration canoe), tan sails carrying a thousand-year history, followed by a giant waka taua (war canoe), with ornately carved prow and white-tipped paddles, modern-day waka ama (outrigger canoes) and a Tongan migration waka. *(Feb 15, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),53)*  
> The man who organised the 42-day wakavoyage to the Cook Islands is planninganother journey around New Zealand. *(Dec 27, 1992,Sunday Times (NZ),2)*  
> He.. set off up the river, alongside Bates' <i>waka</i>. *(Nov 1937,NZ Railways Magazine,18)*  
> **HOCHSTETTER** The creek was here still far too narrow and too shallow, and the load too heavy, to render the propelling of our Waka, by means of paddles, possible. *(1867,New Zealand (Hochstetter),292)*  
> Two outstanding features for me were that the Kaupapa Atawhai waka is now fully launched - a sign of the department's commitment to tikanga Maori and its strength for the greater good of conservation. *(Mar 1993,What's Up Doc,12 : 1)*  
> **STARK** boat - waka [waka] *(1986,Journal,112)* [NOTES: quote from 1827-28 BOULTBEE]  
> It's culturally insensitive to do something traditional like paddling waka with life jackets on. It's like doing a haka in a raincoat so you don't get hypothermia. *(Jan 17, 2000,Evening Post,00)*  
> E'wakka .. A boat or Canoe./ Nooe .. Large./ Kak'ahoo .. Cloth. *(Feb 26, 1777,Cook's Journals,3/817,1967)* [NOTES: 1777 Feb 25 Anderson Journal in Cook Journal (1967) 3/817]  
> **FORSTER** A Comparative Table of the Various Languages in the Isles of the South-Sea ... [English] <i> A Canoe </i>... [New-Zeeland] te-wagga [a1-macron grave, a2-macron] *(1778,Observations Made During a Voyage,284)*  
> **COLLINS** E-waka, <i> A canoe. </i> *(1804,English Colony in NSW,560)*  
> **SAVAGE** Wauka .. A canoe. *(1807,New Zealand (various authors/titles),77)*  
> **NICHOLAS** The constellation forming the Belt of Orion, they call the <i> Whacka </i> or the <i> Canoe </i>, and have some tradition relating to it which he did not communicate; ... *(Dec 2, 1814,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/52,1817)*  
> **LEE & KENDALL** W|aka; A canoe: *(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,223)*  
> boat - wo'nka [waka] *(1826,Boultbee Journal,112,1986)* [NOTES: also 1826-28 Boultbee Vocabulary in 1979 Begg 284]  
> **MARKHAM** When the Bar was crossed Canoes or in the Native Mourie [Maori] Tongue, Walker [<i> waka </i>] Mouries, or Native Boats, boarded us, ... *(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),29,1963)*  
> **BIDWILL** Those [canoes] with top sides ... are called wa-kaw, or in common pronunciation `walkers'. <i>Ibid.</i> 61 We embarked on Towpo about five in the morning in a very large Ti-wai (Tee-why) or canoe, hollowed out of a single log of wood, without top sides; those with top sides, of which they have none on this lake, are called Wa-kaw, or in common pronounciation `Walkers'. *(1841,Rambles in NZ,36,1952)*  
> **SHORTLAND** And it seems probable that the term <i>Waka</i> (canoe), which is used to denote these primary divisions, has reference to this origin of the tribes.  At the present day, these </i>Waka</i> are divided into many distinct </i>Iwi</i>; each of which is subdivided again into </i>Hapu</i>, or smaller communities. *(Aug 15, 1843,Letter)* [NOTES: 1843 Aug 15 Shortland letter in Shortland Traditions and Superstitions (1856) 305]  
> **TAYLOR** ... he said they had a waka (canoe) at their pa which was given by an adulterer as a payment for his sin was it right they should let the wages of sin remain amongst them, ... *(Apr 7, 1847,Journal,V/15)*  
> **ANGAS** Our canoe, which was hewn out of a solid tree, was of the kind used for river conveyance, called <i>kaupapa</i>; it is quite simple without ... the ornamental carving ... that adorn the war canoe and the gaily decorated </i> waka <i> of the harbours on the coast. *(1847,Savage Life,2/18)*  
> **MOSER** ... he commenced a long rigmarole about the `waka', the canoe of his ancestors. *(1863,Mahoe Leaves,38)*  
> **BAINES** Believe me, a New Zealander's canoe is a work of art. `Whake,' is the native name, or rather the native generic term, for all canoes, of which there are many different kinds, as tete, pekatu, kopapa ... *(1874,Edward Crewe,81)*  
> **MORRIS** <i>Waka, n.</i> Maori word for canoe. <i>Waka huia</i> is a box for keeping feathers, originally the feathers of the <i>huia</i> (q.v.). *(1898,Austral English,493)*  
> **TREGEAR** Many of the canoes (<i> waka </i>) of the Maoris are still in evidence to bear witness to the industry, patience, and skill, of the men who designed them. *(1904,Maori Race, The,114)*  
> With regard to the term `<i> waka </i>', or canoe as we call it, the term, having in view the present-day build of such, is certainly a misnomer, and the word `vessel' would be more appropriate, for no canoe of the present type could ... ever have crossed the long stretch of ocean between Rarotonga and New Zealand ... *(1905,TrNZI,37/146)*  
> **SKINNER** These beaches were probably the point of departure for the <i> waka </i> (the wash-through canoe of the Morioris) in making the passage to Pitt Island ... *(1923,Morioris of the Chatham Islands, The,13)*  
> <i> waka n </i> a canoe. [Maori] *(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,12)*  
> **BAKER** Bidwill mentions...that walkers, canoes (waka), was `in common pronunciation,'... <i> waka </i>. *(1941,NZ Slang,82)*  
> <i> waka </i>: canoe *(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,176)*  
> waka The Maori word for a canoe *(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),438)*  
> <i> waka n -s </i> [Maori] : CANOE; <i> broadly </i> : a Maori seagoing craft *(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,2570)*  
> **KEENE** <i> waka </i>: canoe. *(1963,O Te Raki,195)*

### waka
  
noun. [Politics] a political party  
> After that uncomfortable rumpling of his pinstripes, he made sure the only Maori MPs in his caucus were nice, well-behaved ones, who wouldn't rock the waka or upset his fan club at the Tauranga Sunset Village. *(Oct 21, 2003,BLANK ENTRY,B4)*  
> National leader Bill English says he sees no problem with Anderton leaving the Alliance, but he does have a problem with him opposing waka- jumping while building himself a new waka. *(May 7, 2002,Evening Post,7)*  
> And good on you for allowing the powerful currents of the "mainstream" to carry the Green waka forward. *(Jul 22, 2005,Dominion Post,B4)*  
> Now Maori are thinking 'Do we go back to Labour or do we keep trying to row the NZ First waka, or is there another option we haven't tried? *(Sep 20, 1999,Dominion,6)*  
> Perhaps it is time for the Mangai's chosen people to cease being an outrigger and become a waka in their own right. *(Feb 27, 2004,Dominion,B4)*  
> "They tell me you voted for Shipley - don't want a woman to lead the waka, eh bro?" *(Aug 21, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18)* [NOTES: metoph.]  
> Voters don't like the way he swapped waka. *(1999,TV3)*  
> And good on you for allowing the powerful currents of the "mainstream" to carry the Green waka forward. *(Jul 22, 2005,Dominion Post,B4)*  
> The wheels have come off that waka. *(Aug 20, 2001,Radio NZ / National Radio)* [NOTES: Used by Dover Samuel (in Parliament?)]

### waka
  
noun. [Maori] fig. use as in basket, container, collective  
> (heading) How to anchor our waka in a bigger economic space *(Jan 9, 2006,Dominion Post,C3)*  
> [heading] <b>Maori thriving in the business waka</b> *(Feb 25, 2006,Dominion Post,C14)*

### waka
  
noun. a confederation of iwi  
[NOTES: "Maori iwi are usually grouped into confederations, which are named after ancestral canoes (i.e.waka) which are said to have come from Hawaiki. The best known are Tainui and Arawa, but there are others (and I can't help noticing the recent term for Northland confederation is Tai Tokerau which is not a canoe). The use arises because traditionally a  Maori might have a number of iwi affiliations.  (I was once told that when asked their iwi in the Muruwhenua area, locals tend to look down at the ground and give the iwi in whose rohe they are standing, because they belong to all the Muriwhenua iwi). Thus the waka in the quotations [in <i>Kotare</i>]presumably refer to the notion of waka as confederations - which is not a bad way to think of political parties. And waka jumping is I take as heinous as announcing you are Arawa rather than the Tainui you once proclaimed (in the middle of a battle). (Brian Easton, 12/12/1999)]  
> Others signed on behalf of waka (canoeconfederations), trust boards and runanga(multi-tribal councils). *(Feb 1993,Metro,126)*

### waka ama
  
noun. [Sport] outrigger canoe-racing  
> Waka ama is an outrigger canoe race, known to the rest of the world as outrigging. *(Apr 18, 2006,Dominion Post,C9)*  
> They have been invited due to the fact that the coach of their waka ama club in Waitakere, Jean Tetohu, is from Tahiti. *(May 2005,Kokiri Paetae,16)*  
> Waka ama o Whakatane Will be holding a meeting and invite interested parties and waka ama clubs *(Sep 30, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,42)*  
> He said waka ama - one of New Zealand's fastest growing sports - was the perfect medium to draw in Pacific communities. *(Mar 4, 2002,Evening Post,17)*  
> Tackling one of the most dangerous stretches of ocean in the world in an outrigger canoe is not everyone's idea of enjoyable exercise, but 10 waka-ama crews will do just that on Saturday... But the waka-ama don't stop for the changeovers - paddlers will jump out as their replacements climb in after being dropped in the sea ahead of the canoes. *(Apr 29, 2003,Dominion Post,A8)*

### waka ama
  
noun. [Maori] a Maori outrigger canoe  
[NOTES: ama: outrigger of canoe (P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 29)]  
> The secret to a fast run in a waka ama (outrigger) is the hip over. If you don't properly hip over, you tip over. *(Mar 26, 2003,Capital Times,5)*  
> The hui identified four primary categories of Waka. Waka Ama, Waka Hourua, Waka Tete and Waka Taua. The earliest forms of Waka were Waka Ama and Waka Hourua for voyaging purposes in the pacific. *(Oct 2001,Waka Taua Safety Guidelines,1)*  
> .. Mei Everitt, 40, an Otahuhu secretary taking part in a waka ama (outrigger canoe) regatta ... *(Aug 18, 2005,NZ Herald,A7)*

### waka blonde
  
noun.  
See: whaka blonde

### waka hourua
  
noun. [Maori] a twin-hulled voyaging canoe.  
> The Maori King, Te Arikinui Tuheitia Paki, was one of hundreds to attend the launch of Aurere waka builder Hector Busby's third waka hourua at Taipa.. Pacific Island waka enthusiasts who attended the launch have asked him to help them build a waka hourua, he says. *(Sep 3, 2008,Internet - see Notes,1)* [NOTES: www. stuff.co.nz/nz-newspapers/northland-6015/news-1927/northern-news-2237]  
> The newly named waka hourua will then sail across to Taipa escorted by a fleet of single hull waka to land at Taipa Beach in front of the school whare wananga, Nga Taitokorua. . The new waka hourua will continue in the wake of Te Aurere as a training and voyaging vessel for generations of New Zealanders. *(Aug 18, 2008,200 Years of NZ Food & Cookery,1)* [NOTES: www.teaurere.co.nz]  
> There will be two Waka Hourua, Aotearoa One and Te Aurere, two waka taua, two waka tangata and five waka tete taking part in this year's commemorations. *(Feb 1, 2008,Scoop,1)*  
> Being able to swim well is more than just a school subject for Maori- it's an investment in a future which includes Maori people's love of the sea, diving, fishing,surfing, swimming, waka ama, waka hourua, you name it, Maori do it - all the way up to the America's Cup. *(Aug 28, 2008,Scoop,1)*

### waka huia
  
noun. [Maori] a receptacle for, originally huia, feathers.  
[NOTES: aka waka-huia, wakahuia]  
> **TREGEAR** Feather plumes were kept by their owners in small boxes (<i>waka-huia</i>) often beautifully carved. *(1904,Maori Race, The,244)*  
> <i>hu[u]ia,</i> a bird (Heteralocha acutirostris): white-tipped tail-feathers valued as ornaments: bird now extinct. A small, long, narrow, carved box (equivalent of our jewel-box) called waka-huia (waka here means container), a receptacle for feathers; but only huia-feathers. *(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/143)* [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]  
> <i>waka huia</i>: carved box. *(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,176)*  
> **BUCK**  *(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,2/285)*  
>  *(Jan 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,89/1)*  
> **PHILLIPPS**  *(1955,Maori Carving Illustrated,7)*  
> **McLINTOCK** Beautifully carved boxes, waka-huia, were made and used solely for the storage of feathers. *(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/122)*  
> An art object such as a wakahuia (treasure box) is an enriched creation. *(1984,Te Maori,21)*  
> **PHILLIPPS** [Caption] Enlarged waka huia end, Norwich Castle Museum. *(1955,Maori Carving Illustrated,7)* [NOTES: 1961 edition]  
> **BAKER** Rummaging under her cushions, she produced a waka huia beautifully carved with paua inlay and removed the lid, releasing an exotic spicy smell. *(2000,Kokopu Dreams,67)*  
> It consists of two flagpoles, pohaki, and a treasure box, waka huia. ... A waka huia is a lidded box traditionally used by Maori to carry feathers of the Huia, which are a precious item. *(Nov 16, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,9)*  
> Artefacts on display in this area include a waka huia (treasure box) and a pre-European canoe prow or tauihu, both on loan from Te Papa. *(Jul 2000,NZ Geographic,No. 47/94)*  
> When Miss Sybil Nathan left as a delegate to the Red Cross Conference in London she took with her a waka huia, sent from the Junior Red Cross of New Zealand as a token of good fellowship for workers in the junior movement at Home. *(Jul 5, 1930,Evening Post,18)*  
> The waka huia was a sacred box, and was used for carrying to remote parts of the islands the feathers of the huia - a rare bird, which was found only in two ranges of mountains in the North Island, and of which the feathers were sacred and only permitted to be worn by rangatiras (chiefs) and people of gentle blood. *(Dec 17, 1889,Otago Daily Times,1)*  
> They comprise a beautifully carved feeding funnel, a waka huia or feather box, and a large greenstone heitiki (states the "New Zealand Herald"). *(May 3, 1926,Evening Post,8)*  
> **HAYWARD** Mr K admires Mr Shah and on one occasion presented him with a whakahuia.* *[fn] A Maori carved casket for holding the feathers of the now extinct huia. *(1987,Diary of the Kirk Years,107 and f.n.)* [NOTES: misspelling in source?]  
> The display simulates the taonga being in a waka huia, or treasure box, and it is like having the chance to breathe the same air as the ancestors, Dr Tapsell says. *(Mar 3, 2006,Dominion Post,B10)*  
> The boxes (waka huia) were slung fromrafters so people in the house were notendangered, possibly by spiritual beings,he said. *(Jan 8, 1993,Evening Post,3)*

### waka jump
  
noun. [informal; Politics] the move from the representation or membership of one political party to another.  
> How ironic that Helen Clark, whose government introduced no-waka-jumping law, should now be contemplating doing the biggest waka jump - out of Parliament. *(Feb 25, 2009,Dominion Post,B4)*

### waka kaupapa
  
phrase. [Maori]  
> I've been attending Waitangi Day celebrations since 1975 and in the last 15 years have participated in the waka kaupapa ... *(Mar 27, 2005,NZ Catholic,6)*

### waka koiwi
  
noun. [Maori] a Maori burial chest  
> Maori burial chests wll be removed from display at Auckland's Museum... The waka koiwi have been in the museum for the past few years, but last night they were being taken off public display after threats of a protest of about 100 people. *(Feb 9, 2006,NZ Herald,A3)*

### waka korari
  
noun. [Maori] a flax-stem canoe  
> **HUNT** Some noted fisherman [on dying] ... would be secured, lashed in a <i>waka korari</i>, or flax-stem canoe, in a sitting position, as if in the attitude of fishing. *(1866,Chatham Islands,35)*

### waka tangata
  
noun. [Maori] a canoe for community use.  
[NOTES: tangata = of the people.]  
> Waka were built for inland and coastal waterways, but were no longer needed for ocean voyaging. New types became common in Maori communities - waka taua, waka tete, waka tiwai, mokihi, and the modern-day waka tangata .. A late 20th century development has been the construction of canoes for educational purposes. Known as waka tangata, they are very similar to waka tete, with uncarved gunwales and stern posts of simple ornamentation. *(2007,Internet - see Notes)* [NOTES: www.cpc-aoraki.co.nz/about_waka_ama_historical.htm]  
> There will be two Waka Hourua, Aotearoa One and Te Aurere, two waka taua, two waka tangata and five waka tete taking part in this year's commemorations. *(Feb 1, 2008,Scoop,1)*  
> One [waka] was a double hulled waka Aotearoa 1 and two waka tangata paddled by both males and females. [sic] *(Jan 2005,Internet - see Notes,1)* [NOTES: www.terarawa.co.nz/news/general_panui_2005.htm]

### waka taua
  
noun. [Maori] war canoe  
[NOTES: aka waka pitau]  
> The magnificant waka taua symbolised Maori pride so effectively that not even Pakeha platitudes could diminish it. *(Jan 7, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12)*  
> SIXTH RACE. (12.30), Waka Taua Canoes. 1st prize, &#8356;20 .. Entrance, &#8356;1. *(Feb 1, 1859,Daily Southern Cross,3)*  
> For Waka Taua Canoes, unlimited crews. Prize aGBP20. Three canoes to start or no race. Second prize aGBP5. Entrance aGBP1 1s. *(Jan 29, 1856,Daily Southern Cross,4)*  
> 11 a.m. 3. WAKA TAUA, not limited to any number of men. First prize, &#8356;10; second prize &#8356;4. Course: From wharf round hulk, round boat off Cemetery, and back to wharf. *(Mar 13, 1879,Bay of Plenty Times,2)*  
> Fifty years ago large sailing vessels, brigs etc., visited Tauranga Harbour to load with flax, which was mainly cut and prepared by the Maoris at Kaituna, near Maketu, and brought up to Tauranga in large waka taua (war canoes) by natives of the Arawa tribes. *(Nov 4, 1899,Timaru Herald,2)*  
> The hui identified four primary categories of Waka. Waka Ama, Waka Hourua, Waka Tete and Waka Taua.  The exquisite carved Waka Taua, which was developed in Aotearoa, had reached a sophisticated design and function at the time of the arrival of Europeans. *(Nov 2001,Waka Taua Safety Guidelines,1)*  
> For Waka Taua Canoes, unlimited crews.   Prize aGBP20.   Three canoes to start or no race.   Second prize aGBP5.   Entrance aGBP1 1s. *(Jan 29, 1856,Daily Southern Cross,4)*  
> TENTH RACE.   For WAKA TAUA CANOES. *(Jan 21, 1862,Daily Southern Cross,1)*  
> Fifty years ago large sailing vessels, brigs, etc., visited Tauranga Harbour to load with flax, which was mainly cut and prepared by the Maoris at the Kaituna, near Maketu, and brought up to Tauranga in large waka taua (war canoes) by natives of the Arawa tribes. *(Oct 13, 1899,Auckland  Star,1)*  
> The bow ornamentations suggest the waka taua or Maori war canoe, and several of the canoes are decorated with single or double spirals lightly graven on the hull, or on bow or stern pieces. *(Sep 30, 1926,Evening Post,9)*  
> The waka taua, or waka pitau.   The finest type of Maori canoe, often of great size, and used in war expeditions and coastal voyages. *(Jul 27, 1925,Evening Post,5)*  
> I'm sure many this year missed the significance of the full gathering in the whare that normally houses a 35m waka taua. *(Mar 27, 2005,NZ Catholic,6)*  
> The waka taua Te Hono ki Aotearoa was built by Far North waka master Hekenukumai Busby for the arts organisation Toi Maori. It is based at the Volkenkunde Museum in the Dutch city of Leiden. *(Jul 13, 2012,Northern Advocate,A007)*

### waka tete
  
[NOTES: carved prow of waka taua (hard to verify except via citations); sometimes just 'tete'.]  
> Near the bow, too, is the waka tete, the sight of whose awful visage was supposed to inspire enemies with dread. *(Jul 26, 1878,Akaroa Mail and Banks Peninsula Advertiser,2)*  
> Waka tete (prow of waka taua) *(Apr 16, 1896,Press, The,6)*  
> The Wanganui canoes include four good-sized craft, fitted with topsides and ornamented with "tete" and other kinds of carved figureheads, and with gracefully-carved sternposts, brought down by Mr Hamilton from his Museum  collection. *(Nov 2, 1906,Star, The,1)*

### waka tete
  
noun. [Maori] a canoe used mainly for fishing in rivers and coastal areas.  
> Waka tete (or waka pakoko) were shorter and plainer than the large waka taua. *(2007,Internet - see Notes)* [NOTES: www.cpc-aoraki.co.nz/about_waka_ama_historical.htm]  
> The waka tete was requested after Whaingaroa launched a waka taua in 2000 .. The launch of the waka tete is on 19 November 2005. *(2005,Internet - see Notes,1)* [NOTES: www.community.ne.nz/communitycentre/news/2005/waka-launch.htm]  
> **Evening Post** The waka tete.   A canoe with a plain figurehead and sometimes an inferior stern-piece.   Usually smaller than a waka taua, and used in coastal voyages and for sea-fishing. *(Jul 27, 1925,5)*  
> There will be two Waka Hourua, Aotearoa One and Te Aurere, two waka taua, two waka tangata and five waka tete taking part in this year's commemorations. *(Jan 31, 2008,Scoop,1)*

### waka tiwai
  
noun. a `dugout' canoe  
[NOTES: aka tiwai, also tewai]  
> **POLACK** ... the canoe he sat in, which was a very large tewai of the red pine; ... *(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/158)*  
> **BIDWILL** We embarked on Towpo about five in the morning in a very large Ti-wai (Tee-why) or canoe, hollowed out of a single log of wood, without top sides; those with top sides, of which they have none on this lake, are called Wa-kaw, or in common pronounciation `Walkers'. *(1841,Rambles in NZ,61,1952)*  
> **HEAPHY** At low water crossed the river ... in a <i>tiwai </i>or canoe without top sides. *(1846,Notes of an Expedition to Kawatiri and Araura,236)* [NOTES: 1846 Heaphy Expedition to Kawatire in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 236]  
> Today is Auckland regatta ... The Maori canoe race was not in one respect as good as usual. A tiwai ran with the waka tauas and <i>ran away </i>from them. -->[fn] A canoe made out of a single log. *(Jan 31, 1859,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/450-451)* [NOTES: (C.W. Richmond)]  
> AUCKLAND REGATTA SIXTEENTH ANNIVERSARY .. RACES .. 2. For the Tiwai Canoes - Prize aGBP10. Three canoes to start or no race. Second prize aGBP5. Entrance 10s. The same course and distance as 1st Race, one course only. *(Jan 29, 1856,Daily Southern Cross,4)*  
> The tiwai canoes did not put in an appearance [in the regatta] and fortunately so, for it would have been impossible for such little craft to live in a sea like that of yesterday. *(Apr 30, 1868,Daily Southern Cross,3)*  
> .. the canoe being a tiwai, or one without top-sides, took in the slight ripple to windward ... *(Mar 14, 1846,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,5)*  
> FIFTH RACE.   For Second-class Canoes (Waka Tiwai.) *(Nov 24, 1864,Daily Southern Cross,7)*  
> 13th Race - WAKA TIWAI CANOES. *(Jan 26, 1889,Observer (Auckland),10)*  
> The waka tiwai.   Plain dug-out hulls, usually in one piece, and lacking top-strakes, thwarts, floor-grating, and adornment. *(Jul 27, 1925,Evening Post,5)*  
> A short distance past the park-like area where we lunched, there lay on the bank of a sedgy stream a relic of the past, a small dugout canoe (waka tiwai, or river canoe), one of the few not in a museum. *(Oct 6, 1932,Evening Post,10)*  
> AUCKLAND REGATTA, 1860. .. 4th - Tiwai Canoes - 1st prize, aGBP8; 2nd, aGBP3. *(Jan 24, 1860,Daily Southern Cross,2)*

### waka tour
  
noun.  
> Waka tours ranging from two to four days, take in the scenic middle reaches of the river. *(Jan 17, 2006,Dominion Post,A10)*

### waka tupapaku
  
noun. [Maori] a burial chest  
> The bones were scraped and covered with red ochre, the chiefly colour, and then placed in a waka tupapaku, a carved treasurebox. *(Apr 19, 2006,Whakatane Beacon,2)*  
> Waka tupapaku were made only for the bones of chiefs or other notable people. *(Aug 16, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener))*

### waka wairua
  
phrase. [Maori]  
> Amid interjections and passionate speeches not on the programme, Bishop Kitto steered the "waka wairua" (mixed faith assembly) confidently through the service. *(Mar 27, 2005,NZ Catholic,6)*

### waka-hopping
  
noun.  
> Martin Devlin is not in Quinn's class but his departure also goes beyond the usual waka-hopping endemic to broadcasting and journalism. *(Nov 6, 2004,Dominion Post,D8)*

### waka-jump
  
verb. [Politics] to leave one political party for another  
> It took months for a coalition Government to emerge..with many MPs "waka-jumping" to other parties. *(Jul 19, 2005,NZ Herald,A14)*

### waka-jumper
  
noun. MP who changes his or her political party after election.  
See: party-hopper  
> I'm not a waka jumper, but if people don't want me in the waka, they want me to walk the plank, then so be it. *(Dec 16, 2000,Dominion,1)*  
> Where the electorate was merciless was with the NZ First and former NZ First Maori MPs - colloquially dubbed the waka jumpers - who received an (sic) complete thrashing. *(Nov 29, 1999,Dominion,14)*  
> Corkery could do the lead in <i>Mad About Me</i> and waka jumper Alamein Kopu could be a war correspondent on the "current" affairs show, <i>5.30 with Tau</i>. *(Dec 4, 1999,Dominion,27)*  
> Waka jumpers : Tau Henare. Tukuroirangi Morgan, Rana Waitai and Tuariki Delamere thought that Maori voters had given them a personal mandate in 1996. *(Dec 20, 1999,Dominion,2)*  
> I  [sc. Owen Jennings] must inform you that I am alive and well and have recently been re-elected as No 6 on our party list. Nor have I defected in anticipation of the waka jumpers Bill. *(Dec 24, 1999,Evening Post,4)*  
> Now, Anderton's hobnailed, steel-capped, waka-jumper-kicking boot is on the other foot. *(May 7, 2002,Evening Post,7)*  
> They didn't vote me in for a separate Maori party and they didn't vote me in as an independent ... so it would be unfair to be a waka jumper at this particular time. *(Feb 15, 2001,NZ Herald,00)*  
> Peters, he said, is the worst thing to happen to Maori in 20 years and Henare is described as a"waka-jumper". *(Aug 22, 1999,Sunday Star Times,C1)*  
> Mr Samuels said he was not a "waka-jumper", or party-hopper, but he hinted that Labour was making him look elsewhere. "There's a difference between being a waka-jumper and being made to walk the plank." *(Aug 11, 2000,Dominion,2)*

### waka-jumping
  
noun. [Politics] the leaving of one political party to another.  
[NOTES: cf. party-hopping]  
> "Maori voted for NZ First thinking they would get a coalition with Labour, then they were betrayed a second time, with the waka jumping from NZ First," Mr Samuels says. *(Sep 20, 1999,Dominion,6)*  
> How ironic that Helen Clark, whose government introduced no-waka-jumping law, should now be contemplating doing the biggest waka jump - out of Parliament. *(Feb 25, 2009,Dominion Post,B4)*  
> That meant she could not be sacked from parliament under "waka jumping legislation" put in place to prevent the proportion of power allocated to each party from being distorted. *(Feb 13, 2004,Dominion Post,A2)*  
> [heading] Herald columnist Jim Hopkins argues against waka-jumping laws *(Feb 27, 2006,NZ Herald,A11)*  
> The Electoral Integrity (Amendment) Bill - the "waka-jumping bill " - will breathe new life into law that expired when the House rose last year. *(Jan 12, 2006,Dominion Post,B4)*  
> Donna Awatere Huata lost her bid to beat the waka-jumping law because she distorted the proportionality of parties in Parliament ... *(Nov 19, 2004,Dominion Post,A2)*  
> Introduction of what has been called 'the waka-jumping Bill". *(Nov 30, 1999,TV3,Evening News)*  
> [Hdg] Waka-jumping Bill still uncertain. Mana Motuhake leader Willie Jackson is refusing to say whether he will support waka-jumping legislation in the Electoral Integrity Bill. *(Aug 27, 2001,Evening Post,12)*  
> Gordon Copeland's waka-jumping shows a Government fragmenting at the edges. *(May 17, 2007,NZ Herald,A6)*

### wakaka
  
noun. ?misprint [check transcription] for MAKAKA  
> **HOOKER** Wakaka, <i>Lyall. Carmichaelia australis. </i> *(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),769)*

### wakaload
  
noun. the number of people carried in a waka  
> Korokoro, who had remained in Whangaroa, arrived with 10 wakaloads of warriors, and he and Duaterra staged a mock battle for the entertainment of the Europeans. *(2001,National Geographic Magazine,#77, Jan - Feb 2006, p110)*

### wakaport
  
noun.  
> I write to respond to Mirek Marcanik.. about the "wakaport" (as he calls it) on the waterfront... The brief for a waka house was expanded to include facilities such as a meeting house and atea (open meeting space), cafe and a carving facility... The waka house has the potential to be a very exciting project for our waterfront. *(Oct 27, 2001,Evening Post,10)*

### wakatapu
  
verb.  
[NOTES: (?whaka)]  
> **TAYLOR** Ihaia inquired what was meant by St Paul calling himself a prisoner of the Lord whether it meant that he was wakatapued to preach the Gospel. *(Apr 9, 1847,Journal,V/17)*

### wake
  
verb. keep a vigil over (a person's body)  
[NOTES: SOED says 'now dial., esp. Anglo-Irish']  
> As we have waked Kevin over the last few days, I have been very conscious of the importance of the stories we tell. In particular..that these stories operate not only to give meaning to a life but also define a person. *(May 2005,Wellington Archdiocesan News,6)*

### wake it up
  
exclamation. get on with it!  
> **McGILL** <i>wake it up!</i> eg `Wake it up, will you, we haven't got all day to do this.' NZA. *(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,121)*

### wake up to oneself
  
phrase. recognise one's own deficiency  
> **McGILL** <i>wake up to oneself</i> recognise your own deficiency, start accepting responsibility for your actions; eg `Wake up to yourself. You're a mess, you look like you've been through the wringer backwards.' *(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,121)*

### Wakefield
  
noun.  
> **SINCLAIR** In these important respects Wakefield's system offered useful, practical hints for colonizers. On the whole, however, it failed to work. The experiments in New Zealand produced colonies-but not `Wakefield' colonies. It proved impossible to regulate either the expansion of the frontier or the form of class structure by means of a price upon land. *(1959,History of New Zealand,91)*

### Wakefield Plan
  
noun.  
> <i>Failure of the Wakefield Plan </i> *(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,42)*  
> It is an interesting fact that, in spite of the contempt wih [sic] which Mr. Wakefield's plan of colonisation would be received to-day, the ideal - if I may call it an ideal - for which he strove has been realised in this twentieth century, and in New Zealand! *(Aug 17, 1911,Grey River Argus,1)*  
> The great proconsul Sir George Grey, following the Wakefield plan, was in favour of denominational schools aided by the State, and this was the system he established in the Auckland province after an attempt to give it a much wider scope. *(Jun 1, 1929,Evening Post,21)*  
> The German Colonization Company will be made after the Wakefield plan. *(May 7, 1842,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3)*  
> We have to do, not with the Wakefield plan, which aimed at importing the capitalist and labourer together, but with a system the very reverse, where the penniless man is the pioneer, settling on gift land, whence arises no land revenue. *(Sep 24, 1864,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,2)*  
> SECTION 17, Wakefield Plan, NAYLAND STREET, 33 perches, on tram-line, with private entrance to Beach. *(Jan 25, 1908,Star, The,5)*  
> **BURNS** The Wakefield Plan was based on the assumption that vast areas - if possible every acre - of New Zealand would be bought for a trifle, the real payment to the people of the land being their "civilising" through colonisation. *(1989,Fatal Success,14)*

### Wakefield settlement
  
noun.  
> **REED** [title] [subtitle] LAST WAKEFIELD SETTLEMENT *(1947,Story of Canterbury, The)*  
> **McLINTOCK** From the stagnation they faced in the early 1840s, the Wakefield settlements were rescued by the energy of the young `squatters'. *(1959,Descriptive Atlas,38)*

### Wakefield system
  
noun.  
> **MILLER** 'The Wakefield System', declared William Fox on the eve of his departure for New Zealand, 'is one of those discoveries which is so simple that its truth must be apparent to the meanest apprehension.'.... The Wakefield System was a product of hard reasoning. *(1958,Early Victorian NZ,5)*  
> [New Zealand Colonization Dinner] ... the learned Professor concluded by drinking, `Mr. Wakefield - the author of the Wakefield system of Emigration ...' *(Oct 26, 1839,Glasgow Constitutional)*  
> **HEAPHY** The happy effect of the `Wakefield system', has been to cause the supply of labour and the demand to be at all times equal, ... *(1842,New Zealand (Heaphy),68)*  
> **MILLER** The Wakefield System is one of these discoveries which is so simple that it's truth must be apparent to the meanest apprehension. *(1958,Early Victorian NZ,5)* [NOTES: 1842 Fox Colonization and NZ 6 in 1958 Miller Early Victorian NZ 5]  
> **MILLER** [The tendency of the Company's lottery system, Greenwood concluded,`] is to produce dispersion, when the very principle of the Wakefield System is concentration and co-operation.['] *(1958,Early Victorian NZ,109)* [NOTES: 1843 June 7 J.D. Greenwood in The Greenwood Letters [TS] in 1958 Miller Early Victorian NZ 109]  
> **WOOD** The `<i>sufficient</i>' price of the Wakefield system is a fallacy ... Waste land is often dear at a gift. *(1843,Twelve Months in Wellington,50)*  
> **COAD** You see, Wellington was colonised under the Wakefield system and - *(1931,Such Is Life,36)*  
> **COWAN** ... the few farmers did not have enough capital to buy sheep runs ...for land under the Wakefield System was [pounds]2 and acre ... *(1948,Down the Years in the Maniototo,8)*  
> A book could be written about the consequences of wool. For one thing Wakefield's `system', which required smallish farms and numerous farm employees, would not work. *(1961,Distance Looks Our Way,31)* [NOTES: (Sinclair)]  
> I believe, - The Wakefield system to be all rot. *(Oct 21, 1854,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,2)*  
> The two latter [Wakefield brothers] were buried in the Wellington cemetery;  and the remains of the author of the Wakefield System of Colonization will rest beside those of his kindred in that settlement which he was chiefly instrumental in founding, which will be an enduring monument to after-times of his abiiity [sic] and energy;  which his name will be remembered with honour among those who have deserved well of their adopted country. *(May 28, 1862,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,2)*  
> He advocates the adoption of the Wakefield system, and in the event of responsible government being granted to Western Australia, that a large portion of the land revenue should be devoted towards promoting immigration. *(Dec 30, 1887,Otago Witness,23)*  
> **BURNS** The Wakefield System attached great importance to the concept of the colony being established as a complete community, and this meant that all the landowners and their servants would arrive simultaneously. <i>Ibid.</i> 276 Those whose gardens were immature or new, or who had not tried to profit from Fox's bending of the Wakefield system to give them small holdings, were forced on to a diet of potatoes or gruel made from flour *(1989,Fatal Success,100)*

### Wakefield theory
  
noun.  
> **FOX** There is no doubt ... that the original intention was to carry out the Wakefield theory, and there is no doubt at all that the basis of that theory is a high price laid on for the avowed purpose of preventing the labouring man from acquiring land too quickly. *(Dec 31, 1858,Letter)* [NOTES: 1858 Dec 31 Fox to Godley in 1971 Speeches and Documents 34]  
> **BURDON** His views, known as the `Wakefield Theory', were not formed at once but evolved over a period of years. *(1938,High Country,3)*  
> The prosperity is assigned to two causes;  first that Canterbury has put in practice the Wakefield theory of a "sufficient price" for the land, alienating none at a less price than aGBP2 per acre, while other Provinces have parted with their estates to capitalists and speculators at 10s and 5s per acre. *(Oct 10, 1873,Wanganui Herald,2)*  
> Whatever the merits of the Wakefield theory of colonisation, it is certain that the first attempts to carry them into practice were made as if by 'prentice hands - indeed, the most fixed and reliable feature of the arrangements seemed to be the price charged for the land - namely, aGBP1 per acre at Wellington and New Plymouth and 30s at Nelson. *(Aug 26, 1887,Otago Witness,16)*

### Wakefieldian
  
noun.  
> **SINCLAIR** All of the districts where the Wakefieldians had established their settlements, with the exception of Taranaki, were pre-eminently suitable for sheep. *(1959,History of New Zealand,92)*  
> It was accordingly in December, 1830, that the Wakefieldians directed their attention to the "magnificent river just discovered by Captain Sturt," as marking the region appropriate for a new settlement. *(Jan 7, 1930,Evening Post,7)*

### wake-ups
  
adjective. awake, alert  
> **SARGESON** The pair of us were wake-ups when we heard somebody coming up the stairs *(1946,That Summer,92)*

### wakey
  
noun.  
> **HUGHES** Any full 24-hour period was called 'a sleep' and any smaller portion was 'a wakey'. *(1999,Shooting from the Lip,26)*

### wakka
  
noun.  
See: waka

### walk
  
noun. a postie's beat  
> **McKAY** Each postal district is divided into walks, and over a period of time a postie works in several. *(1990,James K. Baxter,193)*

### walk about
  
noun. -  
> **WILKES** [They] were met by a large party of natives, who kept on saying to them, <i>`Walk about one hilling'</i>, by which they soon understood that they were required to pay one shilling for the privilege of walking on the beach and picking up shells; ... *(1852,U.S. Exploring Expedition,1/307)* [NOTES: 1840 Mar in 1852 Wilkes U.S. Exploring Expedition 1/307]  
> **Pfaff** [Simon a Maori speaks] ... we go to Christchurch and speak to many mans, but no one know you. We make a walk about all day, ... but we no see you working. *(1914,Diggers' Story, The,95)*

### walk into
  
verb. get stuck into (food)  
> **PHILLIPS** ... they called at Leachs' did not find him home - nevertheless they walked into some of his mutton and Beer pretty considerably and ... once more got on the road [63] and made tracks for Rockwood ... *(Dec 31, 1856,Journal Kept at Rockwood and the Point,62)*

### walk off
  
verb. to leave, abandon as uneconomic (a farm or land) (also absolute use)  
[NOTES: ?a depression coinage]

### walk off
  
verb. to abandon land under economic pressure, [esp. in 1880s, 1930s and 1980s, but still used. ]  
> NZ Farmer Aug 2. 5 Farmers have walked off buttercup-infested property such as this. *(1951)*  
> TAIT Farms & Stations of New Zealand I 134 The land had been abandoned by two previous owners, the second walking off in 1936. He was another bloke who walked off in the thirties. *(1956)*  
> ANDERSON A Life's Roundabout 169 The 1921 slump caught them off balance and, as happened to so many King Country settlers, they had to walk off. *(1962)*  
> GASCOIGNE From the Top of a Hill 23 The 'slump' finished them and they walked off the place without a penny. *(1965)*  
> STACEY Tales of a Tussock Jumper 16 .. returned men were put on to areas called farms, which gave little in return for immense labour. The majority walked off in a few years. *(1996)*  
> KERR High Times in the High Country 69 Mid Dome's owners walked off without a razoo in the 1930s and three tough brothers from Awaka walked on ... *(2000)*  
> Country Life Radio July 19 Farmers near Mercer will have to walk off their land if stopbanks aren't built on the Waikato river. *(2002)*  
> **GRANT** caption `WALKING OFF: Processes of Rural Development'; with pictures captioned: `The profits walk off', `The men walk of', `The farmer walks off' [in an expensive car], `The farm walks off' *(1980,Unauthorised Version,124)* [NOTES: 1930 Blomfield cartoon in Observer in 1980 Grant Unauthorised Version 124]  
> **BACON** ' ... Have you heard of the soldier settlement they had out here after the First War? ... ` ' ... The Government partly developed the land, then put returned soldier farmers on it. One by one they went broke and walked off. ... Now they say the Government is going to try again. This new aerial top dressing may make a difference.' <i>Ibid.</I89 After all, it is Crown land; the leases reverted to the Crown after the chaps walked off. *(1963,In the Sticks,62)*  
> **BRUCE** Block 3: Taken up in 1919 and abandoned 15 years late, ... Held for 10 years by the second occupier, who also walked off. *(1977,Life in the Hinterland of the South Island,62)*  
> **HUNT** But there were no cattle coming up to listen. ... They'd been in there fern crushing, hundreds of acres of bracken. Men'd just walked off that land in the slump but those cattle were breaking it all in again. *(1981,Speaking a Silence,46)*  
> **HOOPER** These swaggers think every farmer's made of money. Yet all over the country men are walking off farms, bankrupt. *(1981,Goat Paddock,202)*  
> **HALEY** `Those farmers will be walking off their land if it doesn't rain soon,' Walter said. *(1986,Settlement, The,52)*  
> That is the amount a farmer will get if he is forced to walk off his land. *(Jul 19, 1986,Dominion,1)*  
> Mr Alley bought a Taranaki farm ... He walked off the land in November 1926 ... *(Dec 28, 1987,Dominion,2)*  
> **CAMPBELL** Some have chucked up everything and walked off their farms. *(1989,Frigate Bird,112)*  
> John had bought the house the year the war began, when they walked off the farm and moved back to Auckland with the two children. *(2001,Huia 4: Contemp. Maori Fiction,210)*  
> Neilson did not reduce the price, and in July last judgment was given for aGBP634 arrears of interest, whereupon the bankrupt walked off the farm. *(Oct 9, 1926,Evening Post,10)*

### walk off
  
verb.  
[NOTES: to have been stolen; action attributed to the items stolen]  
> One of the learned and scientific Doctor's fishy preparations performed, though not offensively, the Town Hall, from whence [sic] the Worshipful the May's Pilot coat has been stolen, and a pair of blue trowsers [sic] have walked off, presented to the "Fire Relief Committee." *(Dec 16, 1842,New Zealand Colonist & Port Nicholson Advertiser,2)*  
> WALKED OFF.   DARING THEFT OF WHISKY. *(Jul 6, 1909,Marlborough Express,8)*

### walk one's chalks
  
phrase. to move on; to leave  
[NOTES: [?if this isn't a pun on a milkman chalking as as well as watering, the milk.]]  
> **THATCHER** Milkmen give their customers warning,/ They're leaving their usual walks,/ And off to the Wakamarina/ Old Skyblue is walking his chalks. *(1864,Songs of the War,5)*

### walk one's ferret
  
phrase. to fornicate  
See: -  
> **DAVIN** And how are you, you old bastard? You look seedy. Been walking your ferret too often, I expect. *(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,194)*

### walk shorts
  
noun.  
> The motor camp is guarded by the officious walkshort-wearing Gary, in a superb cameo appearance by Ian Mune. *(Mar 12, 2000,Dominion Sunday Times,F4)*  
> The prospect is poor solace to a restless talent: a road works sign, a construction site and a public servant in mustard-coloured walk shorts. *(Jan 31, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14)*  
> Dr Turner trained with the New Zealand Electricity Department back in the walk-shorts and cardigan days of public service. *(Oct 6, 1999,Dominion,24)*  
> Wellington's designers have buried the image of the capital's citizens as walkshorts and socks wearers. *(Oct 28, 1999,City Voice,8)*  
> New Zealand did, he says, give the world walk-shorts and long socks teamed with sandals. *(Nov 30, 1999,Evening Post,Looking Back : 18)*  
> What makes it a female dictatorship is when a male had the tenacity to get up and request he be able to wear dress shorts. *(Mar 26, 2000,Sunday Star Times,A8)*  
> Bring back walk shorts...Why New Zealand males now insist on wearing hot suit trousers through summer is a mystery. *(Dec 19, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),23)*  
> First there are those New Zealand words [in Collins English Dictionary, 2nd ed.] ... for example, ... <i>solo mother, walk socks</i> (but not <i>walk shorts), block, kiwi</i> (as in Golden Kiwi), <i>mate's rates, ... Maori Warden. </i> *(Feb 27, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),65)* [NOTES: (G. Kennedy)]  
> A city long derided for walk-shorts, wind and obscure dependence on the taxpayer has come to feel like one of the coolest places on Earth. *(Dec 13, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15)*  
> Lambert admits to having been wedded to a genre of outfit almost too excruciating to mention - walk shorts and long socks. He says when he arrived in New Zealand with his brand-new New Zealand wife in 1976 his father-in-law met him on the wharf with shorts and socks. He said 'this is what you Kiwis wear.' *(Feb 7, 2001,Evening Post,19)*  
> Because the word "department" suggests a Roger Hall play with walkshorts, cardigans, meat-paste sandwiches and wrapover hair, the place has been reinvented as the altogether funkier Statistics New Zealand. *(Dec 30, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),60)*  
> I have a confession to make: I wear shinpads. Not to work - they don't go well with walkshorts. *(Jun 19, 2001,Evening Post,28)*  
> Peter's family, from a Sydney suburb, also opted for bare legs, including good old Jim - in his walkshorts, long white socks and a cardigan with loose stitches at each elbow, the guy looked as though he was about to conquer nothing more arduous than a stroll to the local chemist. *(Jan 16, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28)*  
> Wall Street dressing killed the great New Zealand walk shorts with long socks and romans sandals look, says Labrum. *(Dec 18, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),21)*  
> Wellington's walkshort-wearing public servants have been replaced by a more dynamic breed of laptop toting latter drinkers. *(May 24, 2000,Capital Times,5)*  
> Tailored walk shorts are the mark of a well balanced, conventional common sense Kiwi male who refuses to become a slave to today's ridiculous fashions. *(Mar 11, 1993,Contact (Wgtn),10)*  
> Walk shorts were brought home from hotter climates by World War II servicemen, and by the late 1960s polyester versions, teamed with walk socks and sandals, had become popular summer businesswear, particularly among civil servants. By the 1980s they had come to represent the ultimate in uncool. *(Nov 29, 2001,Dominion,13)*  
> Why the prissy things are called walk shorts is beyond analysis, since they serve for running, jumping or just standing still, a well as for walking. *(Jan 29, 2006,Sunday Star Times,C9)*  
> The men are in walk-shorts, the women nestle old-fashioned handbags, and the mood is unmistakably bent on revenge. *(Feb 12, 1992,Dominion,10)*  
> More and more city offices and shops aremaking a concession to the warmer weatherby allowing their male staff members towear neat walk shorts and knee-high socksto work during the summer months. *(Jan 5, 1968,Evening Post,8)*  
> A grey, watery man announcing that "Napier is the walk-short capital of New Zealand". *(Nov 12, 2003,Capital Times,9)*  
> The decline of the walkshort and the rise of the Madonna impersonator was deemed more worthy of screen time. *(Dec 6, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28)*  
> And who said the Statistics New Zealand, with their means and averages, are a bunch of walk-short-wearing bureaucrats living in the past. *(Nov 25, 2003,Independent Herald (Wellington),14)*  
> He has worked with government departmentswhere walkshorts and sandals are stillthe most favoured uniform. *(Apr 23, 1995,Sunday Star Times,D1)*  
> Walk outside in your walk shorts,jandals, and brand new T-shirt with "I'vebeen to Ayers Rock" on the front... Pull a small spanner from your walk shorts pocket, lean over the engine, and begin making tapping noises. *(Dec 28, 1995,Dominion,2)*  
> His passion for walk shorts drew attention to the bulge drooping like a sack of jelly over his belt. *(Nov 21, 1992,Dominion,10)*  
> We all agree there's no going back to the days when far too many public servants were blank-faced, walkshorts-wearing job's worths who did as they were bid - albiet not as quickly or efficiently as we would have liked. ... [19] Add to this mix the Maori sovereignty movement, which regards the will of government as impertinent when it comes to Maori self-determination, and you're in a different galaxy from Planet Walkshorts. *(Aug 14, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18)*

> 30% OFF ALL WALK SOCKS. *(Jan 11, 1988,Evening Post,14)*  
> Kennedy)First there are those New Zealand words [in Collins English Dictionary, 2nd ed.] ... for example, ... <i>solo mother, walk socks</i> (but not <i>walk shorts), block, kiwi</i> (as in Golden Kiwi), <i>mate's rates, ... Maori Warden. </i> *(Feb 27, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),65)* [NOTES: (G. Kennedy)]

### walk up
  
verb. [Rural] [as a command to a heading dog] to approach sheep slowly.  
> **LOUGHNAN** walk up: a command given to dogs to approach sheep slowly and deliberately. *(1981,Loughnan Glossary,90)*  
> Walk-up - The command to bring a heading dog smoothly and slowly onto sheep - not tearing in and doing a spot of wool-classing. A lot of the purists teach the walk by putting the dog into a harness and walking it. An alternative .. until it realises that the new 'walk-up' command means exactly that. *(1984,NZ Journal of Agriculture,Jan/Feb 13)*

noun.

### Walker's weed
  
noun. redshank  
[NOTES: (West Coast)]  
> **HILGENDORF** REDSHANK (<i>Polygonum persicaria</i>) is also called persicaria, spotted persicaria, knot weed, smart weed, willow weed, and lady's thumb. Perhaps also it is this weed that on the west coast of the South Island is called water pepper or Walker's weed. It is an annual weed of gardens, crops, and wet pastures. *(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,59,1967)*

### walkie-chalkie
  
noun.  
> **MURIEL DALTON** <i>WALKIE-CHALKIE</i> First noticed this some months ago in a letter to the ... EVENING POST (Wellington). It refers to the traffic man who chalks the tyre line of cars parked by meters. It was so apt and entertaining it seems to have gained currency locally. *(Nov 22, 1957,Letter)* [NOTES: Upper Hutt]

### walking beat
  
noun. a high country mustering beat where terrain is too steep to muster on horseback [usually the top beat.]  
> MCLEOD NZ High Country 24 His horse has been taken by one of the lower men, and with his long mustering stick in his hand, he has a walking beat. *(1951)*

### walking country
  
noun. [Rural] [high country mustering] a grazing area where land is too steep, rough and rocky to muster on horseback.  
> **NEWTON** It was all walking country and no hacks were taken there, the musterers travelling either per 'Harry Pannel' (walking) or riding on the pack dray. *(1947,Wayleggo,35)*  
> **NEWTON** That old chap actually dropped dead on the hill - on walking country when mustering at round about seventy years of age. *(1964,Straggle Muster,135)*  
> **MILLS ET AL** While tractors, trucks and jeeps are now largely used on flat and lowland farms, on any other than 'walking country' the hack comes into his own. *(1964,Practical Guide to Handling Dogs and Stock,64)*  
> **NEWTON** The mustering of the Ngamatea country alone - excluding Timahanga and Pohokura - takes forty-five days: ten days are straight-out walking country and there another five days on which hacks can only be used on some of the easier beats. *(1969,Big Country of the North Island,149)*  
> **KERR** Walking country is rough, steep and rocky, not suitable for horses. *(2000,High Times in the High Country,45)*  
> **KERR** My country's very cold. Hard. Don't want the sheep knocked about. It's all walking country. Think you'll stand up to it? *(2000,High Times in the High Country,95)*  
> **NEWTON** It was all walking country ... the musterers travelling either per Harry Pannell (walking) or riding in the pack dray. *(1947,Wayleggo,35)*  
> **NEWTON** <i>Walking Country</i>: Most South Island high country is too steep and rugged to be traversed on horseback. This is termed `walking country' as apart from easier and rideable country. *(1966,Boss's Story, The,190)*  
> **NEWTON** He was twelve then and he mustered on that class of country - all walking country - until he finally gave the game best at Winterslow in 1958. *(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,87)*  
> Continually on your feet, up hills and down gullies, here, there, and everywhere, driving pegs, setting up flags on points often miles apart in the roughest of walking country, clearing scrub, chipping lines, &c., &c., &c. *(Sep 11, 1880,Otago Witness,18)*

### walking country
  
noun.  
See: -  
[NOTES: (musterers')]

### walking fern
  
noun.  
> Similar plantlets are produced by the walking fern (<i>Asplenium flabellifolium</i>). In this species, the frond usually produces a single bulbil at the very tip, so that when it arches over and touches the soil, the bulbil roots and grows out into a new plant. This growth habit makes it an excellent subject for a hanging basket. *(Jan 2001,NZ Geographic,No.49/69)*

### walking muster
  
noun. [Rural] a muster where terrain is too steep for horses.  
> **SINCLAIR** It was a walking muster and seven of us didn't finish with more than fifty sheep at the end of the first day. *(1973,Boot in the Stirrup,50)*  
> **SINCLAIR** The Harkness block was another walking muster *(1973,Boot in the Stirrup,52)*

### walking school bus
  
noun.  
[NOTES: also (see below) walking bus, (?walking school) van]  
> Churton Park School children are the latest to benefit from Wellington City Council's walking school bus scheme. *(Oct 27, 2004,Northern Courier,6)*  
> One safety procedure that is being encouraged is the "walking school-bus", in which parents will take turns leading groups of kids on a set walking-route to school. Since its arrival in New Zealand in 1999, introduced initially into Gladstone Primary in Mt Albert, Auckland, and Beckenham Primary in Christchurch. the scheme has now reached more than 120 schools. *(Oct 4, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24)*  
> It's the beginning of a trial for the area's first "Walking School Bus" to make the journey to school and home again safer, particularly for very junior pupils... The "walking bus" originated in Christchurch and there are "buses" now in Auckland and Wellington's Newtown. *(Aug 28, 2002,Norwester,5)*  
> Since last November, St Annes school in Newtown has been running three walking school bus routes. Teachers and/or parents walk with the children along a set route, picking up other children along the way. *(Jun 2002,Elements,17/6)*  
> Walking school buses or "vans", smaller groups of children, are led by an adult who collects them at stops on the route. *(Nov 8, 2003,Dominion Post,A7)*  
> Cashmere Avenue School in Khandallah has joined the walking school bus scheme which allows groups of children to walk to school under adult supervision. *(Nov 6, 2002,Dominion Post,A7)*  
> Changes include additional pedestrian crossings and "walking school buses"... [He] said his school now had two walking buses, more parents were car pooling and drop-off and pick-up points had been designated for those who still wanted to drive their children to school. *(Jan 25, 2004,Sunday Star Times,A10)*  
> The Khandallah primary school is about to join the growing list of local schools to adopt the walking bus scheme which allows children to walk to and from school together under adult supervision instead of travelling by car. *(Nov 13, 2002,Northern Courier,2)*  
> More than 240 walking school buses take 3000 children to and from school every day around New Zealand. Walking buses operate like a car pool, with rostered parents volunteers picking children up from their homes and walking along a designated route before dropping them at school. *(Oct 9, 2003,Dominion Post,A5)*

### walking-leaf
  
noun.  
> **MORRIS** <i>Walking-leaf, n.</i> See <i>Phasmid. </i> *(1898,Austral English,494)*

### walking-stick
  
noun.  
[NOTES: ca1935 Havelock children Usually<i> stick insects</i>, but occasionally <i>walking-sticks</i>; and felt as derived from the notion of resemblance to a walkingstick, rather than from one of a moving stick or twig.]  
> (Bathgate) One of these [strange insects found in Otago] is the `walking-stick' of the colonists, which is an apterous insect, belonging to the order <i>Orthoptera </i>and the tribe Phasiuma [Phasma]. It derives its name from an extraordinary resemblance to a twig. *(1870,TrNZI,3/138)*  
> The application of the word `Phasiuma' to the insect commonly called the `walking-stick' is probably an error of the printer. It should be `Phasma', and the hyphen placed between `walking' and `stick' should be omitted - the common name having been given from its resemblance to an animated twig, and not (as the hyphen would suggest) from any resemblance to a stick we use in walking. *(1871,TrNZI,4/216)* [NOTES: (Fereday)]  
> **DOMETT** ... walking stick insects ... *(1872,Ranolf and Amohia,305)*  
> **DOMETT** Phasmid. The `Walking Stick' .. O. Orthoptera; G. Phasmidae. *(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/305)*  
> **MORRIS** <i>Walking-stick, n.</i> See Phasmid. *(1898,Austral English,494)*

noun. get (be given) one's walking-ticket: to be dismissed  
[NOTES: (?orig station) ca1949 Havelock: still in use. -]

> **LANCASTER** Reckon tha fancy cutter'll get his walking-ticket over it. *(1905,Sons o' Men,80)*

### walk-shorted
  
adjective. wearing walk shorts  
> Both the walk-shorted, bow-legged, socks-up driver and the happy-go-lucky guitar man were almost caricatures, but so what? *(Feb 28, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),6)*

### walk-through
  
noun. [Rural] a milking shed designed to allow access from either end.  
> **POWDRELL** There are many types of cowshed, for example .. and the Walk-through type of shed. *(1920,Dairy Farming in NZ,22)*  
> **MCMEEKAN** The walk-through shed is strictly a New Zealand invention ... *(1960,Grass to Milk,151)*  
> Most cowsheds that have been converted from walk-through to the step-up design have had the number of bails increased. *(Feb 10, 1966,NZ Farmer,4)*  
> In the 1983 winter, the 8-bail walkthrough was converted to an 18-a-side herringbone with help of casual labour. *(Mar 1987,NZ Dairy Exporter,26)*  
> **WASWO** The walk-through milking shed was the standard in New Zealand until the early 1950s when the herringbone shed was introduced. ..the herringbone rapidly took over from the walk-through and soon became virtually the standard system of milking throughout New Zealand. *(1996,Farming Progress in NZ 1814-1995,89)*  
> The ruination came because of his strong suggestion they should replace their old walk-through with a 14-a-side herringbone shed so they could then milk 115 cows. [walkthrough] *(Jun 7, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,17)*

### walk-through
  
noun. a type of milking shed (cf. HERRINGBONE)  
> **POWDRELL** There are many types of cow-shed, for example the Race, the Double bail, the Single back-out, and the Walk-through type of shed. *(1920,Dairy Farming in NZ,22)*  
> In modern sheds the walk-through type of bail has completely superseded the old back-out type. *(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,456)*  
>  *(Apr 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,374/3)*  
> Farm dairies are of various types. Most of them used to be of the `walk-through' type, where cows stand side by side and are milked by pipeline milking machines. *(1974,NZ Journal of Agriculture,71)*  
> Those were times of six sets of cups in a walk-through shed; back chains, legropes - those filthy things. *(Jan 1972,NZ Dairy Exporter,39)*  
> **McMEEKAN** In the walk-through shed, cows are milked in paired bails... They are restrained only with a back chain... They leave the bail by walking straight ahead and moving along an internal race back to the holding yard. *(1960,Grass to Milk,149)*  
> A Te Awamutu sharemilker recently converted an old, four-bail, doubled-up walk-through into an eight-a-side, high-line herringbone for a total cost of $1700. *(Nov 1971,NZ Dairy Exporter,15)*

### walkthrough cowshed
  
noun. a shed in which cows stand on floor level or on a raised step and are milked from the side.  
> When the cow was milked her leg was released and a long pole handle opened a door in front of the cow and she walked out into a concrete race. The door was pulled shut, the chain removed and the next cow came in. This was known as the 'Walk Through Shed' and was superior to the type where the cows were backed out. *(Dec 2003,NZ Memories,No. 45/11)*  
> Walkthrough cowshed. Cows stand on floor level or raised step and are milked from the side. Cows leave each bail through the front. *(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,39)*

### walkway
  
noun. a recognised path in reserve or public areas, a nature walk  
> [Hdg] Porirua City's walkway pamphlet. Porirua City Council has recently updated the information to show the sealed and unsealed cycle tracks within the city. Many people know about the award winning Ara Harakeke walkway between Plimmerton and Pukerua Bay... *(Jan 29, 2004,Wellington Contact,32)*  
> The bottom half of the walkway has been closed for considerable time for repairs estimated to cost at least $80,000. *(Oct 2000,NZ Wilderness,10)*  
> **SANSOM** With [the titi] were scatterings of the smaller titi-wainui, the Fairy Prions, and a sprinkling of kuaka, the Southern Diving Petrels, which ducked and rose on the outskirts. *(1982,In the Grip of an Island,91)* [NOTES: [check???]]  
> **MANTELL** The walkway had been placed in one of the latter areas [of bush]. Wide enough - four feet - and sealed *(1980,Murder or Three, A,13)*  
> <i>walkway n. </i>passage or path, for walking along, esp. (<i>NZ</i>) public right of way in the countryside. *(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,868)* [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]  
> "There are a dozen or more private walkways on private land around New Zealand now.... These have all been set up offering a whole new way of getting into the indoors."... *(Nov 9, 2002,Dominion Post,F12)*

### walkway
  
noun. a catwalk or shelf on stockyards or saleyards [that enables drafters, pickers, buyers and sellers to see pens or yards of stock.]

noun.

noun.

### wall lettuce
  
noun. <i>Mycelis muralis </i>(L.) Rehb.  
> **GUTHRIE-SMITH** ... winged gossamer of alien bush-edge weeds - wall lettuce, Canadian groundsell and hawkweed. *(1936,NZ Naturalist,65)*  
> **TAYLOR** WALL LETTUCE (<i>Mycelis muralis</i>) Short-lived perennial native to Europe ... most abundant in some forest margins of the South Island. .. *(1981,Weeds of Roadsides,163)*  
> **WEBB et al.** <i>M[ycelis] muralis</i>... wall lettuce... Def. Glabrous perennial... Coromandel southwards... Forest and scrub., forest margins, stream beds, pastures, lakeshores, almost always in shaded sites. (Europe, Asia Minor, N. Africa, 1899). *(1988,Flora NZ,IV/399)*  
> <i> lettuce, wall [s] Mycelis muralis </i>(L.) Rehb.<i>Ibid.</i> 83 <i>wall lettuce </i>[s]<i> Mycelis muralis</i> (L.) Rchb. *(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,44)*

### wallaby track
  
noun. the open road, chiefly in phrase on the wallaby (track): tramping the country as a swagger  
[NOTES: (orig. and mainly Aust [AND 1849]) on the wallaby, on the wallaby track]  
> **CLARKE** It is lamentable to witness the discomfiture of your after-dinner Colonist, when he meets with some unpretending fellow (who does not even swear), but who has `humped his swag' into strange lands, and traversed the `wallaby-track' under the direst conditions. *(1871,Knocking About in NZ,vi)*  
> **BELL** [a sundowner speaks] `... I could haud my ain in the Parliament Hoose ...'  `As a special representative of all sundowners and followers of the Wallaby Track, I suppose,' said M`Keown. <i>Ibid.</i> 15 O here to the joys of the Wallaby Track,/ Then it's up with the swag and the billy,/ *(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,13)*  
> **McKEE WRIGHT** The harper went on the wallaby track/ *(1900,Wisps of Tussock,1)*  
> **WRIGHT** I thought of old tramps in the summer to stations far out at the back,/ When I met with the hardest of cases that live on the wallaby track -/Men like him, full of tricks and devices, .... *(1897,Station Ballads,24,1945)* [NOTES: The Hard Case]  
> I can see him now as he came on the `Wallaby track'. *(Dec 1904,NZ Illustrated Magazine,178)*  
> <i>wallaby track, on the; on the wallaby (Phr.)</i> tramping the country carrying a swag. *(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),21)*  
> **BATHGATE** You ought to have gone up yourself, and not left it to such a born fool as Mike. He'll have to take the Wallabi [sic] track tomorrow morning. I wonder how I came to keep such an idiot on the place. *(1881,Waitaruna,167)*  
> He has "humped his bluey" through bush, "gone on the wallaby" o'er mountain and dale, and nearly 38 years ago decided that New Zealand was the land of promise for him, and the day that "Mac landed on New Zealand's shores, New Zealand gained a practical cattle and sheep man, an agriculutrist [sic] to the finger-tips, and, needless to relate, such a man soon found his services in great demand in "blazing the track" and pioneering in developing the great primary industries of a young and growing country. *(Sep 1, 1920,Poverty Bay Herald,3)*  
> Old Paddy paid a tenner/For a broken-down old Ford;/The pride and joy of Paddy's heart,/I give you, boys, my word,/If you're ever on the wallaby/You needn't walk too far,/You're always welcome to a life/In Paddy's motor-car. *(Jun 26, 1935,Evening Post,8)*  
>  *(Aug 11, 1864,Otago Daily Times,2)*  
> The diggers are coming;  oh dear, oh dear!/If you want a good dinner, 'tis her, 'tis here,/And the Governor's coming, how queer, how queer,/On the Wallaby Track, after fourpenny beer. *(Aug 11, 1864,Otago Daily Times,2)*  
> And when they do condescend to work it is usually to make a cheque as a benefit for the nearest publican to adorn his mansion internally as well as externally, spending at the rate of aGBP1000 per year for one brief week, leaving themselves penniless to repeat the same old game and "bluey up, and on the wallaby track" again. *(Jul 15, 1893,Evening Post,3)*  
> Two "diggers," overcome by the depression, decide to go on the wallaby track. *(Nov 28, 1933,Evening Post,4)*  
> If some of those benevolent employers of hired labor, who talk about immigration schemes, would take an overland trip on the wallaby, through some of the landward districts of the province, they would find the place perfectly inundated with idle men - men ready and willing to work for a bare subsistence. *(Jul 26, 1870,Southland Times,3)* [NOTES: (nb: date originally entered into database was 267/7/1870)]  
> THE FREE LANCE KANGAROO ON THE WALLABY.   In answer to numerous enquiries we have to state that our Special has finished his wallaby for the present. *(Apr 18, 1885,Observer (Auckland),12)*  
> He was aged and shabby and "on the wallaby," but his manners were ducal, his air that of one who has lived in the great world, and the simple cottager and his wife were impressed. *(Jan 14, 1911,Colonist (Nelson),4)*

### walls
  
noun.  
> **SUTTON-SMITH** There are games recorded in this period for which there is no record before 1900 though they  may well have existed under other names. <i>West Coast</i>: Dunk and Davey, Spang Weazling; <i>Wellington</i>: Who Goes Round My Stone Wall, Beg o' My Neighbor, Bellahonie, Run a Mile, On and Off the Bridge, Here Come Two Nuns, Colours, Isaac and Rebecca. Scrag, Diabolo, Bumble Puppy, Homai, Fives; <i>Auckland:</i> My Fair Young Lady, Rushes and Reeds, Surrender the Tower, Walls, Ball Hopscotch, ball games, Solomon Says; <i>Hawkes Bay:</i> Wolf Wolf Go Home, Homai; <i>Canterbury</i>: Poor Old Tom, Rats and Rabbits and Rips, Steps, Skin the Snake, Steal a Peg, Toodle-em-Buck, The Gag; <i>Golden Bay</i>: Who's That Walking Round My House Tonight, I've Come to See Poor Mary Jane, Steps and Stairs, Creeping, Branding, O'Leary; <i>Otago</i>: Sir Roger de Coverley, Who's That Knocking at My Door. <i>Ibid.</i> 273 Ball-bouncing games have come into their own on the asphalt playgrounds of modern schools and are played in many schools throughout the country. There is no evidence of such games before 1900, but a number of instances between 1900 and 1920 are recorded. The games were known as Fives, Tens, Sevens, and Walls and have been recorded more often since under the names O'Leary or Sevens. Before 1920 there is only one report of the game being played with a rhyme, and this was a rhyme more often used in skipping. *(1981,History of Children's Play,231)*
