### Walmartisation
  
noun. following the pattern set by Walmart in the USA  
> ...the sales are another blurring of the retail calendar and a further sign of the "Walmartisation" of the $3 billion-a-year department store sector - where "big shed" stores on the edge of the town buy in bulk and slash prices. *(Jul 15, 2004,Dominion Post,C1)*

### wampo
  
noun. ? beer slops; the leakage from beer taps or glasses overflowed, re-served to unsuspecting customers  
[NOTES: 1984 P.Ranby, Auckland Letter K.Smithyman `any grog' and quoted P: `intoxicating liquor' : RAF : from ca1930 (Jackson) Prob ex Scot.]  
> **MANDERSON** The resulting concoction.. is commonly called "Wampo". This name was well-known throughout Auckland among barmen. <i>Ibid. </i>14 I refused to sell the slop beer ('wampo' as it is commonly known by barmen). *(1946,Beer Slops,10)*  
> **McLAUCHLAN** `Wampo?' we muttered. `What is or was wampo?' Mr M. then told us that wampo was beer slops from overflowing glasses. In the old days some publicans kept these in a bucket, then strained them through a bar cloth back into a keg. *(1981,Acid Test, The)* [NOTES: 1957 J.R. Sheehan Truth June in 1981 McLauchlan Acid Test 72]  
> **McLAUCHLAN** Mr. M. then told us that wampo was beer slops from overflowing glasses.  In the old days some publicans kept these slops in a bucket, then strained them through a bar cloth back into a keg. *(1989,History of NZ Humour,86)* [NOTES: Quote from Sheehan 1957]  
> The word "dregs" refers to the drippings from the taps in a bar and to the overflow from the glasses as they are filled.  These dregs are sometimes called "swill".. or "slops". When they are mixed with beer for sale the resultant liquid is colloquially known as "Wompo". The term has been in use among barmen for as long as one experienced licensee could recollect - from twenty to thirty years.., and it is known to the man in the street. <i>Ibid.</i> 115 The resultant "Wompo" was then served to the public. *(1946,AJHR,H-38/113)*  
> "Now, I shall tell you of the bad olddays of wampo," said Mr. Manderson. Mr M. told us that Wampowas beer slops from overflowing glasses.In the old days some publicans kept theseslops in a bucket, then strained themthrough a barcloth back into a keg...  Theplastic beer-hose sounded the death-knellof potential wampo. *(Jun 18, 1957,NZ Truth,24)*

### wampo-pusher
  
noun.  
> **McLAUCHLAN** It gave the wampo-pushers what-ho. *(1987,History of NZ Humour,87)* [NOTES: Quote from SHEEHAN, 1957]  
> Still, it gave the wampo-pushers what-ho. *(Jun 18, 1957,NZ Truth,24)*

### wampy
  
adjective. queer, mad  
[NOTES: 11 Jan 1975 Kendrick Smithyman p.c. He must have been wampy (or wompo)[i.e. mad].]  
> **BEATTIE** Old 'Arawata Bill,' like so many men who spend many years in solitude, went quite 'wampy' in the end. *(1950,Far-Famed Fiordland,85)*

### wana
  
noun. [Maori] a New Zealand-bred cultivar of cocksfoot [orchard grass Dactylis glomerata].  
> Dairy Exporter Sept 66 Some examples [of adaptive cultivars] are matua prairie grass, nui perennial ryegrass .. kara and wana cocksfoot. *(1985)*  
> J of Ag Sept 16 Banks Peninsular [sic] farms divide into main areas, the sunny and the shady faces whose needs are totally different. Shady faces: Pawera clover 2 kg, Tahora white clover 3 kg, Nui or Ellet 8 kg, Wana cocksfoot 5 kg. *(1985)*  
> Dairy Exporter Jan 11 He began his renovation programme by undersowing 25% of pastures with a mix of nui and moata ryegrass, wana cocksfoot and pitau white clover. *(1989)*  
> Dairy Exporter Feb 20 Pasture species are Grasslands wana cocksfoot and Maru phalaris. *(1993)*  
> Dairy Exporter June 72 When pastures are resown, Jamie uses a mix of wana cocksfoot for its prostrate and spreading habit ... *(1995)*

### wanaki
  
noun.  
See: whanake

### wananga
  
noun. [Maori] a university or other tertiary institution (short for whare wananga)  
> Wananga are the obvious solution, offering education in a Maori kaupapa. At present there are three wananga - Raukawa, Awanuirangi and Aotearoa - and between them they account for about 24 per cent of all Maori tertiary students. *(Oct 2, 2002,NZ Education Review,10)*  
> Wananga is another exciting move because wananga reach a population that other tertiary institutions don't reach. *(Jul 14, 2000,NZ Education Review,12)*

### wananga
  
noun. [Maori] seminar or workshop  
> The update hui will be followed by a further wananga to discuss the claimant definition for Ngati Pahauwera. This continues the discussion from the wananga on 20 March 2008 ... *(Jul 26, 2008,Dominion Post,C16)*  
> Ngati Pahauwera whanau are invited to a treaty negotiations update hui and the third claimant definition wananga to consider the revised drafting of the claimant definition following the last wananga. *(Aug 23, 2008,Dominion Post,C10)*  
> She was inspired by a group of young Maori people attending a wananga (seminar) at the Takapuahia marae, Porirua, in January.... `Along with that they were learning how a marae functions. The wananga was strongly orientated to a strong self-image, self-confidence and to drawing upon their own leadership qualities,' Mrs Solomon said. *(Feb 7, 1983,Evening Post,24)*  
> On the fifth and final wananga which was on the 12th - 14th May we took the final journey to her papakainga at Matahiwi Marae... *(Oct 2006,Pu Kaea,4)*  
> Papa Joe regularly gives wanangas, or workshops, which are open to anyone who is interested in the philosophy and practical elements of Rongoa Maori. *(May 10, 2005,Dominion Post,TV week news. 4)*

### wanderer
  
noun. a monarch butterfly  
> <i>wanderer ... NZ & Austral</i>. monarch butterfly. *(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,868)* [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

### wandering albatross
  
noun. [Environment] . exulans, a huge seabird with white body and varying amounts of black/brown on upper parts; of two races:  the so-called snowy albatross, <i>D. e. chionoptera</i>, larger and paler than<i> D. e. exulans</i>; the wandering albatross. Both breed in the NZ subantarctic region.  
> The Buller collection of butterflies and several cases of butterflies from the islands adjacent to New Guinea are now on view at the Museum. The new case of birds, which is now only wanting labels, comprises, amongst other things, an adult Royal albatross, two stages of the young in down of the wandering albatross, also a specimen of the shy albatross, and two or three others of the rarer kind. *(Jul 1, 1911,Evening Post,9)*  
> Mr Reischek, the distinguished and persevering naturalist, then read a paper on "The Habits and Homes of the Wandering Albatross." He had on view some splendid and instructive specimens of this majestic bird, captured by himself at the Auckland Islands. *(Aug 4, 1888,Hawke's Bay Herald,2)*  
> Gibson's wandering albatross lost 63 per cent of its population between 1973 aand 1997, he says. *(Nov 26, 2001,Evening Post,5)*  
> **BULLER** DIOMEDEA EXULANS. (WANDERING ALBATROSS.) ... Native name.- Toroa. *(1873,Birds of New Zealand,289)* [NOTES: 1888 2nd edn 2/189]  
> 18. a. <i>Diomedia exulans exulans</i> Linnaeus, 1758  Wandering Albatross (Toroa) *(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,18)* [NOTES: 1980 edition]  
> **FALLA et al.** WANDERING ALBATROSS <i>Diomedia exulans</i> Maori name: Toroa. *(1966,Birds of New Zealand,30)*  
> Wandering Albatross <i>Diomedia exulans</i> LINNAEUS, 1758 ... OTHER NAMES: Wanderer, toroa. *(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,58)*

### wandering Jew
  
noun. OED: Wandering Jew (after 1c), Sailor(s, Jenny, Willie, popular names of certain plants  
> Before the garden became smothered in wandering Jew Hugh.. would plant potatoes beside Adela's productive vegetable plots. *(Jun 1999,North & South,32)*  
> Wherever there were houses and farms there were weeds: blackberry, wattle, privet, wild ginger, <i>Montbretia</i>, wandering Jew. *(Nov 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 60:51)*  
> <i>wandering Jew n.</i> creeping plant with bright blue flower. *(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),628)*  
> <i>wandering Jew n. </i>creeping plant with bright blue flower. *(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),21)*

### wandering Jew
  
noun. <i>Tradescantia</i>  
> <i> wandering Jew [s] Tradescantia fluminensis</i> Vell. *(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,83)*  
> **TAYLOR** WANDERING JEW (<i>Tradescantia fluminensis</i>) ... introduced from and fields in Canterbury *(1981,Weeds of Roadsides,165)*

### wandering Willie
  
noun. periwinkle; <i>Tradescantia fluminensis</i>  
> They can rip out your jasmine and spray your wandering willie as soon as look at you. *(May 3, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16)*  
> Wandering willie (Tradescantia), running rampant through Wellington city surrounds, is a graphic example of how an exotic plant can quickly come to dominate the landscape. *(Oct 14, 2003,Dominion Post,B5)*  
> Wandering Willie, Wandering Dew, Wandering Jew, White Spiderwort, Tradescantia fluminensis - by any name, this plant is a menace. *(Aug 24, 2002,Dominion Post,F17)*  
> **HIULGENDORF** PERIWINKLE (<i>Vinca major</i>), also called Wandering Willie, is plentifully found as a garden escape in many localities. It has endless trailing and twining stems sprawling over hedges and banks, glossy green South America, is common in some places in the North Island. ... *(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,135,1967)*  
> <i>wandering Willie [=] periwinkle: wandering Jew [s] </i> *(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,83)*  
> Weeds threaten many of these habitats.. they include familiar nasties like old man's beard, wild ginger and wandering willy. *(Oct 16, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7)*

### wanga
  
noun. penis

### Wangaloan
  
adjective. [Geology] .  
[NOTES: Geol. classification]  
> With the trans-Antarctic seaway, the Wangaloan fauna could easily have slipped into Otago from the other side of Antarctica (fig. 1). *(Mar 1994,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,103/15)*  
> Daniel, I.L., 1961:  Wangaloan strata in the Brighton-Abbotsford area. *(Oct 1966,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,21/10)* [NOTES: Title of paper]  
> Full Day Excursions:  F.3.  Boulder Hill, Wangaloa.  Wangaloan Stage.  (Of special interest to Tertiary stratigraphers and paleontologists). *(May 1969,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,27/2)*

### Wangaloan
  
noun. [Geology]  
> During the strategic lectures at the University of Canterbry during 1966-1967, I reluctantly digressed into the Wanagaloan, never really knowing what to do with it. But now my perspective has changed. *(Mar 1994,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,103/15)*

### Wanganella weather
  
noun. A long spell of calm fine weather, as when the Wanganella was stranded at the entrance to Wellington Harbour in 1947.  
See: cockatoo's weather  
[NOTES: ca1948 R. Mason `Heard in Wellington: a spell of unexpectedly fine, calm weather, but with uncertainty of when it is going to break.']  
> Wellington, having had the experience of "Wanganella weather," may soon be persuaded that there is also such a thing as "Ranfurly Shield weather". *(Aug 29, 1953,Evening Post,10)*  
> `It never rains but it pours' might seem to be the most unsuitable of adages as this long spell of `Wanganella weather' continues, ... A new phrase entered the Wellington vocabulary in early 1947 - `Wanganella weather'. ... The transtasman passenger liner Wanganella ... was wrecked on Barrett's Reef at the entrance to Wellington Harbour on January 19, 1947 and was not freed from the reef until February 6. For these 19 days the sun shone and there was hardly a breath of wind and the ship was saved ... *(Mar 18, 1978,Evening Post,19)*  
> Wanganella weather was something my grandmother spoke about, in honour of the ship of that name which in 1947 sat for weeks on Barrett Reef in Welington Harbour, unable to be refloated due to the uncharacteristically still conditions. *(Jun 11, 2002,Evening Post,6)*  
> **TOOGOOD** For the eighteen days that she was to remain on the reef, the weather was still and perfect. Wellingtonians came to call such a long, fine, calm spell 'Wanganella weather'. *(1979,Out of the Bag,82)*

### Wanganui
  
noun. a biscuit  
> Believe it or not, this mouth-watering delicacy is called a Wanganui and it's a popular item at Dorothy's Patisserie in downtown Wellington... But before you rush off to the Race Relations Office, hear Rood out. He has been selling Wanganuis for years, long before the place became a byword for urban renewal, and the recipe dates back to the 1930s when someone from Wanganui worked in the shop for a while... For the record, a Wanganui costs $1 and consists of butter cream wedged between biscuits darkened by cocoa powder. What next - chocolate kenmairs? *(Apr 22, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),9)*

### Wanganuian
  
adjective. Geol. classification  
> Since the early years of the Geological Survey, when Hutton advocated a Wanganuian age for the "Great Glacier Period" and when Hector classed some Wanganuian beds as Pleistocene, it has been customary to draw the Pliocene - Pleistocene boundary between the top of the Castelcliffian Stage (Wanganui Series) and the overlying Hawera Series. . . . *(Feb 1981,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,51/21)*

### Wanganuite
  
noun. a person born or living in Wanganui  
> All loyal Wanganuites think no small beans of the local fire brigade, and they have the same opinion of themselves. *(Aug 4, 1906,NZ Truth,59/3/6)*  
> The Wanganuites, however, are not backward in skite. *(Jul 7, 1907,NZ Truth,107/3/4)*

noun. [informal] Wanganui

> While the movie was the biggest thing to hit Wangers since the 1880s Land Wars on which the story is based, there was still restraint. *(Jan 28, 2006,Dominion Post,B4)*

verb. clear off  
> <i>wank off</i>: beat it *(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use)* [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

noun.

### wanky
  
adjective. [informal] pretentious  
[NOTES: in ODE: contemptible, worthless, or stupid]

> Van Been peppers her conversation with "I don't mean this to sound wanky", a sometimes needed disclaimer in the conceptual arts world. *(Jul 11, 2001,Capital Times,10)*

### wao
  
noun.  
See: whao

### waoriki
  
noun.  
See: waoriki

### waoriki
  
noun.  
[NOTES: aka whauriki, roariki]  
> According to Mr. I. Hopkins, Government Apiarist, ... the responsibility [for poisoning wild honey] rests with the yellow flowers of a cress-like plant called `whauriki,' which grows in swamps. *(Apr 6, 1905,NZ Medical Journal,IV/13/22)*  
> Would waoriki (<i>R. rivularis</i>) or any other species of Ranunculus produce the symptoms described? What treatment, if any, do you advise? *(Jan 15, 1913,NZ Journal of Agriculture,559)*  
>  *(Nov 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,461-463)*  
> **LYON** A peculiar fatality in wild honey was a poisonous quality in the honey itself. ... The natives blame a small yellow flower growing on the banks of streams in the King Country, which they call whariki, but whether this is so I cannot say. *(1952,Faring South,105)*  
> <i>Ranunculus rivularis. (Waoriki.) </i>The expressed juice, which has blistering qualities, is used for rheumatic ... joint-diseases. *(1905,TrNZI,37/113)*  
> <i>waoriki [s] Ranunculus rivularis</i> Banks et Sol. ex DC. *(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,83)*  
> **TAYLOR** WAORIKI (<i>Ranunculus rivularis</i>) ... This native is our most poisonous buttercup. It grows in both Islands but its occurrence is localised. *(1981,Weeds of Lawns,25)*  
> **BROOKER et al.** <i>Ranunculus rivularis</i> Banks & Sol. ex DC. Maori names: <i>Waoriki, raoriki </i> *(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,83)*  
> **RICHARDS** <i>R[anunculus] rivularis </i>... Water Buttercup. RAORIKI *(1952,Chatham Islands,29)*  
> There is a weed called Waoriki which grows in the swamps at Maungatawa so the natives say, it is discribed [sic] as being a white clover with a yellow blossom like that of the furze plant. *(Jul 5, 1883,Bay of Plenty Times,3)*  
> It was considered that poison was obtained by the bees from the blossom of the whauriki, which grows in the swamps, and it was recommended that the honey be analysed for the satisfaction of the jury and the public, and that the Government insert in the "Gazette" a notice to the Maoris relative to the danger of eating honey taken at this season of the year. *(Jan 2, 1890,Auckland  Star,8)*  
> "The waoriki is chiefly a swamp plant, the wharangi rangiora and wharangi-piro are familiar shrubs or small trees." *(Jul 17, 1934,Evening Post,8)*

### wap
  
noun. abbrev. of wapiti  
[NOTES: (hunters')]  
> **THOMSON** `What about a go at wapiti shooting? ... The high `tops' are practically free from snow, and the `waps' will be out above bush level seeking the young tussock growth.' ... at any minute we might sight a `wap'. *(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,121 [and passim])*

### wapiti
  
noun. also called (HNZD2) elk a large North American deer (<i>Cervis</i> species) introduced to NZ, in NZ always <i>Cervus canadensis</i>: North American usage seems to prefer <i>elk</i>; the term <i>American Elk </i>is occas. used in NZ

### wapiti block
  
noun. Fiordland area in which wapiti shooting licenses are available.  
> The Stuart Mountains are also part of what is referred to as the "wapiti block", an area containing the finest trophy a New Zealand hunter can gain. *(Jan 10, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15)*

### War Babies
  
noun.  
[NOTES: WWI]  
> **ROSS** The men from the Antipodes have brought with them a humour and a slang of their own. Their definition of the various batches of volunteers is worth quoting. The first contingent became known as `The Tourists' ... . The came `The Dinkums' ... then ... `The Super-Dinkums', `The War Babies' and `The Hard Thinkers', the latter having thought a great deal before they came. *(1916,Light and Shade)*

### war belt
  
noun.

[186] His loins were girt with a broad war belt in which he carried his pattoo, and his spear was in his hand. *(1819,Letters & Journals,185,1932)* [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

noun.

> "WAR CAKE" WILL KEEP FOR MONTHS. *(Jul 1, 1940,Evening Post,12)*

### war cloak
  
noun.  
[NOTES: war-cloak]  
> **TREGEAR** The old Maori dogs, nevermore to be seen by mortal eyes (they have all perished, but their hides are on the war-cloaks), were the true uncrossed descendants of the old cattle-dogs which guarded the herds of Arya on the Asiatic plains. *(1885,Aryan Maori,94)*  
> Every black warrior was painted with red ochre from head to foot, in various rings and bands, and clad in his war cloak. *(Jun 8, 1913,Otago Witness,17)*  
> War cloaks of dog skin were used by chiefs, and sometimes other kinds of cloaks. *(Nov 1, 1901,NZ Illustrated Magazine,116)*  
> And brave Rangi, hearing her cry, picked up his sword of flashing gold, and flung over his shoulders his crimson War-cloak. *(Mar 5, 1932,Evening Post,16)*

### war crock
  
noun.

### war dance
  
noun.  
[NOTES: war-dance]  
> **HYDE** .. the lost war-dance thudded through the North. *(1937,Selected Poems,37,1984)*  
> **Ihimaera** ...and should the ship get into trouble with icebergs, I'm counting on you to clear the way by doing a Maori war dance. *(2007,Ask the Posts,283)*  
> An unfortunate selection appears to have been made of the company's chief agent, who made some valuable acquisitions of territory, held sacred to the uses of the company by the natives to this day, and now incorporated, after the lapse of fourteen years, with the other possessions of the present company, yet he suddenly abandoned the enterprize [sic] on mistaking a war dance performed in honour of him for a hostile demonstration. *(Aug 21, 1839,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,4)*  
> The bonfire once ablaze there was a long pause before what was expected to be the grand event of the night, the much-talked-of war dance by the Ngatiporous. *(Apr 3, 1887,Poverty Bay Herald,3)*  
> War Dance, Competition, Tribe v. Tribe.   Prizes, aGBP15, aGBP5. *(Dec 10, 1898,Observer (Auckland),16)*  
> WAR DANCE WITH TE WHATEWHAS (BATTLEAXES). *(Jun 21, 1901,Auckland  Star,3)*

> It must be acknowledged that a war dance, properly performed, is not a sight any father of a family would care about his daughter witnessing. *(Mar 8, 1873,Auckland Weekly News,14)*

### war flag
  
noun.  
[NOTES: war-flag]  
> The Maories observed [the advance of the Royal Artillery] at first with apparent indifference, and continued to fly the flag of truce in the pah. ... until about eleven o'clock when the white flag was lowered from the Maori staff, and replaced <i>by a blood-red war flag. </i> *(Apr 6, 1861,Otago Witness,6)*  
> .. and could he awaken in the minds of his countrymen similar apprehensions, and imbue them with his own chivalric spirit, he would not be long ere he again unfurled his war flag, and hurled defiance at the English Government. *(Aug 15, 1846,New Zealander, The,2)*

> We do not suppose that the settlers of the West Coast of this island will rest more uneasily in their beds on account of the supposed war flag being unfurled. *(Jan 19, 1892,Poverty Bay Herald,2)*

### war in the north
  
noun.  
[NOTES: usually ref to the Heke, or flagstaff, war]  
> We have a police magistrate and three constables here, though there is open war in the north between the races, and in the south the greatest danger of it. *(Jun 14, 1845,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,59)*  
> The assistance rendered by "the British Resident and the Missionaries," on all occasions, long prior to the formation of the Colony, and their support given to Her Majesty's Government on the arrival of Governor Hobson, and during the late war in the North, has been too thoroughly ingrafted on the history of New Zealand to be shaken. *(Feb 7, 1854,Daily Southern Cross,3)*  
> THE WAR IN THE NORTH, by a Pakeha-Maori. *(May 9, 1870,Daily Southern Cross,2)*  
> He was through the Heke war in the north in 1845. *(Jul 24, 1895,Evening Post,5)*  
> A reference to the records shows that the engagement at Ruapekapeka was the last of the Maori war in the north. *(Apr 5, 1910,Evening Post,3)*

noun. the NZ Wars

### war pa
  
noun.  
> ... a certain native of the Kingite party ... Ibid.[W. King of Umuroa, the first or second chief of the tribe] is said to be highly incensed, and to have already commenced a war pah. *(Aug 23, 1862,Otago Witness,5)*

### warahipi
  
noun. [Maori] the art of rolling fleecewool between one's hand and thigh, into strands for knitting.  
See: also uruahipi  
> NICHOLSON The Loving Stitch 219 The technique, often called uruahipi or warahipi, where strands of wool fibre were drawn out of fleeces, rolled on the thigh and knitted had long been practised by Maori women fleece-os working in shearing sheds. Uruahipi was dying out but the Wool Week promotion revived the skill and introduced it to the general public as 'kiwicraft.' *(1998,BLANK ENTRY)*

### waratah
  
noun. [Rural] a metal fence-batten.  
> J of Ag July 15. 29 The fence erected constitutes Waratah standard, one every chain with three plain wires, No. 1 gauge. *(1940)*  
> **GORDON** The new ones [fences] all have seven wires, held up by Y-shaped standards known in the trade as waratahs. Where there's going to be stock pressure, they put in wooden posts instead of waratahs. *(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,41)*  
> Daily Telegraph Nov 10. 21 Clearing sale...waratah standards; 43 6in Waratah stds, 20 5in Waratah stds *(1993)*

### Waratah
  
noun. a timber-felling machine  
> On an exposed hillside, a lone Waratah is cutting down the last pine trees for the season. <i>Ibid.</i> 83 School groups regularly visit the forest and watch machinery such as the impressive Waratah tree harvester. <i>Ibid.</i> 84 But the sight of the Waratah munching its way through a line of trees seemed to send the harvesting analogy out the window. *(Sep 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 53/79)*

### waratah
  
noun. [Aboriginal; trademark] a slim iron fencepost whose cross-section comprises three ribs each at 120 degrees to the other (also used as a route marker on high ground).  
> The Waratahs - they took their name froma fence post. *(Jun 4, 1993,TV1)* [NOTES: Holmes Show]  
> He was watching me hammering in somesteel waratah posts for a temporaryfence. *(Jul 11, 1993,Sunday Times (NZ),24)*  
> "I designed a waratah driver, once," hesaid... I was puffing a bit as I hammered thewaratahs through the clay. *(Jul 11, 1993,Sunday Times (NZ),41)*  
> The Waiau Pass itself, its steel warratah route markings drooping as if wilted by heat but in fact bent by winter avalanche, is a summer tramping route only, but in the right season does not seem too hard. *(Mar 17, 2002,Sunday Star Times,C5)*  
> At the cliff edge, three waratahs - metal stakes - are embedded into the ground. Our ropes will be tied to these... *(Oct 25, 2003,Dominion Post,E12)*  
> **PICKERING** Out on the tops the old remedy for indicating a route was a pile of stones collected together - the cairn. Anyone can build them, and so once again a more official marking system had to be found. Routes became, as the maps put it, `poled', which [26] meant that metal standards were studded along the tops, These can also be called snow-poles or sometimes waratahs. *(1988,Hills, The,24)*  
> **GLEN** There were concrete posts and heavy gauge wire and netting of various sizes strung on to waratahs - for the life of me I could not see what this distinctly Australian name had to do with a 2-metre hunk of mild steel. *(1980,Bush in Our Yard,129)*  
>  *(1979,Heinemann NZ Dictionary,1242)*  
> **MARSHALL** ... I trailed the back netting in behind them and put in waretahs to hold it firm. *(1984,Day Hemingway Died, The,17)* [NOTES: (Takahe Man)]  
> **GIFKINS** There, anchored by waratahs at each corner, was displayed one of his wife's old sheets. *(1987,Summer in the Cazte d'Azur,6)*  
> **HUGHES** An ignorant air force ground crewman.. decided to fill [the space] with star pickets. These were short steel waratahs with one end shortened to a point. *(1999,Shooting from the Lip,90)*  
> Undulating tops travel leads to Atkinson, marked by a short waratah. *(2008,NZ Geographic,94/30)*  
> Pound in waratahs or similar metal stakes, securely fastening logs to them by weaving wire under and over ... *(Jul 29, 2006,Dominion Post Indulgence,18)*

noun. a fencing implement used for driving metal standards into the earth.

> **BULLER** CHRYSOCOCCYX LUCIDUS. (SHINING CUCKOO.) ... <i>Native names.</i>auroa, Pipiauroa, and Pipiwarauroa. <i>Ibid.</i> 108 ... [The Grey Warbler] is the willing victim of our two migrating Cuckoos, the Warauroa and Koheperoa ... *(1873,Birds of New Zealand,77,1888 2nd edn 1/132)*

### warbles
  
noun.

### war-canoe
  
noun.  
[NOTES: war canoe]  
> **CRAIK** The war-canoes of the New Zealanders ... have their heads ... elaborately carved. *(1830,New Zealanders, The,131)*  
> The war canoe race, in which fully seven crews are expected to take part, will be rowed-off at 4 o'clock in the afternoon, and directly after, while excitement in the event still prevails, all the competitors will land at North Shore, march or be conveyed by vehicles to the Takapuna Jockey Club Racecourse, and there perform a real war dance. *(Oct 25, 1882,Auckland  Star,2)*  
> Wakatua (War Canoes) Race - Crews and Canoes in War Dress. *(Dec 10, 1898,Observer (Auckland),16)*  
> 8   For the first time for many years the large Maori war canoes have taken no part in this year's Ngaruawahia regatta. *(Mar 23, 1917,Hawera & Normanby Star,8)*  
> The might of the Maori of old - his fierce hakas of welcome;  the sweep of the paddles of his lean war canoes - will resound again on the Waikato River next Friday after a lapse of decades when traditional ceremony will mark the opening of the new house for King Koroki at Ngaruawahia, states the "New Zealand Herald" of today. *(Mar 14, 1938,Evening Post,7)*  
> It would be interesting to know how many persons travelling along Worcester street, Christchurch, have observed that the prow of a Maori war canoe, fashioned in concrete, surmounts the State Fire Insurance building. *(Nov 23, 1945,Ellesmere Guardian,4)*

### warchie
  
noun.  
> **MIDDLETON** There was a flutter of feathers ... and a Grey Warbler flew out. ... `Look!' I said. `I've found a warchie's nest.' *(1959,Stone, The,27)* [NOTES: (The Stone)]

### war-club
  
noun.  
[NOTES: war club]  
> **CRAIK** While thus employed, and with numbers of the islanders mixed with them, in one moment each was fallen upon by ... barbarians, who ... instantly overpowered ... resistance ... beating out their brains with ... their short stone war-clubs. *(1830,New Zealanders, The,47)*  
> **WAKEFIELD** The natives procure ... quantities of a green jade, or serpentine, much prized by them for [314] the purpose of making war-clubs ... It is called by them `Ponamu', whence the name once assigned to the Middle Island -  `<i>Te Wahi Ponamu</i>' or, `The Place of Green Stone'. *(1848,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),313)*  
> Though already quite sufficiently married, the obese chieftain the other day took home a new wife to his harem, whereat the "boss" female lifted a war-club and flattened the old fellow's bump of amativeness with it. *(Jan 18, 1884,Hawera & Normanby Star,2)*  
> In ploughing less than an acre a settler found 60 curios, and among them a mere or war club. *(Jul 14, 1892,Otago Witness,43)*  
> The articles include war clubs and shields, fish hooks, canoe paddles, images, women's dresses and trinkets, arrows, feathers, and fibre. *(Sep 21, 1914,Feilding Star,2)*  
> One decoration that was much admired was a species of trophy in which the emblems of literature, agriculture, and the mechanical arts, decked with laurel and flowers, were represented, surmounting alike the sword and the war club of our two mingling races. *(Oct 2, 1852,Wellington Independent,3)*  
> The chiefs raised high aloft their costly war-clubs (meremere),* made of greenstone;  and whosoever of the Maoris had a stick with him flourished it with wild enthusiasm, while the remainder held up the ends of their blankets ... *Literally, "fire of the gods." *(Oct 8, 1863,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,3)*  
> I was in the house, and saw Kereopa in the pulpit with a war club in his hand. *(Dec 20, 1871,Taranaki Herald,2)*

### Warden
  
noun. = Goldfields Warden  
> In one day they obtained 2oz. of rough gold with a shovel and tin-dish, and immediately reported the circumstance to the Warden at Dunstan. *(1887,Handbook NZ Mines,8)* [NOTES: 1863 V. Pyke's Report in 1887 Handbook NZ Mines 8]  
> **PYKE** The affairs of the gold-fields are administered by judicial officers, entitled `Wardens', who are specially appointed for that purpose. *(1868,Province of Otago in NZ, The,41)*  
> **HAY** Presently the reading [of the proclamation under the Mining Act] is done, the Warden lifts his cap ..., announcing that the field is open. *(1822,Brighter Britain,2/288)*  
> Here it seems desirable to place on record the names of the chief officers who were first appointed to the charge of the Otago Goldfields. In doing so I must premise that for some unexplained reason the title of `commissioner' was at first assigned to these gentlemen in place of that of `warden' as directed by the Goldfields Act. The proper and correct designation was however afterwards bestowed. *(1887,History of the Early Gold Discoveries  in Otago,62)* [NOTES: 1887 Pyke History of the Early Gold Discoveries in Otago (1962) 62]  
> <i>warden n.</i> Government officer in charge of a gold field. *(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),628)*  
> <i>warden ... n.</i> Government officer in charge of a gold field. *(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),21)*

### Warden
  
noun.  
> **AYSON** He had to find rent of ground to give him the right to run cattle on `the hundred,' and had to pay two and sixpence assessment a head to the Wardens to be spent on road-making. *(1937,Thomas,94)*

### Warden
  
noun. = Maori Warden q.v.  
See: -

### Warden's Court
  
noun.  
[NOTES: mining]  
> It shall be lawful for the Governor, by Order in Council, from time to time to constitute for any Gold Field, or for any part thereof, Wardens' Courts for the administration of Justice therein, and to appoint Wardens as Judges of such Courts, with power to act alone or with Assessors or Juries, and in such manner, and to exercise all or any of the powers hereinafter mentioned as the Governor shall think fit to direct.... *(1858,NZ Statutes)* [NOTES: 1858 Gold Fields Act in N.Z. Statutes. 1858 21 and 22 Vict. No.74 442-446 in 1971 Speeches and Documents 41 [42????] XV.]  
> **MUELLER** I could not well refuse, understanding that some of the diggers were stopped from working until the case was settled at the Warden's Court ... *(Jul 1, 1866,My Dear Bannie,185,1958)*  
> The Warden's Court has jurisdiction to determine all suits and complaints cognisable by Courts of civil or criminal jurisdiction. *(1902,Brett's Colonists Guide,1167)*  
> Price was told off to open the Warden's Court. *(1914,Diggers' Story, The,121)*  
> LAWRENCE. - Resident Magistrate's Court, every Monday and Friday, at eleven o'clock a.m.;  Warden's Court, every Monday, at 2 o'clock p.m. *(Apr 1, 1876,Tuapeka Times,2)*  
> WARDEN'S COURT, THAMES .. THE following applications were granted:  J. E. Banks, Brogan Licensed Holding, Whangamata;  W. C. Savage, Britannia, Waihi;  J. G. Ralph, Wairarapa, Waitekauri. *(Oct 27, 1888,Te Aroha News,2)*  
> He also practised in the Warden's Court as a mining agent and represented Thames in the House of Representatives from 1911 to 1928. *(Aug 31, 1944,Evening Post,6)*  
> Land in mining districts, especially Otago, was often held not under orthodox tenures but as business site, residence site and special licences which were subject to the jurisdiction of the Wardens Court. *(Jan 1999,NZWords,2)*  
> **BEARDSLEY** 'Ickey. Oi 'ave 'ere a bluey for you to appear in the Warden's Court at                        Greymouth on the fourteenth inst. *(1984,Blackball 08,131)*

### warehou
  
noun. various (blue, silver, white warehou) saltwater food-fishes, esp. <i>Seriolella brama (Neptonemus brama)</i> (see blue warehou); also a sea fish, TREVALLY =SEA BREAM; spg also occas. <i>wharehou </i>  
[NOTES: aka wharehou, whario, wareho]  
> **SHAW** Besides cod, there are seals, trumpeter, moki, barracouta, flounders, skate and warihou, of some of which the most fastidious gastronomist would only be too happy to make their acquaintance for the first time. *(1858,Gallop to the Antipodes, A,135)*  
> **WAKEFIELD** The <i>Wareho</i> is a fish somewhat resembling the <i>kawai</i>, but of much better flavour. This is an excellent fish when cured and smoked. *(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,161)*  
> **TAYLOR** Fam. <i> Gadioideae </i> contains the <i> Warehou </i> and the <i> Hapuku </i>, two of the best of the New Zealand fish. *(1855,Te Ika a Maui,624)*  
> **HURSTHOUSE** ... the best [sea fish] are the Hapuka ..., the Moki, the Wharehou[ao-acute], the Patiki (a small plaice), the Kawai (a coarse common fish rising at the fly in tidal waters and sometimes dubbed salmon) a sort of mackerel, and some mullet, rock-cod, bastard-skate, and snapper. *(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/122)*  
> **HECTOR** WAREHOU ... The Sea Bream (<i> Neptonemus Brama </i>) is a fish deservedly prized by the natives. *(1872,Edible Fishes,112)*  
> Rurima is famous for its fish; hapuka (<i> Oligorus gigas </i>), ... moki (<i> Latris ciliaris </i>), king-fish (Seriola lalandii), <i> wharehou (Neptomenus brama)</i>, barracoota (<i> Thyrsites atun </i>), mackerel (<i> Scomber australasicus </i>), and the delicious little maomao ... *(1873,TrNZI,5/153)*  
> **SHERRIN** In Auckland it is sometimes called the yellow-tail, but this name appears to be also used for the king-fish. The fish known as trevalli in the Dunedin market is a different fish, allied to the warehou. <i>Ibid.</i> 104 WAREHOU (Neptonemus Brama). ... Of the warehou, Dr. Hector says: `The sea-bream is a fish deservedly prized by the Natives. In Wellington it appears in the market in October, ...' *(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,99)*  
> 7.<i>Portland Island.</i> - Tarakihi, moki, frost-fish, barracouta, king-fish, john-dory, warehou, maomao, parrot-fish, flying-fish, smooth-hound, soldier fish. *(1892,TrNZI,24/203)* [NOTES: (Thomson)]  
> Of the edible [pelagic] fishes ... such as the frostfish, barracouta, horse-mackerel, kingfish, dory, warehou, mackerel, and garfish; while only the ling, hake and haddock ... are among those of more southern types ... *(1892,NZ Official Handbook,167)*  
> **MORRIS** <i>Warehou, n. </i>Maori name for the fish <i> Neptonemus brama </i>, Gunth., called <i> Snotgall-Trevally </i> in Tasmania, and called also <i> Sea-Bream. See Trevally.</i> *(1898,Austral English,499)*  
> **DRUMMOND & HUTTON** The warehou, tarakihi, and gurnet are common in the north, and the moki, butterfish, and frost fish in the south, while the kahawai, hapuku (the groper of Dunedin), blue cod, barracouta, the little sea mullet, sometimes called the herring, and the flat fish are found all round the coast. *(1902,Nature in NZ)*  
> **TREGEAR** Snapper, <i> warehou </i>, butterfish, trumpeter, moki, flat-fish (patiki), and other fine sea fish were plentiful. *(1904,Maori Race, The,107)*  
> <i> Seriolella brama </i>, Gunther. (?) Warehou. *(1906,TrNZI,38/550)* [NOTES: (Thomson, Otago)]  
> <i> Seriolella Brama </i> ... Warehou. *(1911,Records of the Canterbury Museum,1/227)*  
> **SENIOR** Amongst the sea-fishes fit for food there are ... a number of periodical fishes ... and these include frost-fish. barracuda, horse-mackerel, king-fish, dory, warehou, mackerel, and garfish. *(1880,Travel & Trout,288)*  
> <i> Seriolella brama </i> Gunther. The warehou is commonly sold in Dunedin as `trevally', but the latter name belongs to <i> Caranx platessa </i> Cuv. & Val. *(1913,TrNZI,45/230)* [NOTES: (Thomson, Otago)]  
> Warehou. <i> Seriolella brama ...</i> *(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/270)*  
> SERIOLELLA BRAMA (Gunther[u-dieresis]). <i> Warehou </i>. Commonly sold as `trevally' in Christchurch and Dunedin. Rare at Bay of Plenty and Auckland. ... Distribution: A southern species; rare in the extreme north. *(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,IV/120)* [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]  
> At the Portobello Marine Fish - hatchery the swimming form has been taken from the stomachs of the following fishes: Smooth-hound (<i> Mustelus antarcticus </i>), Spined dogfish (<i> Squalus fernandinus </i>), Warehou (<i> Seriolella brama </i>), Red cod (<i> Physiculus bacchus </i>), Hapuka (<i> Polyprion prognathus </i>), Tarakihi (<i> Dactylosparus macropterus </i>), Maori chief (<i> Nolhothenia macrocephala </i>), Kelp-fish (<i> Coridodax pullus </i>), Blue cod (<i> Parapercis colias </i>), Spotty (<i> Pseudolabrus celidotus </i>), parrot-fish (<i> Pseudolabrus miles </i>). ling (<i> Genypterus blacodes </i>), and Leather jacket (<i> Pseudomonacanthus scaber </i>). *(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/111)*  
> At the Portobello Marine Fish - hatchery the swimming form has been taken from the stomachs of the following fishes: Smooth-hound (<i> Mustelus antarcticus </i>), Spined dogfish (<i> Squalus fernandinus </i>), Warehou (<i> Seriolella brama </i>), Red cod (<i> Physiculus bacchus </i>), Hapuka (<i> Polyprion prognathus </i>), Tarakihi (<i> Dactylosparus macropterus </i>), Maori chief (<i> Nolhothenia macrocephala </i>), Kelp-fish (<i> Coridodax pullus </i>), Blue cod (<i> Parapercis colias </i>), Spotty (<i> Pseudolabrus celidotus </i>), parrot-fish (<i> Pseudolabrus miles </i>). ling (<i> Genypterus blacodes </i>), and Leather jacket (<i> Pseudomonacanthus scaber </i>). *(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/111)* [NOTES: (Thomson and Thomson)]  
> ... and fishing-rods for warehou </i>Ibid.</i> 623  The warehou is a salt-water fish found plentifully about Cook Strait, and along the east coast into part of the Ngati-Porou waters. *(1926,TrNZI,58/599)* [NOTES: Buck]  
> **PHILLIPPS** <i>Seriolella brama </i>(Guenther). WAREHOU *(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,31,1971)*  
> **SPEIGHT** One of our best food fishes is the Warehou, which, although rare from Lyttelton to Timaru, seems to be abundant at certain periods off Kaikoura. *(1927,Natural History of Canterbury,199)* [NOTES: 1927 Archey `Native Fishes of Canterbury' in Speight et al. Natural History of Canterbury 199]  
> **BEST** When hooked, the warehou has to be taken up with a korapa, or landing-net,... <i>Ibid.</i> 43 The following fish were caught with hook and line: araara, maomao, moki, mango, ngoiro, pakirikiri, kahawai, tamure, tarakihi, and warehou. The kanae, kehe, kopipiro, moho, nanu and other species were taken by net. *(1929,Fishing Methods,41)*  
> <i> Seriolella brama </i>: Warehou (called trevally in Otago). *(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,72)*  
> <i> Seriolella brama </i> (Guenther). <i> Warehou </i>. *(1938,TrRSNZ,68/409)* [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]  
> <i>warehou </i>, a sea-fish (Neptonemus brama), vulg. the "h" is not sounded; a good market fish. *(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/160)* [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]  
> <i> warehou </i>: edible fish. *(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,176)*  
> **LYON** Many school fish, such as the wharehou and the moki had their set seasons ... *(1952,Faring South,77)*  
> **GRAHAM** Warehou <i> Seriolella brama </i>(Gunther) ... Warehou <i> Seriolella ampla </i> Griffin *(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,405)* [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 405]  
> **GRAHAM** WAREHOU <i> Seriolella brama </i> (Gunther) In Otago, commercial and sport fishermen, auctioneers, retailers, consumers of fish and readers generally have for many years been giving the name Trevally (<i> Usacaranx lutescens </i>) to small Warehou up to twelve inches in length. It is to be hoped that this misnomer will continue to be replaced by the proper name of Warehou. *(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,218)*  
> **PARROT** 3 THE WAREHOU <i> Seriolella brama </i> (Gunther) ... Maori name: WAREHOU. </i>Ibid.</i> 65 The Warehou is a southern fish becoming less plentiful in the North Island. It is an excellent food fish, and is always in good demand. It is also found in Australia where it is known as the `Whario' a corruption of the Maori name Wharehou, and in Tasmania it is sold as `Snogtail' Trevally. <i>Ibid.</i> 170 Warehou <i>Seriolella brama</i> Trevally; Sea Bream *(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,6)*  
> warehou The Maori name for an edible New Zealand sea fish *(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),439)*  
> It should be noted that the name sometimes varies according to district. Thus in Otago small-sized warehou (fish) are called trevally, and in the South Island generally the European name groper is preferred to that of the Maori hapuku. (Ed.) ...Warehou .. .. warehou .. .. <i> Seriolella brama </i> *(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,705)*  
> **MORELAND** WAREHOU [<i> Seriolella brama </i>] ... In southern waters the name trevally is sometimes used. *(1967,Marine Fishes,44)*  
> **DOOGUE & MORELAND** WAREHOU ... <i> Other names: Seriolella brama </i>; trevally *(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,222)*  
> **MATUSCH** Sub-order STROMATEOIDAE move This group includes ... the warehou ... of Wellington, the South Island and Bay of Plenty waters, and the silverfish ... of groper grounds throughout; the latter two are species of<i> Seriolella. </i> *(1967,Animals of NZ, The,217)*  
> **SORENSON** WAREHOU (a) Scientific name:<i> Seriolella brama </i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Sea Bream; Bream. (c) Other common names: None known, except that in southern waters this species is sometimes called Trevally. (d) Principal localities where taken: Cook Strait; Nelson; Greymouth. ... The Spotted Warehou, <i> Serolella punctata </i>, taken in very small quantities, is usually counted in with the warehou catch. *(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,50)*  
> Warehou marked `snapper' was selling in some WELLINGTON SHOPS FOR $10 TO $15 A KILOGRAM. *(Nov 28, 1985,Dominion,1)*  
> <i> warehou n.</i> NZ blue-green and silver sea-bream. [M] *(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,869)* [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]  
> [species name] common warehou .. [scientific name] <i> Seriolella brama </i>.. [other name(s)] warehou, blue warehou, [occas. BREAM] *(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters)*  
> [index to common names 283-293] warehou 2180-203 *(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography)*  
> Wareho (the whole fish), 1s;  barracouta (very large), 9d to 1s;  schnapper, five for 1s, at the City Buffet. *(Jul 8, 1891,Taranaki Herald,2)*

### Warepan
  
adjective. Geol. classification

### warepuni
  
noun. variant of wharepuni  
> **LEE & KENDA;LL** W|are p|une; A close house, or bed-room. *(1820,Grammar and Vocabulary of Language of NZ,225)*  
> **TAYLOR** I walked along the beach to a little kainga and entered a warepuni which may be likened to a kind of oven for sleeping in ... *(Jul 2, 1846,Journal,IV/68)*  
> **ANGAS** The night being exceedingly cold, I slept in the <i>ware pune,</i> or `close house:' after a fire had been lighted inside, and the little den heated to at least 90 [degree sign] of Farenheit, I entered it and lay down upon my blanket; ... .<i>Ibid.</i> 2/21 [check spg: ?misprint] Just outside the pah stands a `were puni,' or `hot-house,' for strangers to sleep in. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/133 We stopped to dine on potatoes at a small new settlement, where the natives were building a couple of <i>ware punis</i>, or `warm houses,' and several plantations appeared in the vicinity. *(1847,Savage Life,2/123)*

### war-mat
  
noun.  
[NOTES: aka war mat]  
> **NICHOLAS** Having brought off with him from the North Cape a handsome war-mat, *(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/101)*  
> **SAVAGE** he [Hongi] presented it to me the moment he got on board, ... <i>Ibid.</i> 53 The war-mat [so tightly woven, that when hanging loose a spear will not penetrate it] is generally ornamented with a border, which frequently displays a great degree of taste: the Vandyke is a common pattern. *(1807,New Zealand (various authors/titles),27)* [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]  
> **MARSDEN** Shunghee said, `I have seen King George, as you may see by my war mat, or coat of mail, my sword, and helmet.' ... The men threw off their common mats, put on their war mats, and prepared for action. *(1823,Letters & Journals,357,1932)*  
> **ANGAS** Amongst them were several fine old chiefs, most elaborately tattoed, and adorned with the <i>topuni</i>, or war-mat; which is made of dog's hair, assorted, and interwoven with a garment of fine flax, so as to resemble a cloak of rich fur. *(1847,Savage Life,1/236)*  
> **COWAN** In olden days the Kapenga Plain was celebrated for its special quality of <i>harakeke</i> (flax), much used in making strong, tough <i>ihupuni</i>, or war-mats, which were worn as a kind of armour in hand-to-hand battles. *(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/399,1983)*  
> The different chiefs were all standing up with their war mats gracefully thrown over their shoulders, their hair neatly tied in a bunch upon the crown of the head, and ornamented according to the general fashion of their country, with the white feathers of the gannet. *(Jul 24, 1855,Daily Southern Cross,1)*  
> "With their war-mats on."   The natives formerly made a thick matted sort of cloth, impervious to spear-thrusts, which they used as armour.   In more modern times this defence was only used to save the breast from injury in climbing trees. *(Sep 15, 1864,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,3)*  
> Utu then gathered some herbs, and put them on Houmea's lips, and repeating an incantation, the children came forth out of her mouth - Tutawake with an ornamental staff (taiaha) in his hand and Nini with a spear in his, and both clothed in a pukupuku (a war mat). *(Feb 28, 1880,Poverty Bay Herald,2)*  
> That morning, when the air seemed full of rumours of battle and death, Tutange was girded with the sacred war-mat, the maro-taua. *(Dec 24, 1909,Star, The,4)*  
> The war mat was made of flax fibre twisted into strong thread, and was stouter than any canvas. *(Feb 8, 1894,Otago Witness,40)*

### war-mat
  
noun. often =to[o]puni

### warp merino
  
noun. a high-grade Merino fleecewool.  
> HENDERSON Wool & Wool Classing 115 There is almost a premium for long-stapled, sound Merino wool of high grade - called 'Warp Merino' - and the making of a line or lines of this is a feature of Merino classing plans when a good clip is being handled. *(1965)*  
> WOOLPRO Glossary of Wool Terms 40 warp - a traditional term for merino wool of good length and colour, absolutely sound and regular, with a clear well defined staple and used for the production of warp yarns for weaving. *(2001)*

### war-party
  
noun.  
[NOTES: aka war party]  
> The other division of the war-party had gone by the plains ... without meeting anyone. *(1883,TrNZI,15/440)*  
> **LAMBERT** ... a strong <i>taua</i> (war-party) was being organized ... *(1936,Pioneering Reminiscences of Old Wairoa,149)*  
> This, it should be remembered, was after the return of the war party from their raid to Kawhia, when Waka Nene and Patuone with their following left for Hokianga, laden with spoil, and accompanied by numerous slaves, leaving Te Rauparaha to carry out his future plans of conquest and obtain fresh allies. *(Jan 13, 1874,Colonist (Nelson),3)*  
> Hauhau scouts and small war-parties daily sallied out from the fort seeking game in the shape of stray pakehas. *(Oct 30, 1906,Star, The,4)*

### warpath
  
noun.  
[NOTES: war path, war-path]  
> **BULLER** At the time ... this place could be only reached by a canoe journey ... from Foxton, or by a rude bush-track - one of the Maori war-paths of former times. *(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/162)*  
> **COWAN** The Rangers' field equipment was simple. On the war-path in the Wairoa and Hunua bush their bed was a bundle of fern, and the forest was their tent. *(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/268)*  
> This bush was traversed in all directions by old war paths, affording great facilities for concentrating bodies of men secretly on any particular spot. *(Jun 8, 1858,Daily Southern Cross,3)*  
> The Ngatiruanui tribe are coming up to William King by way of the old war path through the bush. *(Jun 23, 1860,Otago Witness,5)*

### warrant of fitness
  
noun.

> **RICHARDS** Road code, drivers' licences and warrants of fitness were unheard of in those days. *(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,80)*  
> **RICHARDS** Road code, drivers' licences and warrants of fitness were unheard of in those days. *(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,80)*  
> <i>warrant of fitness</i> NZ certificate of roadworthiness legally required for a motor vehicle. *(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,870)* [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. W.O.F. 1356; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 541]]

### warrior gene
  
noun. the monoamine oxidase (MAO) gene which is purported to explain a supposed genetic tendency towards aggression in Polynesian males.  
> Meanwhile, more Polynesians have the warrior gene than white people. *(Aug 13, 2006,Dominion Post Indulgence,C9)*  
> The gene, dubbed the warrior gene, has been associated with risk-taking, including gambling, addiction and aggression. *(Aug 10, 2006,NZ Herald,A1)*  
> The authoritative <i> New Zealand Medical Journal </i> published papers from the Otago School of Medical Sciences and Christchurch School of Medicine and Health Sciences, exposing the 2006 warrior gene research and its shallow sample of just 17 males as a sensationalised disservice to science and Maori. *(Jul 15, 2007,Sunday Star Times,A9)*

### wart cress
  
noun. hogweed; twin cress  
> **HILGENDORF** TWIN CRESS (<i>Coronopus didymus</i>, formerly <i>Senebiera didyma</i>) is often called wart cress, but, as this name is given to at least two other species, I suggest dropping the name entirely and substituting twin cress as a good descriptive term; ... This weed is also called swine cress, hog cress, and scurvy grass [and land cress]. <i>Ibid.</i> 103 WART CRESS (<i>Coronopus squamatus</i> syn. <i>C. procumbens</i>). A more robust plant than twin cress with large wrinkled and sessile pods. ' *(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,93,1967)*  
> <i>wart cress [s] Coronopus squamatus</i> (Forsk.) Asch. *(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,83)*  
> **TAYLOR** WART CRESS (<i>Coronopus squamatus</i>) ... European plant, long established in New Zealand along with twin cress, but much less common leaves and beautiful pale blue silver-shaped [check???] flowers an inch or more across. *(1980,Weeds of Crops,107)*

### wash
  
verb.  
[NOTES: goldmining]  
> ...a hole was dug ... and a quantity of the soil washed, but without any visible satisfactory result ... [A] favourable indication was noticed at a bar in the creek, and a quantity of the gravel and earth removed, a panful was taken from about two feet below the surface and washed, it produced a number of scales of gold. *(Nov 9, 1852,NZ Government Gazette,V/26/165)* [NOTES: (Report of Sub-Committee on Recent Gold Discovery in the Coromandel District)]  
> **EARP** I went there [to Arataonga] yesterday `prospecting', and found gold in the first pan washed. *(1853,New Zealand (various authors/titles),255)* [NOTES: 1852 Nov 10 (Auckland) in 1853 Earp NZ 255]  
> **HEINZ** Your committee had a quantity of stuff taken out of this hole and washed under their inspection, and every panful produced a few specks of gold. *(1974,Bright Fine Gold,108)* [NOTES: 1853 Earp NZ in 1974 Heinz Bright Fine Gold 108]  
> The only tools we took with us were, a spade, tin dish, and pannikin. We tried a number of places along the bed of the creek, sinking in each from two to four feet; out of the whole we washed not more than six buckets full, ... *(Jun 29, 1861,Otago Witness,5)*  
> **PASCOE** ... looking towards the bank of the stream I observed some splendid looking washing stuff - fine gravel mixed with quartz and ironstone. I took the lid of the billy and washed about two handsful, and found some fine specks of gold - what diggers call `the colour'. *(1960,Over the Whitcombe Pass,42)* [NOTES: 1863 Lauper in 1960 Pascoe (ed) Over the Whitcombe Pass 42]  
> **PASCOE** ... looking towards the bank of the stream I observed some splendid looking washing stuff - fine gravel mixed with quartz and ironstone. I took the lid of the billy and washed about two handsful, and found some fine specks of gold - what diggers call `the colour'. *(1960,Over the Whitcombe Pass,42)* [NOTES: 1863 Lauper in 1960 Pascoe (ed) Over the Whitcombe Pass 42]  
> **HEINZ** Went out to Dome Gully and had only washed three pans of dirt ... Tried to borrow a cradle *(1974,Bright Fine Gold,95)* [NOTES: 1863 May 22 Diary of an Unsuccessful Digger in 1931 Aug 27 Star (Christchurch) in 1974 Heinz Bright Fine Gold 95]  
> A `new chum' on the digging was busily working in a claim, and washing a dish of stuff, while the banker and an old digger were quietly observing him. After painfully panning out, he threw the dish down with disgust, and exclaimed ... `Not even the color!' [check spg???] *(May 6, 1864,Press, The)* [NOTES: 1864 May 6 Press (Christchurch) in 1966 Turner English Language 17]  
> **MONEY** I was one day washing a dish of stuff in the creek below the claim, ... *(1871,Knocking About in NZ,79)*  
> **SWAINSON** Persons desirous of constructing Tram Roads, or forming Reservoirs or Dams on the gold field, for the purpose of washing gold, must make application to the Commissioner for permission so to do: ... *(1853,Auckland,162)* [NOTES: 1852 Coromandel Gold Field. Provisional Regulations in 1853 Swainson Auckland 162]  
> On the resumed diggings, the parties ... washed [7 1/4oz of dust] ... the quantity set forth, and are continuing their work with every prospect of finding eventually the matrix Gold, which the nuggets now obtained indicate to be in the vicinity of the workings. *(Jan 12, 1853,NZ Government Gazette,6/1/2)* [NOTES: (Heaphy's Report from Coromandel)]  
> How employed/ Down to bar at junction of path and stream, ... setting `Tom', &c. Digging and washing./ Remarks/ The earth passed through the `Tom' was top stuff, incidental to opening a new bar. *(Dec 10, 1852,NZ Government Gazette,V/30/187)* [NOTES: (Heaphy's Tabular Journal) Wednesday, Nov. 3./]  
> **BUTLER** We did not wash any of the gravel, for we had no tin dish, neither did we know how to wash. *(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,iv/56)* [NOTES: 1860 Mar 24 in 1863 Butler First Year iv/56]  
> **HAAST** I gave orders again to wash for gold, and every dishful of dirt yielded some specks. *(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,18)*  
> **HEINZ** Sunk paddock ... <i>Saturday 24th</i>. Washed hard from very early to very late and brought home the blacksand. *(1974,Bright Fine Gold,96)* [NOTES: 1863 June 23 Diary of an Unsuccessful Digger in 1931 Aug 27 Star (Christchurch) in 1974 Heinz Bright Fine Gold 96]  
> The prospects were no secret, as Chinamen and others in the district were allowed to wash dishes, and in every dish washed gold was got. *(Dec 19, 1900,Grey River Argus,4)*  
> .. there is a fascination in the idea of literally digging or washing gold from the earth which naturally captivates even sedate and calculating minds, and is strengthened by every new account of the success of lucky individuals. *(Mar 10, 1852,New Zealander, The,3)*  
> So far operations have been chiefly confined to stripping, but fair prospects have been met with, gold being seen freely in some of the stone when washed. *(Dec 14, 1882,Thames Star,3)*

### wash
  
verb.

### wash
  
verb. to launder money  
> Sir Michael Fay, a partner in a firm that has profited enormously from economic restructuring and associated privatisations, gave Mr Thompson the money with the request that the donor's identity be kept secret - hence the "washing" of the money with all its unsavoury suggestions of Watergate-style slush funds. *(May 20, 2002,Evening Post,8)*  
> National's former president, Geoff Thompson, says he "washed" the donation through his law firm's trust account to keep Fay Richwhite's identity secret. *(May 19, 2002,Sunday Star Times,A6)*

### wash
  
noun.  
[NOTES: goldmining]  
> **ACLAND** <i>Wash</i>.- Place and plant for washing sheep. Until the 1870s washing sheep before shearing was a fairly common practice. If the sheep were hot- water washed, their owner had to build an expensive apparatus. The remains of these <i>sheep washes</i> were not uncommon twenty years ago. If there were technical terms for this process they are not recorded here, except that the most important place was at the spout. Everybody used to get very wet. *(1951,Early Canterbury Runs,391)*  
> **PRESTON** `Much of the wool was scoured on the farm [in the early days], especially the merino. ... But scoured wool took a good deal of time - the bales were carted to the washpool, the wool washed, dried and turned in the open - wind and rain stopped work frequently'. `There was a `wash' and a drying paddock at both Longlands and Haldon; the pool was fed by a race which had to be cleaned out before each shearing and kept in repair ...' *(1978,Woolgatherers,43)*  
> **BARRINGTON** The creeks look very well for gold; splendid quartz boulders, and a fine wash, but could not get bottom anywhere, as it is too deep. Saw the bottom high up the creek, but very hard and no gold. *(Mar 29, 1864,Diary)* [NOTES: 1864 Mar 29 Barrington Diary in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 404]  
> The party bottomed on a bluish-grey wash. ibid. 358 (Hints to Prospectors, J.A.Miller) ... sand, gravel and stones ... together are called in mining parlance `wash'. *(1887,Handbook NZ Mines,130)*  
> **WARDON** He struck gold in payable wash ... The wash was rather thin. *(1892,MacPherson's Gully,14)*  
> . aided by ... an old alluvial miner - the locality was diligently prospected, and one or two `colours' are said to have been found in the wash in the bed of the Mangaone Stream ... *(1893,TrNZI,25/354 .)*  
> **MOFFAT** ... as the bar [in the river] commenced to dry in patches [as the river dropped], our hopes were high. The prospects were splendid. The wash was about six inches thick, lying on a false bottom, consisting of a thin layer of rusty cement, and under that thin white gravel [at Gabriel's Gully]. *(1896,Adventures by Sea and Land,58,1979)*  
> **KITTO** Great attention should be paid to the gold saving appliances, as no matter how much wash is treated, if you do not save the gold it will be impossible for the dredge to pay. There should be a save-all box as well. This box can be of wood or iron, fitted up with steel ripples, and should be placed in such a way as to catch all of the dirt that escapes the main shoot or drop plate. *(1900,Practical Dredgeman's Manual,11)*  
> **PFAFF** I worked a claim on the Scandinavian Hill ... This lead was about 600 yards long, and the wash varied from 1 foot to 4 feet. *(1914,Diggers' Story, The,79)*  
> The first miners used only the pick, the shove, the dish, and the cradle. But the wash that would yield a profit with these was soon exhausted. *(1931,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,12/159)*  
> <i> Cradle:</i> The apparatus, made of wood used for washing gold-bearing wash or dirt. *(1935,Gold Mining Year Book,11)*  
> ... the gravel beds are quite payable, but a difficulty is that bands of clay overlie the wash. *(1939,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,(NS) 39/110)* [NOTES: (Williamson, Naseby)]  
> **HISLOP** ... his first dishful did produce two very nice flat match-head samples [of gold]. `What is this heavy prospect doing so close to the road in that innocent looking wash?' I said. <i>Ibid.</i> 69 I was invited ... to stop the weekend [near Cromwell], and as Donald was going out ... to try the wash in a nearby burn, I thought it a great idea. <i>Ibid.</i> 69 ... all we got for our trouble with the tin dish were a few colours and black sand, which is always an indication that you are in gold-bearing wash. *(1943,Pure Gold,55)*  
> **MILLER** ... I would have found much less excitement in washing the equivalent of the gold in half-crowns buried in the gravel wash. *(1946,There Was Gold,141)*  
> **EVANS** Generally the wash was about fifteen feet down. *(1962,Waikaka Saga,77)*  
> **MAY** WASH-DIRT: the auriferous gravel, sand, clay or cement in which the greater proportion of gold is found. Synonyms were `wash', `pay-dirt', or just `dirt'. *(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,528,1962)*  
> <i>wash, washdirt n.</i> earth containing alluvial gold. *(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),21)*  
> Gold deposited usually sinks to a "wash, that is some conglomerate that will hold it. *(Nov 1937,NZ Railways Magazine,37)*

### wash
  
noun.  
[NOTES: sheep wash]  
> **G.M.WILSON** After walking about 4 or 5 miles, we came to McLeans [sic] wash and I went down to see how it was worked. ... They have only to divert a small stream and bring it down to the wash in a small race ... First, to take the rough off the sheep, they were swum through a creek in a basin built up on each side and deep, at the end they were thrown in at. ... A yard carried them on to the hot water soak ... and from there they are pushed down to the spouts, six in number. *(Feb 3, 1873,Diary)* [NOTES: 1873 Feb 3 G.M.Wilson Diary in 1973 Wierzbicka Wilson Family 166]

### wash
  
noun. a sheepwash or woolwash.  
See: wash dip, washpool  
> BALFOUR Diary Dec 12 Had a walk with Barnes and Peacock to Wash Gully but I think it will be no go to make a wash there. *(1875)*  
> BATHGATE Waitaruna 83 There are some other men coming to put up a new wash, or dip, or something, in a few days. *(1881)*  
> LOUGHNAN Glossary 89 wash: a sheep wash, but now long obsolete. *(1981)*  
> THORNTON Heritage of Farm Buildings 214 At The Levels station George Rhodes used a cold-water wash located at Washdyke, hence the origin of its name. At Pareora Edward Hassell constructed a warm-water wash that had the advantage of removing the grease from the wool rather than dirt only. *(1986)*

### wash
  
verb. to wash sheep prior to shearing [1800s] or to dip sheep in tobacco water and/or arsenic to free them from scab [1800s.]  
> PHARAZYN Diary Dec 15 After breakfast to river .. began washing and continued to nearly 7 in the evening. Washed nearly 300 when fern caught fire. *(1846)*  
> St Leonards Station Diary Jan 25 .. we were obliged to give up shearing .. the sheep who were washed yesterday not being dry enough. *(1856)*  
> RICHMOND Letter Oct 22 in Scholefield (1960) Richmond-Atkinson Papers I 319 After looking at the woolshed and contrivances for washing, during which Boyse and I nearly tilted ourselves off a marvellous platform contrived for wisking the sheep into a tank of water five feet deep. *(1857)*  
> The Levels Station Diary Dec 18 in Woodhouse (1937) George Rhodes of the Levels 130 Washed 1,100 wethers. *(1857)*  
> Puketoi Station Diary Nov 12 Washed wethers. *(1859)*  
> MONEY Knocking About in NZ 7 A mile or so distant was the pool where the sheep were washed ... *(1871)*  
> Straight Furrow Nov 6. 18 There are a number of creeks in this country named Washpool where early settlers washed their sheep each year. *(1963)*  
> RICKARD Strangers in the Wilderness 80 The custom of washing sheep is commemorated in at least two place names. *(1967)*  
> **BUTLER** [on colonial expressions] I was rather startled at hearing one gentleman ask another whether he meant to wash this year and receive the answer `No'. I soon discovered that a person's sheep are himself. If his sheep are clean, he is clean. He does not wash his sheep before shearing, but he washes; ... *(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,32)* [NOTES: 1860 Jan 27 in 1863 Butler First Year 32]  
> found that they were not ready and they will wash now in some Lagoon on their own side. *(Nov 29, 1861,Puketoi Station Diary)*  
> Mr Acland and I went to the wash pen in the morning. They have been washing since Monday and will be at it all next week. On Tuesday a large bird flew over the pen. All hands and the cook took after it ... a black swan was captured. *(Oct 20, 1864,Chudleigh Diary,149,1950)*  
> Gebbie washing sheep - very ill. *(Nov 23, 1850,Torlesse Papers,177,1958)*  
> McGill bullock driver ... All hands washing Humes ewes. *(Nov 29, 1859,Puketoi Station Diary)*  
> McMaster washed sheep *(Jan 27, 1862,Puketoi Station Diary)*  
> The usual swimming in creeks, generally washes the ground of the wool clean, but has little effect on the back and top of the staple. *(Oct 16, 1869,Otago Daily Times,3)*  
> His wool is a clean, greasy staple, without sand or dust, and having a plentiful supply of clean water, he washes the dry sheep and hoggets. *(Dec 15, 1877,Otago Witness,18)*  
> Mr John Scriven protests in a letter to the Bradford Chamber of Commerce against the present mode of washing sheep. *(Aug 3, 1888,Clutha Leader,3)*  
> Where there is a small flock, a suitable place in which to wash, and clean paddocks for the sheep, he would recommend washing. *(Dec 15, 1877,Otago Witness,18)*

noun.
