### wash dip
  
noun. a series of pools in a manageable stream, used for washing or scouring sheep or wool.  
See: woolwash; washpool; sheepwash  
> MURISON Puketoi Station Diary Jan 30 Mr Oliver, Wilson and Girvan to the wash dip with coal. *(1869)*  
> ibid. Feb 1 Wilson to wash dip with scrub. *(1869)*  
> WILSON & FRASER Book of the Pioneers 29 A wash dip consisted of a long elevated soaking pen, probably 50 ft long, divided into four sections by stop-gates. *(1936)*

### wash down
  
verb.  
[NOTES: gold]  
> ... washing down these hills *(1862,Otago: Its Goldfields and Resources,20)*  
> A fatal mining accident occurred at the Whitecombe, near the Waikaia, on Friday evening last, to Auguste Laloli, who was in the act of washing down, when a portion of the claim slipped down and badly crushed him. *(May 17, 1876,Tuapeka Times,2)*  
> The Rees United Company washed down the boxes at their elevators, and obtained 126oz 15adwt. *(Apr 28, 1885,Otago Daily Times,2)*  
> Mr Thomas Callender, secretary of the Matakitaki Gold Dredging Company (Limited), reports having received the following telegram:- "Washed down on Wednesday, 8oz 13dwt of gold. *(Apr 3, 1893,Otago Daily Times,3)*  
> The Local Industry washed down for 23aoz on Saturday. *(Mar 22, 1905,Tuapeka Times,3)*

### wash gold
  
noun.  
> **HAAST** The wash gold is fine, scaly, and very much rolled, so that there is no doubt it has come some distance in the great river-bed. <i>Ibid.</i> 265 An assay ... of stone ... from this reef, did not give the least trace of gold, and it is therefore evident that the wash-gold found in small quantities ... is derived from the interlaminated quartz; ... *(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,85)*  
> They got colour from the surface down perhaps a distance of five or six feet, but the greater part was roof gold, and men who know would like to see "wash" gold. *(Aug 7, 1861,Daily Southern Cross,4)*  
> Experts declared that these were not wash gold, and all were convinced they looked decidedly suspicious. *(Oct 14, 1887,Star, The)*  
> PARNELL and party, who are prospecting the south side of the river, struck wash gold to-day in the roof of the tunnel giving half-a-pennyweight to the dish. *(Aug 5, 1891,Auckland  Star,8)*  
> In many of the West Coast rivers, as the Waiho, Waikupukupu, Mapourika, Hokitika, Grey, Waimangaroa, Whakamarina, and many others, the true "wash" gold is so heavy that it is impossible to believe that it has been deposited in its present position by running water. *(May 23, 1900,Evening Post,2)*  
> **LAWSON** Brighton gold is all wash gold, brought down from the hills. *(1951,Gold in their Hearts,65)* [NOTES: [?1870s]]

### wash off
  
verb.  
[NOTES: (goldfields)]  
> **PYKE** When the Senior Partner first sighted them, one ... was washing off a pan of earth; whilst the others ... eagerly awaited the result. *(1873,Wild Will Enderby,1/xi/40,(1889,1974))*

### wash pen
  
noun.

### wash pen
  
noun. a sheep-pen attached to a pool where sheep are washed or dipped.  
[NOTES: aka washing pen, wash-pen]  
> Duppa St Leonard's Station Diary Jan 6 All hands employed about wash pen. *(1857)*  
> **PHILLIPS ROCKWOOD** John went with him to wash-pen in afternoon. <i>Ibid.</i> Nov 17 H.P. Seal Richards ... went eeling down to Wash-pen Creek ...  Dec 28 ibid 26 John Richards & Seal mending Washpen ... *(1855,Journal,20)*  
> Mr Acland and I went to the wash pen in the morning. They have been washing since Monday and will be at it all next week. On Tuesday a large bird flew over the pen. All hands and the cook took after it ... a black swan was captured. *(Oct 20, 1864,Chudleigh Diary,149,1950)*  
> There seems to be a growing disposition among flockowners to shear their flocks in the grease, under the impresssion [sic] that unless they are able to get up their wool in a really creditable manner, it is useless to wash their sheep at all, and without properly constructed washing pens, and suitable paddocks, conveniences which few of them as yet possess, it is better not to make the attempt. *(Nov 10, 1860,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,2)*  
> The improvements on the property have been extensive, are in good order, and adequate for working double the number of stock.   They consist mainly of Woolshed, Drafting Yards, Washing Pens, Lever Press, and four secure Paddocks. *(Jan 25, 1870,Hawke's Bay Herald,2)*  
> The improvements consist of an eight-roomed dwelling-house, garden, shepherd's house, out-station, two large paddocks, woolshed, wash-pen, &c., &c. *(Oct 18, 1854,Lyttelton Times,7)*  
> Six consecutive weeks of heavy rain - my lambs are all dead or dying, the wash pen is carried away, and all chance of building the new shed seems gone for this year. *(Nov 7, 1874,West Coast Times,3)*  
> Plans for bridges over Wairiri creek and Cordy's wash-pen creek were considered, and tenders were ordered to be called for their erection. *(Oct 9, 1880,Star, The,3)*

### wash up
  
verb. end up  
> If you've ever wondered where retired athletes wash up, watch the veritable elephant's grave yard that is <i>Sportzah</i>. *(Jul 30, 2003,Capital Times,9)*

### wash up
  
verb. .  
[NOTES: goldmining]  
> They expected to net thirty pounds weight labor, and would not be able to wash up more than half their washdirt. *(Feb 29, 1864,Southland Times,3)*  
> At Vinegar Hill diggings Learmonth's party washed up 500ozs for four months' work. *(Jul 18, 1882,Grey River Argus,2)*  
> The Roxburgh dredge washed up on Saturday for 13a?oz of gold. *(Sep 9, 1896,Tuapeka Times,3)*  
> The Antonios dredge broke the bottom tumbler, and has not washed up. *(May 20, 1908,Otago Daily Times,28)*

### wash up
  
noun.  
[NOTES: goldmining, cf washing up; aka wash-up]  
> All the sluicers are taking advantage of the plentiful supply of water they now have at command, and are preparing for a general wash-up before the winter fairly sets in. *(Jun 12, 1872,Otago Daily Times,2)*  
> There will be no wash-up from the Success Dredge to-night owing to a breakdown in the machinery. *(Sep 26, 1918,Wairarapa Daily Times,5)*

### wash-bag
  
noun. made out of chamois - ?wash leather from ?gold-wash was this used in NZ ??cits Grandad used it??

### washbox
  
noun. round or rectangular tank of metal, wood or concrete used for cold water rinse of wool.  
[NOTES: (woolscourers')]  
> **BREMNER** <i>Washbox</i> archaic term: *(1985,Woolscours of NZ,10)*  
> ... in some places the wash-dirt contains little or nothing. *(1861,NZ Goldfields,30,1976)* [NOTES: 1861 Oct 1 Dunedin in NZ Goldfields 1861 (1976) 30]  
> All the wash-dirt is auriferous ...; but this sluicing party calculate that half a pennyweight to the load will pay them a pound sterling each per day. <i>Ibid.</i> 34 ... a pick is seldom required till the washdirt is reached. *(1862,Otago: Its Goldfields and Resources,20)*  
> **SMALL** Go to almost any mound/ Of wash-dit on the ground,/ Specks of gold you'll surely see/ Shining out most brilliantly;/ *(1866,NZ & Australian Songster,13,1970)*

### washday at the pa
  
phrase.  
> It seems to be wash day at the pa for the Brits who appear to be airing some strange sleeping patterns in their bed linen over the last few weeks in Montana Sunday Theatre. *(Sep 25, 1992,Dominion,22)*

### washdirt
  
noun. [Geology]  
See: cf dirt, wash  
[NOTES: aka dirt, wash dirt, wash-dirt   [?is paydirt chiefly US]]  
> Fairly good dirt is being obtained, but no picked stone has come to hand during the past week. *(Sep 5, 1887,Thames Star,3)*  
> . . two miner's [sic] pans were filled with wash dirt and taken to the surface, where the gold was washed out. *(Feb 12, 1931,Auckland  Star,3)*  
> It will be remembered that when the first miners came to Thames they all assumed that gold would be found in nuggets, and they diligently washed the dirt from the different creeks. *(Oct 26, 1931,Auckland  Star,3)*  
> The stopes over the level are being carried on as usual, and the leader is continuing to look fairly well, and producing a good quantity of crushing dirt, through which gold is seen every breaking down; there have also been 10 or 12lbs of picked stone selected during the week. *(Oct 3, 1887,Thames Star,2)*  
> Dare and party had a return of 17oz 10dwt from three tons of dirt from the lower level; and Maher and party had a return of 7oz 4dwt. *(Nov 29, 1930,Auckland  Star,17)*  
> Further general dirt and 56lb of picked stone will be treated this week. *(Feb 1, 1941,Evening Post,9)*  
> Mr Ewing has the largest show of washdirt, some of it being from his Vinegar Hill mine and some from Kildare Hill. *(Jan 23, 1890,Otago Witness,19)*  
> **HEINZ** Average of washdirt six inches. *(1974,Bright Fine Gold,92)* [NOTES: 1863 May 17 Diary of an Unsuccessful Digger in 1931 Aug 27 Star (Christchurch) in 1974 Heinz Bright Fine Gold 94]  
> **HARPER** ... we came to several shafts sunk about eighty feet on a `lead' of gold lately discovered. Gold here [West Coast] is usually found in strata, about three feet thick of washdirt ... *(Feb 5, 1868,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,10,1914)*  
> **PYKE** ... to render themselves more secure from [flooding], they removed the `washdirt' to a spot above flood-mark, where they could afterwards sift the golden grains from the soil at leisure. <i>Ibid..</i>  3/vii/95  He was what is termed in mining phraseology, a `hatter', - meaning one who works by himself. ... Alone and unaided he scooped the wash-dirt into his cradle, which he vigorously rocked with his left hand, whilst with the right he ladled water from the river into the `hopper' or seive [sic]. ... He cast aside the `dipper', and hastened towards the eddy. *(1873,Wild Will Enderby,1/xi/39)*  
> **MORRIS** <i>Washdirt, n. </i> any alluvial deposit from which gold is obtained by washing; or `the auriferous gravel, sand,clay, or cement, in which the greatest proportion of gold is found.' (Brough Smyth's `Glossary,' 1869.) Often called dirt (q.v.). *(1898,Austral English,500)*  
> It was not until the end of 1864, however, that the discovery of rich `wash-dirt' in the Greenstone district ... called public attention to the golden possibilities of Westland. ...[3] When the wash-dirt was close to the surface it was bared [check] by pick and shovel. *(1906,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,(NS) 1/2,3)* [NOTES: (Bell and Fraser)]  
> <i>Wahs&dirt </i> (-d&etilde.rt), n. (Mining) Alluvial soil containing gold which may be extracted by washing. *(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2033)*  
> <i>wash-dirt n.</i> alluvial deposit that can be profitably worked for gold by washing. *(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,12)*  
> **HOWARD** Other seekers ... moved down ... to Codfish Bay, where payable gold was found in company with black sand occurring in wash-dirt three feet deep. *(1940,Rakiura,233)*  
> **HEINZ** <i>Washdirt</i>: Auriferous gravels or sands. *(1947,Bright Fine Gold,200)*  
> Workings [at Mount Ida, Otago, in 1871] were for the most part alluvial, the distinctive process used being `paddocking'. [fn] Paddocking was the systematic sluicing of large areas of flat ground from which the surface layers were first removed to reveal the `wash-dirt' beneath. *(Apr 1953,NZ Geographer,29)* [NOTES: Kibblewhite]  
> **MAY** WASH-DIRT: the auriferous gravel, sand, clay or cement in which the greater proportion of gold is found. Synonyms were `wash', `pay-dirt', or just `dirt'. . *(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,528,1962)*  
> A party sunk through the slate bottom some four or five feet, and found gravel and washdirt beneath. *(Oct 16, 1861,Lyttelton Times,5)*  
> I got on the washdirt between 5 and 6, and the dredge broke it up first rate. *(Jul 27, 1888,Otago Witness,8)*  
> The following telegram has been received from the acting-mine superintendent at Ross:- "35oz 8dwt of gold from 880 trucks washdirt." *(Jul 30, 1914,Evening Post,8)*  
> The washdirt in the bank is looking better and the bottom has dipped to 18 feet in one part of the cut. *(Aug 11, 1934,Evening Post,12)*  
> We have a cradle to put the washdirt in. *(Nov 11, 1897,Otago Witness,50)*  
> Mr White took them to the spot where, on a former occasion , he had washed gold out of the earth on the beach; and early on Wednesday morning they set to work, and succeeded in washing out eight or ten specks of gold from each dish of dirt. *(Mar 21, 1852,Daily Southern Cross,3)*  
> Another drive has been taken in about 60ft lower down and nearer the point; the lode is also visible here, and good prospects can be washed from loose dirt and gold can be seen in the solid body of the conglomerate. *(Jul 28, 1870,Grey River Argus,2)*  
> He writes to me as follows: "In the drives into the conglomerate of the quartz ranges, the average thickness of dirt washed, is about two feet from the base rock, and the gold produced from one cubic yard of such earth would be as nearly as I can calculate, worth from 25s. to 30s. *(Sep 30, 1859,Colonist (Nelson),2)*  
> The stopes in all parts of the mine are yielding good crushing dirt. *(May 31, 1878,NZ Herald,3)*  
> The gold-bearing wash dirt now is between two and three hundred feet in width, and we are dredging to a depth of 13 feet. *(Feb 17, 1896,Inangahua Times,4)*  
> This dish of dirt he found contained a nugget, weighing 18oz 10dwt. *(Sep 3, 1904,Evening Post,12)*  
> Tributers in the Kaponga mine, Coromandel, crushed 38 loads of dirt from the old tip, and obtained gold valued at aGBP70. *(Sep 27, 1904,Auckland  Star,2)*  
> A fair quantity of crushing dirt was obtained last week from the stope on the reef south, and strong colours of gold were seen in the ore. *(Sep 28, 1904,Auckland  Star)*  
> The best wash dirt is a seam of quartz rubble lying on a bed of stiff clay. *(Oct 8, 1904,Auckland  Star,7)*  
> Strong colours of gold are to be seen in the general dirt, which should be of a payable nature. *(Aug 2, 1921,Auckland  Star,7)*  
> It is evident that the run of gold discovered in No. 3 winze continues upward, and some very good crushing dirt will be obtained from this block. *(Jun 14, 1878,NZ Herald,2)*  
> The 51lb of specimen included in last crushing yielded 7oz 19dwb of gold, while the 10 tons of general dirt returned 26oz 17dwts. *(Feb 3, 1896,Auckland  Star,3)*

noun. a gold pan (cf pan, tin dish) --

> **COAD** In no time he had his wash dish ready and was sluicing for gold. *(1931,Such Is Life,53)*

### washdown
  
noun. a cleansing of dairy sheds and yards after milking and stock trucks after transporting of stock.  
> Dairy Exporter October 3 Cut yard washdown ... *(1986)*  
> Dairy Exporter March 11 Cut yard washdown time by up to 75%. *(1987)*  
> Dairy Exporter Feb 69 On one large property with a circular yards, the washdown process is quickened by having the yard hose wrapped around the backing gate. *(1993)*  
> Taranaki Effluent Disposal Strategy 11 All three major meat processors in the region have stock truck effluent disposal facilities (including washdown facilities.) Riverlands Eltham stock delivery and washdown sites are combined. *(2001)*  
> Daily News March 23. 26 Farm equipment required: backing gate, 10-11 mtrs, electric drive, washdown pump. *(2002)*  
> Daily News May 11. 47 Clearing sale .. sheep netting, washdown hoses. *(2002)*  
> Daily News June 15. 30 Freek ANKA nozzle worth $49 with any washdown pump. [washdown hose; washdown pump.] *(2002)*

### washdyke
  
noun. a washpool for washing sheep prior to shearing.  
> RHODES Letter April 4 in Woodhouse (1937) George Rhodes of the Levels 140  I purchased 24 acres near the Gorge of the Pareora and 28 acres at the Wash Dike. *(1859)*  
> RICKARD Strangers in the Wilderness 80 The custom of washing sheep is commemorated in at least two place names. The Washpool is the name of a farm at Maraekakaho in Hawke's Bay, while washdyke is where the Levels Station washed its sheep. *(1967)*  
> KERR From the Beginning 133 The Washdyke is a [sic] historic spot for there the first sheep in the county were washed by George Rhodes and his men ... *(1976)*  
> BREMNER Woolscours of NZ 69 To Yorkshireman George Rhodes, sheepfarmer of 'The Levels' in the 1850s, a creek was a 'dyke' and in the creek north of the future city of Timaru he washed his sheep before shearing, and the Washdyke it became. [wash dike] *(1985)*

### washer
  
noun. a person who washes sheep in a washpool or sheepwash (19C)  
See: sheep-washer  
> **SIMPSON** Afterwards went to sheep wash on Longridge Stream - there is a good stream of water for washing and battery and suitable hut for washers. *(Jan 19, 1880,Diary of Tours of Southland Estates)*

### washery
  
noun. laundry  
> He went to the Kent Terrace washery for his "duds". *(May 7, 1910,NZ Truth,5)*

### wash-house
  
noun.  
[NOTES: Jim Cameron writes: <i>Wash-house</i> is defined in NSOD as an outhouse or room where domestic washing is done. Collins tags it as <i>formerly</i> a building or outhouse. In NZ the wash-house was located in the backyard but later often off the back porch or otherwise attached to the house. It was often used as storeroom also (Cf. <i>Hot October</i> p.7.) Subsequently it came fully into the house and was rechristened laundry. But the term is still extant in the old sense, and not uncommon. ?NZ certainly much used until recently.]  
> My brother Sandy was wasting the water inthe wash-house. *(Sep 14, 1927,NZ Tablet,39)*  
> **BOOTH** I had been home before she arrived.. leaving my bloodstained jersey lying on the copper in the washhouse. *(1997,Deadline : My Story,24)*  
> I slipped out to the washhouse... You had to handpump the water there, and carry it to a copper in the wash-house where you heated it. *(Dec 24, 1947,NZ Observer,21)*  
> I sort of gaffer-taped a smile and didn't take it off except once I thought 'I'm going to cry' and I rushed into the wash-house and burst into tears. *(Feb 15, 2001,Evening Post,18)*  
> The wash-house, usually attached to the house, was separate, to prevent steam escaping into other areas. *(Jul 26, 2000,Evening Post,23)*  
> ... the townsfolk of Lyttelton should not allow their clothes to be washed by persons using the washhouse in the barracks. *(Mar 22, 1851,Lyttelton Times,6)*  
> **BARKER** Outside, ... a little wash-house stands near the kitchen door ... and a fowl-house and pig-stye, and a coal-shed. *(1866,Station Life in NZ,65,1870)*  
> Carpenter commenced Wash-house - *(Jul 10, 1866,Puketoi Station Diary)*  
> **BARKER** ... however, they dug a passage [through the snow] to the wash-house, and returned in triumph with about a pound of very rusty bacon they had found hanging up there; ... *(1867,Station Life in NZ,161,1951)*  
> **MANSFIELD** On one side the grass patch was bordered by the scullery and wash-house and over this whitewashed lean-to there grew a knotted vine. <i>Ibid.</i> 1922 460 The washhouse was a perfect place for such a meeting because they could make as much noise as they liked ... . It was a small tin shed standing apart from the bungalow. Against the wall there was a deep trough and in the corner a copper with a basket of clothes-pegs on top of it. *(1920,Stories,236,1984 Alpers)*  
> **FRAME** She's in the wash-house. *(1984,Some Other Country,100)* [NOTES: 1951 Frame `Swans' in 1984 Some Other Country 100]  
> **SARGESON**  *(1969,Joy of the Worm,37)*  
>  *(1969,Landfall,23/58)*  
> **LELAND** <i>washhouse</i>: A small outbuilding behind the main house where the LAUNDRY was traditionally done in a (see:) <i>copper. </i> *(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,110)*  
> **HENDERSON** But for all this, Mum burnt Ted, grimy and worn, under the copper in the wash-house, and something curled up and died. <i>Ibid.</i> 17 `Mary and I [newly arrived from Britain] heard ... Herbert Henderson come into the kitchen, then into the laundry (i.e. wash-house [author's comment]), into the dairy, out into the meatsafe.' *(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,13)*  
> But whoops, it's hard to shake off the washhouse, pros or not. *(Apr 2, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),5)*  
> **BATISTICH** [She] put on a sack apron she took from a kit and said briskly: 'Where the wash-house, Missus?' and set to work. *(1987,Sing Vila in the Mountain,63)*  
> **EDMONDS** There we sat, with a washing bowl of plums and peaches,.. salvaged from the wash house where there was nothing much to fall. *(1989,Hot October,7)*  
> **EDMONDS** He and I made toast on an old kerosene stove kept in the wash house, while Mum concocted the tomatoes-and-bacon or onions-in-cheese sauce, or whatever it was to go with it. *(1989,Hot October,66)*  
> **GEE** The wash-house was a lean-to shed at the back of the house. *(1994,Crime Story,91)*  
> **FRAME** Oiutside beside the huge magpie-filled macrocarpa tree there was a decayed wash-house with a copper in one corner for boiling clothes washed in the wooden wash-tubs. *(1984,Angel at My Table, An,51)*  
> **ALPERS (ed)** A strange company assembled in the Burnell's washhouse after tea. *(1984,Stories of Katherine Mansfield, The,460)* [NOTES: check page. Original quote 1921-1922]  
> **HASMAN-JUDD** The wash house they are to share with their landlady is several yards from the back door. It has a copper, two wooden tubs and a wringer. *(2000,Woman Who Loved Plants & Other Stories, The,30)*

### washing
  
noun.  
[NOTES: goldmining (usu. pl)]  
> **EARP** California ... diggers here say that the surface washings in those places are often not more rich than these; even on very good diggings. *(1853,New Zealand (various authors/titles),255)* [NOTES: 1852 Nov 10 (Auckland) in 1853 Earp NZ 255]  
> **PFAFF** There were over a thousand diggers ... waiting to see the result of the prospect when we bottomed. We ... struck very good gold ... We built a paddock, rigged up a windlass, and got several very good washings out of this shaft ... *(1914,Diggers' Story, The,56)*  
> **D'AVIGDOR** When the inspection of the washings or diggings was over, Barry ... at last hit upon a happy idea. <i>Ibid.</i> 169 When the men come into the nearest town from their washings with the gold-dust, they sell it at the bank, and receive a cheque in exchange. *(1888,Antipodean Notes,129)*  
> The Greymouth Argus states that the Mont d'Or washing for eight weeks amounted to 300oz. *(May 17, 1876,Tuapeka Times,2)*

### washing
  
noun.

### washing
  
noun. i.e. of sheep

### washing
  
noun. a cleansing of wool, usually on the sheep's back.  
> BISLEY Practical Treatise 51 Do you advise my shearing in the grease, or washing on the sheep's back? *(1897)*  
> Farmers' Union Advocate Feb 3. 12 The offerings [of wool] from many stations were considerably less than last year. This shortage was due to more being kept for washing. *(1906)*  
> Washing sheep in the afternoon. The washing is all over. They have washed some 14000 sheep and lambs this week. *(Nov 19, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,67,1950)*  
> **BURDON** Washing consisted of two separate operations, the first of which was called `soaking'. The soak was a sort of bath ... let into the ground beside a creek ... It was filled and kept full by water flowing in through a two-inch pipe and a supply of boiling water was kept handy ... Soap and sometimes ammonia were added to it. After the sheep were let out of the soak they were driven ... [to] the creek ... tipped in ... and washed clean by men standing in the water. *(1938,High Country,83)*  
> After looking at the woolshed and contrivances for washing, during which Boyse and I nearly tilted ourselves off a marvellous platform contrived for wisking [sic] the sheep into a tank of water five feet deep ... *(Oct 22, 1857,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/319)* [NOTES: (C.W. Richmond)]  
> Every farmer knows the expense and inconvenience of washing, not only the expense, but in the case of big, heavy fat sheep, the risk. *(Aug 3, 1888,Clutha Leader,3)*  
> It is plain, therefore, that Australia has advantages which operate favourably to the success of hot water washing on the sheep's back which Otago has now. *(Oct 16, 1869,Otago Daily Times,3)*  
> He said that where a man has good strong sheep, and every convenience for washing, he would recommend washing. *(Dec 15, 1877,Otago Witness,18)*  
> Washing V. Shearing in Grease. *(Jul 23, 1887,Southland Times,3)*

### washing
  
adjective.  
[NOTES: (goldfields)]  
> The gullies ... require [?requiring] only the cradle for washing operations. *(1862,Otago: Its Goldfields and Resources,19)*

### washing dirt
  
noun.  
> **SMALL** A thumping pile of washing dirt he happen'd just to see;/ He let a mighty howl then,/ And says he, `Upon my soul, then,/ I'm thinking that's the pile of stuff yez stole away from me.' *(1866,NZ & Australian Songster,21,1970)*

### washing down
  
noun. a hosing and scrubbing of cowshed and yards after milking.  
> GAMBRILL Flock House Farming 34 When the washing down is finished we go over and feed the pigs. *(1987)*

### Washing Machine Charlie
  
noun.  
[NOTES: WW2, RNZAF Islands]  
> **COOZE** The [Jap] engines were not synchronised and droned with an uneven beat, which led to the nickname of Washing Machine Charlie by which the Jap is known throughout the Islands. *(1944,Kiwis in the Pacific,34)*

### washing off
  
noun.  
[NOTES: gold industry: removal of dirt]  
> The most favorable anticipations had been formed regarding the probable yield, but the washing off, which was finished about the 1st inst., has exceeded the estimate of the most sanguine. *(Oct 19, 1886,Thames Star,4)*  
> In the extension of the western crosscut a reef formation, 12 inches thick, was passed through, which produces a prospect of course particles of gold by washing off. *(Aug 7, 1896,Auckland  Star,3)*

### washing pond
  
noun.  
> **HENRY WILLIAMS** We saw also the sheep dip, the washing pond &c., the manager's house &c. *(Jul 9, 1882,Diary)* [NOTES: 1882 July 9 Henry Williams Diary in 1952 Stevens John Grigg 64]  
> INTELLIGENCE has been received of the drowning of a little boy at Otahuhu last evening, who, in company with another boy, had gone to Mr Sutherland's wool washing pond at the head of the Tamaki to bathe. *(Nov 28, 1876,Auckland  Star,2)*

### washing stick
  
noun.  
[NOTES: cf. copper stick]  
> **LAWLOR** Back would go the clothes to the replenished and now boiling copper, a handful of washing soda and small pieces of soap would be tossed in, and the clothes prodded vigorously and often with the washing stick. *(1962,More Wellington Days,102)*

### washing-place
  
noun. a sheep-washing place = wash-pool  
[NOTES: (station hist) aka washing place]  
> ... a horse-paddock, ram-paddock, ... oats or wheat paddock, and a good, permanent washing-place. *(1857,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,90)*  
> .. and yet Mr Campbell having tried the experiment in an imperfect manner last year, was so satisfied with the result, and so confident that if he had had proper appliances he could have shorn white wool, that he has gone in for a first-rate permanent washing place. *(Aug 3, 1868,Otago Daily Times,3)*

### washing-stuff
  
noun.  
[NOTES: (goldminers')]  
> This morning a man ... showed me a prospect which he had obtained from several dishes of washing-stuff, in a claim on the Waitahuna. It consisted of about [17] half-a-dozen specks of gold - about half a grain; ... *(Sep 23, 1861,NZ Goldfields,16,1976)*  
> This morning a man ... showed me a prospect which he had obtained from several dishes of washing-stuff, in a claim on the Waitahuna. It consisted of about [17] half-a-dozen specks of gold - about half a grain; ... *(Sep 23, 1861,NZ Goldfields,16,1976)*  
> The ground sank through consisted only of the upper soil, a bed of clay, and a little coarse gravel, below which was the washing-stuff - a layer of rotten slate, interspersed with quartz rubble, and other <i>debris</i>. [e-acute] *(Sep 27, 1861,NZ Goldfields,28,1976)* [NOTES: Tuapeka]  
> **PASCOE** ... looking towards the bank of the stream I observed some splendid looking washing stuff - fine gravel mixed with quartz and ironstone. I took the lid of the billy and washed about two handsful, and found some fine specks of gold - what diggers call `the colour'. *(1960,Over the Whitcombe Pass,42)* [NOTES: 1863 Lauper in 1960 Pascoe (ed) Over the Whitcombe Pass 42]  
> <i>wash, washing stuff n. </i>alluvial soil from which gold is washed. *(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),628)*  
> The result of this sinking was not satisfactory, and contrary to most sinking in Australia, where the gold is not met with until a man gets down to what is called the washing stuff, generally only a few inches from the bottom. *(Jan 28, 1857,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,2)* [NOTES: written from Nelson, NZ]  
> Owing to the expensive mode of working adopted - entailing, as it does, the cost of the stripping of the over burden by pick and shovel, and the carriage of the washing stuff some distance to the sluice at the creek, the amount of gold saved (leaving the chance of striking other patches out of consideration) cannot, in my opinion, leave much profit. *(Mar 8, 1882,Southland Times,4)*

### washing-up
  
noun.  
[NOTES: (goldmining)aka washing up]  
> **PYKE** The miners, knowing the importance of `making hay while the sun shines['], strained every effort to obtain the auriferous `dirt,' and stacking it on their claims, leaving the washing up and `cradling' to be done when the getting more dirt was impossible. *(1887,History of the Early Gold Discoveries  in Otago,79,1962)*  
> **DOUGLAS** ... I may as well finish what has been a hard but still glorious existance [sic] in washing up & seeing if I have really found anything worth opening. *(Jul 5, 1890,Letter)* [NOTES: 1890 July 5 Douglas letter to G. Roberts in 1957 Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas 112]  
> I am confident that the public cannot stand the strain and a washing-up must take place soon. *(1908,NZ Truth,135/2/5)*  
> **BATHGATE** We [bank officers] had to go to Hyde and Sowburn when we heard there was to be a washing up in any of the claims. *(1925,Random Recollections,27)*  
> **HEINZ** It is a good idea to make the riffles in two sections, as this helps washing up, and there may be occasions when it is necessary to wash up the top set only. <i>Ibid.</i> 32 Washing up on these large claims was complicated and interesting to watch, particularly the final phases when one might see a billy of gold recovered. The length of the sluicing run depended on the richness of the ground, some claims washing up monthly others after several months. *(1952,Prospecting for Gold,28)*  
> **MAY** WASHING-UP: the whole operation of saving gold from the wash-dirt. `Cleaning up' had the same meaning. *(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,528,1962)*  
> One case I may mention, is that of O'Hara's party, the owners of the long water-race, who have just had a washing-up on Richmind [sic] Hill:  the result being, for five weeks work, eighty ounces, and having been part of that time employed in cutting a tail race and opening out the claim. *(Oct 16, 1863,Otago Daily Times,4)*  
> The Scandinavian brought their washing-up for the season to a finish last week, and by what is to be learned the returns have been very satsfactory [sic]. *(Jun 14, 1900,Otago Witness,37)*  
> Dr Neely to-day received a telegram from the managing engineer of Ross Goldfields, Ltd., stating that the result of the wishing-up for last week's work was 68oz. of gold from 1660 trucks. *(Jul 12, 1910,Wanganui Chronicle,5)*  
> **PYKE** A tributer may deduct such amount from the earnings of the mine after each washing-up or retorting. *(1892,Gold-Miner's Guide,10)*  
> **HEINZ (1923)** Some of the lighter particles of goldwere held by the oil and the goldbuyerinvariably had a "washing up" in ahandbasin as soon as his customers hadleft.[Greymouth Evening Star, 13 Dec. 1923] *(1974,Bright Fine Gold,166)*

### washing-up bill
  
noun. a Bill presented to the House of Representatives near the end of a session containing those odds and ends of matters and legislation that need to be passed, but often cannot be fitted into other Bills (e.g. alienation of pieces of Crown land, State Forest, etc.)  
> The Native "Washing-up" Bill introducedinto the House of Representativescontains several matters of generalinterest affecting the Native race. *(Oct 6, 1928,Evening Post,10)*  
> Then we see the abuse of the Washing-upBill for the incorporation withoutadequate consideration of manycontentious matters which normallyrequire separate Bills *(Oct 26, 1935,Christchurch Star-Sun,12)*  
> By the Native "Washing Up" Bill.. theprovision.. for granting areas of Crownlands to landless South Island natives..is revived. *(Nov 1, 1919,Otago Daily Times,8)*  
> The Washing-Up Bill, presented to Parliament this week, validates the purchase.. of certain lands as a site for public buildings. *(Oct 26, 1917,Pukekohe & Waiuku Advocate,2)*  
> **R. MASON** My father [the Hon. H.G.R. Mason] says `ever since I can remember' and thinks it probably goes back to the beginning of N.Z. parliament. [Article on the number of bookmakers there are in N.Z] *(Jun 7, 1952,Letter)*  
> But Ngata replied, as he did to numerous such invitations in later years, by saying that he could not leave New Zealand because of political commitments. He had to prepare legislation for the forthcoming session. `You wouldn't have the heart to entice me away from a prospect so alluring! Compare washing up bills with banana groves, Maori Councils with your lotus-eaters!' *(1986,Na To Hoa Aroha,1/27)* [NOTES: 1986 Sorrenson Na To Hoa Aroha (Ngata-Buck Correspondence) 1/27]  
> It was not a clause for a principal Act, but was better fitted for a "washing up" Bill. *(Oct 19, 1895,Poverty Bay Herald,2)*  
> Mr Seddon objected to consent being given, saying that the bill was no place for dealing with the reserve, and that while he did not actually object to the proposal regarding the Turu-turu pah being handed over to the Hawera Borough Council, he preferred to deal with the matter in a "washing-up" bill at the end of the session. *(Aug 21, 1900,Hawera & Normanby Star,4)*  
> In addition there would be the usual washing-up Bills - the Reserves and Other Lands Disposal, Local Legislation, and Statutes Amendment Bills. *(Oct 12, 1944,Evening Post,8)*  
> The "Washing-up" Bill introduced in theHouse of Representatives today is anunusually lengthy measure, containing 130clauses. *(Oct 6, 1915,Otago Daily Times,2)*

### washing-up grant
  
noun.  
> ] ... authorised `washing up' grant ... [B.D.A. Greig] *(1950,Tramper,18(8)/2)* [NOTES: [R. Mason]Check date - arbitrary one here]

### wash-out
  
noun.  
[NOTES: WWI]  
> **BOLITHO** When the `wash-out' signal sounded ....Was that the `wash-out' ... ? *(1918,Book of the C1 Camp, Tauherenikau,10)*

### Washpen Creek
  
noun. a river where sheep were washed before shearing [1800s].  
> Press Jan 25 Now with farming taking a back seat they have formed a delightful walking track through the rhyolite canyons and gorges of Washpen Creek. The walk out down below Washpen Creek to the barn is especially beautiful. *(2001)*

### washpool
  
noun. a pool in which sheep or wool were washed [usually constructed of a series of dammed pools in a stream and now commemorated in such place names as Washpool, Washpen or Sheepwash Creek.]  
See: sheepwash; woolwash; wash dip  
> MURISON Puketoi Station Diary Nov 11 Hurdles taken down to Taieri for washpool. *(1859)*  
> Hawke's Bay Weekly Courier June 25. 5 After leaving the station about five miles you turn across the country, leaving the main road at what is called the Washpool creek, it having been used for that purpose in the early days ... *(1886)*  
> WILSON & FRASER Book of Pioneers 8 Other Maori names in the district are Waikaka and Okapua, the stream known as 'the Washpool creek' because of the sheepwashing operations carried out on its banks. *(1936)*  
> MCDONALD White Stone Country 53 Elwell spent much of his time at the Waikoura, helping to build a shepherd's hut and a washpool for sheep. *(1962)*  
> Straight Furrow Nov 6. 18 There are a number of creeks in this country named Washpool where early settlers washed their sheep each year. *(1963)*  
> RICKARD Strangers in the Wilderness 80 The custom of washing sheep is commemorated in at least two place names. The Washpool is the name of a farm at Maraekakaho in Hawke's Bay ... *(1967)*  
> MARTIN Forgotten Worker 85 Then they washed the sheep at the creek 'washpool' and put them into the shed for shearing. *(1990)*

### washpool
  
noun.  
[NOTES: wash-pool, wash pool]  
> Packed bale of greasy wool. put up washpool in Wedderburn. *(Nov 15, 1859,Puketoi Station Diary)*  
> **BUTLER** ... yet without good wash pools and a good woolshed the wool can never be well got up. It is true I can wash well enough and can manage to stave over next shearing ..., when my wool money will be something considerable ... *(May 31, 1861,Letter)* [NOTES: 1861 May 31 Butler to his Father in 1919 Jones Samuel Butler - A Memoir 1/93]  
> **ELWELL** His work at [the station] ... was to be ... the formation of a wash-pool ... for the sheep. *(1878,Boy Colonists,32)*  
> **PRESTON** `Much of the wool was scoured on the farm [in the early days], especially the merino. ... But scoured wool took a good deal of time - the bales were carted to the washpool, the wool washed, dried and turned in the open - wind and rain stopped work frequently'. `There was a `wash' and a drying paddock at both Longlands and Haldon; the pool was fed by a race which had to be cleaned out before each shearing and kept in repair ...' *(1978,Woolgatherers,43)*  
> In January of the three following years defendant washed his sheep in a new washpool by said stream;  but about Nov. 1858 he received a letter of complaint from plaintiff. *(Oct 8, 1859,Otago Witness,3)*  
> The estimated cost of a bridle track from Pillans' to the wash pool creek was L20. *(Jul 28, 1893,Clutha Leader,6)*

### wash-tub
  
noun.  
[NOTES: constr with THE [this may be a practical joke on a new chum and not meant seriously] --(?nonce)]  
> **McHUTCHESON** Among the various methods [of descending a steep hill] in vogue, none, perhaps, is more picturesque than that known as the `wash-tub' ... you sit down, tie your legs together, grasp a foot firmly in each hand, shut your eyes, and roll off. *(1892,Camp-Life in Fiordland,5)*

### wash-up
  
noun.  
> **HEINZ** Monday 29th. Finished wash-up of first paddock. *(1974,Bright Fine Gold,95)* [NOTES: 1863 quote. From Diary of an Unsuccessful Digger, Christchurch Star, 27 Aug. 1931]  
> This explanation seemed to me insufficient to account for the large quantities of lead obtained at the periodical `wash-up'; ... *(1903,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,35/403)*

### Wasser
  
noun.  
See: Wazza  
[NOTES: also Wassa]  
> **JACKSON** Doubtless any old soldier who fought in Egypt in the last war will remember the `Wasser', with the streets the same, the houses ...; but the soldiers of this war call this unsavoury portion of Cairo the `Berker'. *(1943,Passage to Tobruk,97)*

### waste
  
verb. to beat up; incapacitate  
[NOTES: (criminal's) 1984 16 M E65 Pakuranga Coll. 30A TO WASTE [verb for fighting: beat, kill: M/F16-17 36] -->1984 16 F E3 Pakuranga Coll. 30A WASTE,TO]  
> **NEWBOLD** He fell on to ground, then I started swinging the club and wasted him good and proper. *(1982,Big Huey, The,199)*

### waste land
  
noun. [historical]  
[NOTES: often in plural]  
> Having by these methods obviated the dangers of the acquisition of large tracts of Country by mere Landjobbers, it will be your duty to obtain, by fair and equal contracts with the Natives, the Cession to the Crown of such Waste Lands as may be progressively required for the occupation of Settlers resorting to New Zealand.  All such contracts should be made by yourself, through the intervention of an Officer expressly appointed to watch over the interests of the Aborigines as their Protector. *(1971,Speeches & Documents,13)* [NOTES: 1839 Aug 14 Instructions from the Secretary of State to Captain Hobson, C.O. 209/4 pp.251-81 in 1971 Speeches and Documents 13]  
> And we do hereby give ... to the governor of our said colony of New Zealand ... full power ... to make ... grants of waste land, to us belonging ... to private persons ... *(1949,Selected Documents,8)* [NOTES: 1840 Nov 16 Charter for Erecting the Colony of NZ in 1949 Rutherford ed. Select Documents 8]  
> The sale and settlement of waste lands is the next of the general topics to which I propose to advert in this despatch. *(Dec 9, 1840,GBPP,(No.311) 30)* [NOTES: 1840 Dec 9 Lord John Russell to Governor Hobson in GBPP House of Commons 1841 (No.311) 30]  
> **WOOD** The <i>`sufficient'</i> price of the Wakefield system is a fallacy ... Waste land is often dear at a gift. *(1843,Twelve Months in Wellington,50)*  
> **MONRO** At the same time, seeing that people were determined to emigrate, were enamoured of the Wakefield system, and had made up their minds to buy waste land at any price, as was the case a few years ago, it was certainly very reasonable conduct on the part of the British Government to pass such an Act [as Lord Stanley's]. *(1844,Notes of a Journey)* [NOTES: 1844 Monro Notes of a Journey (Nelson Examiner July 20 et seq.) in 1898 Hocken Contributions 259]  
> **F.MATHEW** Any attempt on his part to assert the right of the Crown without purchase, to what are called `Waste Lands' ... *(1844,Report,2/18)*  
> **WAKEFIELD** At first, the Government here appeared inclined to ... take upon itself that charge of disposing of the waste-lands for the common good ... *(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,73)*  
> The assets [of the New Zealand Company] consisted of the unsold waste land in the province of New Munster. *(Sep 27, 1851,Lyttelton Times,5)*  
> **SWAINSON** [of the NZ Association] ... a body of private individuals associated together for the purpose of buying, selling, and making a profit by the sale of the waste lands of the Crown. *(1853,Auckland,10)*  
> **RUTHERFORD** As the maintenance of uniformity in the mode of disposing of the waste lands of the Colony would not seem to be of essential importance, ... *(1949,Selected Documents,59)* [NOTES: 1854 May 27 Address of Officer administering the Government to the General Assembly in 1949 Rutherford ed. Select Documents 59]  
> **FITTON** But it is a work of time ... to bring even a portion of 100 acres (the usual size of a rural section) of wasteland into a state of good cultivation. *(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),248)*  
> ... there are some persons who do not approve of the price that has been fixed on our Waste Lands. *(Jul 1, 1857,Lyttelton Times,2)*  
> **TAYLOR** [Maori] fears afterwards were abundantly confirmed by Earl Grey's celebrated despatch to Governor Grey, ordering him [111] to seize the waste lands of the natives, contrary to the express provision of the treaty, which guaranteed all their territorial rights to them. *(1868,Past and Present of NZ,110)*  
> **VOGEL** Public - or as they are called `waste' - lands are sold on several principles. *(1875,Official Handbook of NZ,103)*  
> . <i>Charadrius obscurus</i>, Gml. Dusky, Red-breasted, or Mountain Plover, Big Dottrel [si], Tuturiwatu, Paturiwhata, Tituriwhatu-pukunui. - This excellent gamebird formerly bred on the Canterbury Plains ...; since farms and settlements have occupied the waste lands of the plains, for the most part it has retired to the mountains of the back country. *(Sep 1885,NZ Journal of Science,61)* [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]  
> **REED** These `first purchasers' were to be accorded a special privilege; ... unsold rural areas, termed `waste lands', might be leased for pasturage in the proportion of five acres for every acre purchased, at an annual rental of sixteen-and-eightpence for each hundred acres. *(1947,Story of Canterbury, The,106)*  
> **BUTLER** Most of the waste lands in the province are now paying three farthings per acre. There is the danger also of scab. *(Feb 10, 1960,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,iv/42)*  
> **VEITCH** `Hundreds', an old English word referring to enough land to support a hundred families, was the name given to those large blocks of land which were set aside at appropriate intervals for settlement. `Waste lands' or runs, which lay outside the `Hundreds', were leased by the Government to pastoralists. *(1976,Clyde on the Dunstan,43)*  
> **ANDERSON** The early settlers had bought their land in Britain for three pounds an acre. They were privileged colonists because all the unsold rural land, termed as `waste land', could be leased for pasturage at sixteen shillings for each 100 acres.(contrast US <i>wild lands</i>) *(1981,Both Sides of the River,8)*  
> He condemned the way waste lands were administered and urged that only men who knew something about it should be appointed to Waste Land Boards. *(Nov 7, 1893,Bruce Herald,3)*  
> Mr. John Studholme (Ashburton) said that in Canterbury pinus insignis had become quite a marketable timber, and he himself had planted sixty acres recently of waste land. *(Aug 2, 1913,Evening Post,15)*  
> How can a man with a family, thrown on a section of waste land, remote from other settlements, make a living? *(Mar 5, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,8)*  
> The sale of waste lands held on Monday.. shews that the demand for land in good localities is not by any means diminished. *(May 3, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,23)*  
> Something would have to be done if the Dominion was to develop and reach real prosperity, he said, and discussed the possibility of developing waste lands into small holdings where young couples could make a living. *(Jul 14, 1937,Evening Post,13)*

### Waste Lands  Committee
  
noun.  
[NOTES: aka Waste Lands committee]  
> .. we have some faith in the advantages expected from the Waste Lands committee's report ... *(Jan 16, 1912,Lyttelton Times,4)*  
> "Telegram just received from Mr Mongomery, to the effect that Waste Lands Committee Legislative Council have decided that the Railway Bill will be confined to making the line to Little River only, by Government, to whom the endowments are to be handed." *(Sep 20, 1878,Akaroa Mail and Banks Peninsula Advertiser,2)*  
> The Impounding Act Amendment Bill was referred to the Waste Lands Committee. *(Aug 11, 1885,Bruce Herald,3)*  
> The Land for Settlement Bill was in the Council this afternoon.   Mr Stevens, Chair of the Waste Lands Committee, said the Bill had undergone such changes at the hands of the Committee that it was almost unrecognisable from the Bill sent to the Council. *(Sep 27, 1894,Poverty Bay Herald,3)*  
> The Land for Settlement Consolidation Bill was read the second time and referred to the Waste Lands Committee. *(Oct 3, 1900,Bay of Plenty Times,3)*  
> In 1877, as a member of the General Assembly, he was on the Waste Lands Committee, and had charge of the Land Act of that year in its passage through the House. *(Nov 30, 1925,Evening Post,9)*

### Waste Lands Act
  
noun.  
[NOTES: cf Waste Lands Bill]  
> WASTE LANDS ACT. *(Aug 21, 1844,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,2)*  
> "To investigate the effects of the Waste Lands Act, 1854, and subsequent similar Acts in order to determine the rights of the Maori people to surplus lands and to remove all existing anomalies and injustice ..." *(Oct 16, 1935,Evening Post,12)*  
> The Provincial Waste Lands Act is reserved for the signification of her Majesty's pleasure. *(Sep 30, 1854,New Zealand Spectator & Cook's Strait Guardian,3)*  
> I also beg to request that the powers recently delegated by the Governor under 6th clause of the "Waste Lands Act" to the late Superintendent may, if His Excellency should think fit, be also conferred on me. *(Feb 15, 1862,Hawke's Bay Herald,3)*  
> NOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN, that the following Allotments of Rural Land will be offered for sale by public auction, in terms of the 35th clause of "The Otago Waste Lands Act, 1866," in the Land Office, Dunedin, on Monday, the 8th day of August next, at 12 o'clock noon, at the upset price of 10s per acre ... *(Jul 9, 1870,Otago Witness,12)*  
> The new regulations he succeeded in passing were embodied by the General Assembly in "The Marlborough Waste Lands Act, 1867." *(Apr 13, 1907,Marlborough Express,5)*  
> The new 'Auckland Waste Lands Act' authorises the actual giving away of land to anyone who will cultivate it. *(Apr 30, 1910,Observer (Auckland),16)*  
> On the sea front of this property was a small section of 84 acres, granted to William Kells, a corporal in the 65th Regiment, under "The Waste Lands Act, 1858." *(Apr 21, 1920,Observer (Auckland),8)*

### Waste Lands Bill
  
noun. a controversial land allocation bill presented to Parliament in 1854.  
> SEWELL Journal June 23 in McIntyre (1980) Sewell Journal 1853-1856 46 It [a Bill] is a sort of pendant to the Waste Lands' Bill which is to be moved on Friday for Wakefield's convenience ... *(1854)*  
> ibid. July 30 in McIntyre (1980) 61 Our Bills have gone through the House, our Waste Lands' Bill the most opposed by Wakefield and others. *(1854)*  
> ibid Sept 10 in McIntyre (1980) 83 We are driving a Waste Lands' Bill through (FitzGerald's not mine) simply with the view of stopping the Governor from handing over the Waste Lands to Wakefield to be jobbed away in Electioneering. *(1854)*  
> NZPD September 12 The Council then went into Committee on the Waste Lands Bill. *(1854)*

### Waste Lands Board
  
noun.

### Waste Lands Board
  
noun.  
[NOTES: 1930 Acland 4]  
> ... as these runs are likely to be wanted for settlement, I should not recommend their being thrown into a gold field till after they have been thrown into Hundreds when the Waste Lands Board could refuse to sell any portion ... considered highly auriferous. *(May 20, 1869,Letter)* [NOTES: 1869 May 20 letter in 1962 Evans Waikaka Saga 16]  
> .. it is enacted that it shall be lawful for the Waste Lands Board, with the approval of the Governor, to set apart in any part of the district or blocks, land within which licenses to occupy land or leases thereof on deferred payments may be granted either exclusively, or within which land shall be open for license or lease on deferred payments, or for sale on immediate payment ... *(Jan 8, 1877,Otago Daily Times,3)*  
> The right of any applicant to purchase the land as a bona fide working settler upon land, shall be determined by the Waste Land Board ... *(Jul 21, 1854,Daily Southern Cross,3)*  
> A meeting of the Waste Lands Board was held on Thursday, there being present Messrs J. G. Holdsworth (Commissioner and Chairman), H. Bunny, and T. Mason. *(Jan 12, 1884,Manawatu Times,3)*  
> [A CARD.]   E. I. Lord (Junr.) .. Waste Lands Board attended. *(Jun 20, 1908,Grey River Argus,2)*  
> "That in the case of unleased Crown lands the Waste Lands Board of the district should be responsible for reporting the existence of Californian thistle and other noxious weeds, and for the cost of their eradication." *(May 31, 1912,Ashburton Guardian,7)*

noun.

noun. land believed not to be used by Maori

noun.

noun.

> J of Ag June 20. 415 The wool from the covered hoggets showed very little wasty tip. *(1938)*

noun.

phrase. being in solitary confinement

### water fern
  
noun. a tall bracken-like fern, Histiopteris incisa (aka soft fern).  
[NOTES: [JD notes that Brownsey gives only Christella dentata for this.] [nf OED2]]  
> J of Ag August 20. 98 The experiments include the following .. burning and sowing of hard fern and patches of water-fern 1924 ibid 102 Water-fern and other young growth comes away through the felled scrub during the spring...In the case of hard fern and water-fern and felled wineberry, fuchsia etc, early summer burning after a good dry spell would seem advisable. *(1924)*  
> NZ Farmer March 23. 5 There is sometimes some problem from invasion by hard fern or water fern ... *(1967)*  
> ADDENBROOKE Home From the Hill 157He galloped away out of sight so I followed his footprints by creeping between the water-ferns in his direction. *(1992)*  
> NGAWHIKA Tell Them I've Gone Pighunting 153 These ravines and gorges had never been planted in pine and were choked thick with scrub, waterferns, toitoi and blackberry. *(1996)*  
> HINDMARSH Come Be a Pioneer 217 She [a mare] was very distressed, partially hidden in a patch of water fern and couldn't stand up. [waterfern] *(1997)*  
> The water fern (<i>Histiopteris incisa</i>) and several species of <i>Hypolepis</i> are opportunist spcies that get through their life cycle as quickly as possible. *(Jan 2001,NZ Geographic,No.49/78)*

### water fern

> **CROOKES** Take, for example, the water fern, Azolla rubra, which is so common on stagnant pools and slowly-moving streams. Certainly at the first glance there is nothing about it that even remotely resembles a fern. *(1926,Plant Life in Maoriland,137)*  
> The plain fact of the matter is that <i>Azolla rubra</i> is a floating water fern (retoreto), native to New Zealand, which covers the surface of still waters in both islands. It is partial to a pond, it puts up with a bog, a swamp, any kind of water that doesn't move an inch... Or to the Wairarapa, where the water fern gives its bizarre red-carpet treatment to kahikatea swamps. *(Aug 28, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94)*

### water joey
  
noun.  
> The watercart supplied water to the traction engine, which drove the engine by producing steam. The driver of the cart was called a 'water joey'. *(Feb 2002,NZ Memories,No. 34/55)*  
> This is where the 'Water Joey' comes in.You must have water to get steam so wehad a water tank to go along too. *(1990,Dovedale's History,00)*  
> **WICKHAM** Water Joeys [metaphor Jupiter Pluvius - check] *(1891,Casual Ramblings,24)*  
> **OGILVIE** [reference 1880] About a dozen men were needed to operate such a [threshing] mill. The engine driver who was the boss, the `feeder' who fed in the sheaves, ... a cook, a `water joey', and a `chaffie'. *(1971,Moonshine County,151)*  
> **NEAVE** Later, traction engines [for threshing] ... had their own galley and cook, and it was a great sight to see engine, mill, water-cart and dog box - a two wheeled hut. The boss and Water Joey slept near the machinery to be handy to get up steam in the morning, and then get the cookshop going. *(1980,Land of Munros, The,90)*  
> **KERSE** In many areas, the most difficult job was to organise a water supply for the [traction] engines. ... Natural water supplies had to be exploited and as these often had very difficult access the life of a `water joey' was no bed of roses. <i>Ibid.</i> 148 The usual gang for stack threshing [c1900] was 12 men. The boss kept steam up and attended to the mill adjustments and lubrication, three men worked in the bag hole, the bag-rammer, the bag-sower, and the bag-carrier; two men were on top of the mill, a band cutter and a man to turn the sheaves to the band cutter: two men were on the stack forking to the `turner', and then there was the chaffy, two men on the straw stack, a builder and a straw `walloper' and last of all, the water joey. Threshing out of the stock required an even larger gang, usually about three more men. *(1984,Knapdale Run, The,147)*  
> "Has anyone seen the water Joey?   I suppose he has gone over to the pub." *(Nov 4, 1927,Ellesmere Guardian,5)*  
> A steam-mill needs a "water-joey". *(1944,Korero (AEWS Background Bulletin),2(7) : 9)*  
> There is the "water joey" who drove the water cart for the old threshing mills which were powered by traction engines and who sometimes had unofficial skills in augmenting the supplies of the cookhouse. *(Feb 11, 1977,Press, The,19)*  
> **TRAMP**  *(1891,Casual Ramblings,24)* [NOTES: No sign of water joey p.24 or any otherpage. The man only went from hotel tohotel barely taking in the countryside.]  
> **ANDERSON** "Where's that blasted water-joey? Me boilers are nearly dry."... One thing about the young water-joeys. they didn't dash off to the boozer, but they were irresponsible little devils... <i>Ibid.</i> 2 The water-joey's first job in the morning was to catch the draught-horse, yoke it to a dray which carried a 200-gallon square water-truck, then drive to the nearest water-race or pond and fill the tank by hand-pump. *(1976,Water Joey,1)* [NOTES: 1977 edition]  
> **SINCLAIR & HARREX** The 'water-joey' who drove it [sc.  the threshing mill].. had to maintain the water supply for the traction engine, and for cooking and washing. *(1978,Looking Back,86)*

### water joey
  
noun. a threshing mill team member who carted the water to the steam engine and galley.  
See: tanky; waterman  
> ANDERSON The Water-Joey 2 The water-joey's first job in the morning was to catch the draught-horse, yoke it to a dray which carried a 200-gallon square water-truck, then...fill the tank by hand-pump. *(1976)*  
> LOUGHNAN Glossary 46 The water-joey was the man who carted the water to the steam engine, and to the galley, of course, of a threshing mill. *(1981)*  
> KERSE Knapdale Run 147 In many areas, the most difficult job was to organise a water-supply for the engines... As these often had very little access the life of a 'water-joey' was no bed of roses. [water joey; joey] *(1984)*

### waterpot
  
noun.  
[NOTES: (shearers')]  
> <i>Woolshed terms.</i>-The <i>water pot </i>is a tin of water (usually disinfected) which a shearer has tied by him to wet his shears in. To <i>gap</i> shears is to make (accidentally) a g. in the edge. <i>Sweat rag:</i> kept by the shearer to wipe his brow. <i>To grind</i> (shears) is always used without the noun. A shearer never `grinds his shears.' <i>Moccasins</i> are the sacking slippers which most shearers make to work in. Unless the owner of the station is a careful, observant man, he will hardly recognise his own wool pack tops. *(Jan 27, 1934,Press, The,15)*  
> **NEWTON** As each man finished his sheep he gave his blades a brief touch with the oilstone and placed them in the `water-pot' nailed to the wall beside his porthole. As he caught his next sheep the water would free the grease which would otherwise clog the blades. *(1949,High Country Days,50)*  
> **OGONOWSKA-COATES** <i>Waterpot. </i>A tin full of water beside the shearer's stand into which he dips his shears to rinse off the grease from the previous fleece. *(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,98)*  
> <i>water pot:</i> Old paint tin, plastic bucket or similar item with water in it for a shearer to leave his/her shears in while catching a sheep. Contains disinfectant to stop the transfer of disease form one sheep to another. Also contains wool to stop shedhands form drinking out of it. *(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/29)* [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]

### water-race
  
noun.  
[NOTES: re goldmining]  
> I have the honour to enclose herewith application ... for water races from Waikaka ... and Shepherds Hut Creek. *(May 20, 1869,Letter)* [NOTES: 1869 May 20 letter in 1962 Evans Waikaka Saga 16]  
> Water-race companies often made their fortunes more easily than the miners ... From January 11 to March 11 1870, the miners of Naseby went on strike, and finally induced the `water-squatters' to lower their rates from five pounds a `Hogburn' head, to two pounds. [fn] A `Hogburn' head was the stream of water which would run through a box seventeen inches wide by one inch in depth with five pounds of pressure. *(Jul 25, 1931,Otago Daily Times)* [NOTES: c1870s in 1931 July 25 Otago Daily Times in 1948 Cowan Down the Years in the Maniototo 43]  
>  *(1929,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,26/6)* [NOTES: 1887 Gordon Reports on Goldfields, Roads, Water-races and other Works in connection with Mining page 15 in 1929 NZ Geol. Survey Bull. No 26 (N.S.)/6 (Morgan, Waihi)]  
> **D'AVIGDOR** A vast clearing ... is filled with a perfect network of `water-races', as these wooden channels are termed. *(1888,Antipodean Notes,124)*  
> **THOMSON** [A miner] Digs a ditch with sloping base,/ Which he terms a water race;/ *(1909,Ballads about Business,67)*  
> **B EATTIE** Many creeks were dammed and water-races were everywhere. If a miner asked for so many heads, the water was measured out to him by a gauge. *(1947,Pioneer Recollections,2)*  
> The Superintendent has very promptly performed his promise to the deputation from the Lower Manuherikia, that he would take steps to preserve the rights of the owners of water-races, which it was said were jeopardised by the recently published 4th Rule of the Gold Fields. *(May 3, 1866,Otago Daily Times,6)*  
> The expenditure and liabilities for survey and construction of water-races undertaken by the General Government up to 30th June, 1876, was L331,026 ... *(Aug 11, 1876,Grey River Argus,2)*  
> At the Warden's Court yesterday Mr Warden Bush stated that, for the future, plans of water races, machine, special or business sites, or for any other right or privilege for which a plan is required, must be deposited in the Warden's Office within three months of the lodging of the application, that being the time limited by the statute for the survey and depositing of plans for special claims and licensed holdinge [sic], unless an extension of time has been applied for and granted. *(Nov 17, 1897,Thames Star,4)*

### water-race
  
noun. a stormwater ditch or drain (esp. at the sides of roads)  
> **BARRY** They were very `cheeky' and I knocked one of them into a water-race. *(1879,Some Ups and Downs in NZ&A,284)*  
> **WEBB** ... the dog paddled slowly up and down in the water-race on the opposite side of the road, *(1925,Miss Peters' Special,47)*  
> **SUMPTER** Cleaning out a water-race with a flame-thrower ... *(1948,In Search of Central Otago,7)*  
> **SIMPSON** My first job on relief was cleaning out the water races in Paparua County. *(1974,Sugarbag Years,90)*  
> Tenders for No. 6 Contract, Rakaia water-races.   Loan for Repair of Headworks of Malvern Water-race. *(Aug 30, 1887,Star, The,3)*  
> "All lands in any district or subdivision, which by their altitude, configuration, or other physical causes are excluded from deriving any possible benefit from a water-race, shall be exempted from all rates to be levied in respect of such water race;  and also all lands which are supplied with water from springs, streams, or private water races thereon shall also be so exempted from rates unless the owners thereof respectively consent to the same being levied." *(Dec 12, 1904,Ashburton Guardian,2)*  
> A letter was received from the Ashburton County Council, stating that the Council would grant 20ft. pipes for water races free on condition that the Council pay nothing further in connection with the fords so piped, and providing that the Council had the right to say on what roads races shall be piped. *(Feb 7, 1865,Ashburton Guardian,3)*

### water-race
  
noun. a ditch or drain to or from an animal enclosure or paddock.  
> WILSON & FRASER Book of the Pioneers 21 .. a short water race in Section 8 joined the race leading from the dam to the sheepwash. *(1936)*  
> STAPLES Cooks & Shepherds Come Away 138 Water-races needed cleaning out more than ever now that the grass was growing so fast. [water race] *(1964)*

### water-race company
  
noun.  
> **PFAFF** I came down in 1872 to the Auckland Beach, where I bought ... a water-race company and opened up a claim. *(1914,Diggers' Story, The,142)*  
> **MAY** In a separate category were the water-race companies, specializing in the sale of water to mining parties and common to all fields. Although only two or three registered, the amount of capital invested in this branch of the industry was probably considerable. *(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,242,1962)*  
> The directors of the Waikaia Water-race Company visited the district on Tuesday, and are in hopes that the race will be a great success. *(Sep 30, 1892,Mataura Ensign,3)*  
> ".. He has also secured an option to purchase the water rights held by the Rimu Water-race Company." *(Jun 26, 1909,Ashburton Guardian,1)*  
> The parties who so nobly volunteered their services and accomplished this difficult undertaking were the members of the "Water-race Company." *(Jul 8, 1863,Otago Daily Times,5)*  
> MONT D'OR GOLD MINING AND WATER-RACE COMPANY, LIMITED. *(Jan 23, 1889,West Coast Times,2)*

### watershed
  
noun.  
[NOTES: [?omit: see discards]]  
> In the old days this meant the line dividing the drainage area of two rivers. Now most people mean by it the area drained by a river. (C.) *(1933,Press, The,13)*  
> In the old days this meant the line dividing the drainage area of two rivers. Now most people mean by it the area drained by a river. (C.) *(Dec 30, 1933,Press, The,13)* [NOTES: Acland Glossary]  
> **TURNER** In New Zealand, for example, there is a local use of the word <i>watershed</i> to mean the catchment area which feeds a rive. It seems to be so use in a story by Philip Wilson [<i>Landfall</i>, no. 19, September 151, p. 180.] in the phrase `the deer that were destroying the watersheds'. An English lecturer in geography tells me that she has spent much time in trying to eradicate this usage from the essays of geography students. *(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,85)*

noun. the waterside , the wharves

> **DAVIS** ' ... I came down to the waterside. The old union. Never been off it since, except for the six months during the 'fifty-one bust up.' *(1964,Watersiders,108)*

### waterside worker
  
noun.  
> <i>Waterside worker</i> - longshoreman. *(1944,US War Dept. Short Guide to NZ,39)*

> The use of waterside workers as tallyclerks should be discontinued. *(Sep 4, 1952,Evening Post,8)*

### watersider
  
noun. [New Zealand & Australia] A dockside worker.  
[NOTES: also called (informally) wharfie, watersider a wharf cargo worker. Internet search Feb 2006: no spread to other Englishes.]  
> For some time certain sections of watersiderswho term themselves loyal men have been making periodic attacks on certain officials of the harbour board. *(Feb 4, 1914,Evening Post,10)*  
> **SARGESON** I see they're having trouble with those watersiders again.<i>Ibid.</i> 187 Now, about those water-siders. *(1949,I Saw in My Dream,160)*  
> In her review.. Diana Wichtel says she heard Aunt Daisy complaining about watersiders' wives getting the bread made by flour unloaded by servicemen  during the 1951 waterfront dispute. *(Dec 14, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12)*  
> [letter from] `Watersider' [about] `work on the wharves' *(Feb 4, 1914,Evening Post,10)* [NOTES: [?cit in OED]]  
> [cast of Carnival play includes] Watersiders, Factory Girls, cabinet Ministers, ... Tabbies from Takapuna, Sheelahs from the Salvation Army and Femmes savantes from Symonds Street *(1921,Auckland University Carnival Book,19)*  
> **LEE** I watched watersiders work. *(1934,Children of the Poor,193,1950)*  
> In the Coronation Hall, the watersiders' hall, a couple of men stood by the fire, talking. *(1948,Our Own Country,96)* [NOTES: (`Lyttelton') from Korero, AEWS magazine]  
> **SHADBOLT** ... I would stay with my uncle, the watersider, ... and I would study at university. *(1959,New Zealanders, The,29,1986)* [NOTES: The Woman's Story]  
> <i>watersider n. Austral. & N.Z.</i> a dock labourer. *(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,1330)* [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 872; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 542]]  
> The liners Turakina and Kent were berthed in Wellington this morning, and were being worked by the watersiders. *(Oct 9, 1925,Evening Post)*  
> [the single employer organisation] was broken up and stevedoring companies empolying labour emerged. Watersiders, a term with a stigma, became stevedores. *(Dec 14, 2005,Dominion Post,C4)*  
> Westport watersiders do not anticpate extension of the strike to Westport. *(Nov 30, 1910,West Coast Times,4)*  
> The watersiders refused to handle passengers' luggage on the Awatea because it was raining. *(Feb 9, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,11)*  
> In the heyday of shipping, more than 2000 watersiders, together with associated tally clerks and harbour board workers, were engaged on the Wellington wharves. *(Dec 5, 2001,Dominion,28)*

### water-squatter
  
noun.  
[NOTES: (goldminers' obs)]  
> **COWAN** Water-race companies often made their fortunes more easily than the miners ... From January 11 to March 11 1870, the miners of Naseby went on strike, and finally induced the `water-squatters' to lower their rates from five pounds a `Hogburn' head, to two pounds. [fn] A `Hogburn' head was the stream of water which would run through a box seventeen inches wide by one inch in depth with five pounds of pressure. - J.J.T., Otago Daily Times, July 25, 1932. *(1948,Down the Years in the Maniototo,43)* [NOTES: c1870s in 1931 July 25 Otago Daily Times in 1948 Cowan Down the Years in the Maniototo 43]

### Wattie's tomato sauce

Even at quite posh restaurants, the waiters will, when asked, dutifully produce a little white ramekin dish of tomato sauce, albeit wordlessly and somewhat superciliously. *(Apr 24, 2004,Dominion Post,E7)*  
> But the highlight of the evening had to be the genuinely daggy Aotearoa platter, brought to the table with the shrimp cocktail riding high in the retro martini glass, with battered mini-hotdogs on sticks with authentic dipping sauce of Wattie's tomato, as well as Marmite and cheese club sandwiches, good roast lamb and mint, kumara shavings and, for sweets, a thick pancake rolled up with lemon and sugar just like mum used to make. *(Jul 19, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),26)*
