### wattle yard
  
noun.  
> **KENNAWAY** The wild brutes tried to break away in every direction, and ... poor C. would have stood a small chance ... if we had not been there to help him in forcing them into a small wattle yard near at hand; ... *(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,135)*

### wattle-and-daub
  
noun.

### wattle-and-daub
  
adjective.  
[NOTES: also wattle-and-dab]  
> **HALL-JONES** a small wattle-and-daub hut ... *(1971,Mr Surveyor Thomson,30)* [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection) 1850s in 1971 Hall-Jones Mr Surveyor Thomson 30]  
> **THOMSON** The accommodation-house consisted of a small wattle-and-daub hut containing two appartments.[check spg] *(1867,Rambles with a Philosopher,30)*  
> **GUTHRIE** Sections were allotted upon which ... they first built wattle and daub huts, ... *(1930,NZ Memories,118)*  
> **AYSON** The usual method adopted with wattle-and-daub houses was for men to puddle the clay with their feet, and when this was well mixed to add [64] chopped grass to the clay. *(1937,Thomas,63)*  
> **HYDE** Starkie went out into the main and only street [in Central Otago]. ... Wattle and daub huts, walls of thick yellow clay, ... *(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,57)*  
> **BEATTIE** There were no fern trees in the nearest bush, so the construction of the huts [c1840] was of a decidedly wattle-and-daub nature, but all were roofed with thatch. <i>Ibid.</i> 41 These huts were of the old-time `wattle-and-daub' construction - that is, posts were driven into the ground and `wattles' (round sticks) were nailed across them 4 or 5 inches apart, and then clay was `daubed' in between and around them and smoothed over. *(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,5)*  
> ... we may divide colonial architecture into periods or ages. First, the wattle-and-dab period, with its contemporaneous, but more advanced, varieties of fern tree and totara bark ... *(1876,TrNZI,8/166)* [NOTES: (Blair Building Materials Otago)]  
> `Old-Timer's' wattle-and-dub houses should surely be wattle-and-daub. It is a flight of imagination tracing `dub' back to the Spanish American `adobe'. *(Feb 10, 1921,Quick March,13)*  
> **LYON** Even back in these gale-rifted solitudes, [of Cape Terawhiti], one came on the remains of `wattle and dab' homesteads which marked the abortive attempt of some early settler. *(1952,Faring South,140)*  
> **PARK** A wattle-and-daub house is tight and snug. [Otago 1863] *(1978,One-a-pecker, Two-a-pecker,124)*  
> .. he had already caused to be constructed a church of reeds - much after the style of Parson Johnson's "wattle and dab" church at Sydney in the early days. *(Mar 6, 1916,NZ Truth,6)*  
> He described his early days with his brothers in Canterbury - how they built a wattle and dab hut to serve as a shelter while they were employed cutting firewood for the settlers living in the open country. *(Jan 15, 1926,Evening Post,7)*  
> I have no doubt many of Mr Fulton's constituents at the Taieri could enlighten him regarding the relative state of their finances before Gabriels Gully was discovered and 12 months after that memorable event in the history of Otago - not one but what could testify that the bawbees were circulating more freely after the gold discovery, wattle and daub houses disappeared, sledges gave place to buggies, population streamed in from the neighbouring Colonies, and the march of progress that has placed Otago in the front rank of the provinces commenced. *(Aug 25, 1883,Otago Witness,12)*  
> Then you build a tin hut or a wattle and daub humpy, or a raupa whare, and call it an hotel. *(Aug 2, 1913,Observer (Auckland),16)*  
> Most of the contents of the kitchen, a detached wattle and dab erection, was saved. *(Aug 25, 1865,Otago Daily Times,5)*  
> The buildings too are well enough, but I confess (pace all loyal Invercargillites) I like to look at a fern tree cottage or a wattle and dab hut better than at your large heavy and rather sombre-looking structures of brick and cement. *(Jun 8, 1885,Southland Times,2)*

### wattle-and-daub
  
noun. an early method of making house walls, of woven slender branches plastered with mud. spg also <i>wattle and dab</i> an early colonial form of building imported from rural England  
[NOTES: aka wattle and daub, wattle-and-dab, wattle and dab]  
> **BIDWILL & WOODHOUSE** There were very few houses built with the sawn timber, nearly all being made with wattle and dab or "cob". *(1927,Bidwill of Pihautea,61)* [NOTES: Quote from Orbell, 1909]  
> The first white settler in Wellington was..  a Mr. Todd.. who had a wattle-and dab hut on the beach when the first ship arrived. *(Aug 21, 1890,Otago Witness,32)*  
> **WILLIAMS** The circumstance of the burning of Capn. Duke's house has made us somewhat thoughtful relative to <i>raupo</i> buildings. ... This question will require some consideration but wattle and dab is exceedingly expeditious and very durable. *(Dec 17, 1827,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,91,1961)*  
> **TAYLOR** They [natives] invited me into their hut which in imitation of the European cottages was built of wattle & dab & had a chimney. *(Apr 1983,Journal,16)*  
> ...a house of `wattle and dab' ... [that is] woven supplejacks covered with a plaster of mud. *(1846,NZ Journal,171/166)*  
> **POWER** I have planted a large piece of ground with peas, beans, cabbages, &c.; have made a large fence round my garden, and have been superintending the erection of my `wattle and dab' mansion at `Ballybotherem'. *(1849,Sketches in NZ,39)*  
> ... `wattle and dab' system of architecture ... *(1850,Otago Journal,6/95,1848)*  
> **PAUL** As soon as maybe after his arrival, [the new settler] engages himself as a shepherd, or bullock-driver, or hut-keeper at a station, ... makes a hut of sod or wattle and dab, ... encloses [his land] with ... a post and rail or ditch and bank fence. *(1857,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,27)*  
> **HART** [At Christchurch, 1852] a number of tenements were built of sod, raupo, wattle, and dab. This latter ... consisted of saplings, with clay puddled in between. *(1886,Stray Leaves,27)*  
> **MORRIS** <i>Wattle-and-dab,</i> a rough mode of architecture, very common in Australia at an early date. The phrase and its meaning are Old English. It was originally <i>Wattle-and-daub</i>. The style, but not the word, is described in the quotation from Governor Phillip, 1789. *(1898,Austral English,503)*  
> **HOCKEN** By degrees little houses sprang up in every direction, the usual order of architecture being that of `wattle and dab'; saplings were fixed side by side, and the interstices filled with clay, ... *(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,101)*  
> **AYSON** After breakfast ... we continued ... through Lovell's Flat, named after John Lovell who built a wattle-and-dab hut there for his shepherd. *(1937,Pioneering in Otago,26)*  
> <i>wattle-and-dab n.</i> (originally wattle-and-daub) rough huts of upright posts, wattled with acacia twigs and plastered with clay. *(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),21)*  
> **BEST** I shall describe Govt House. Govt House is a Mauri built dwelling of Wattle and dab and Wattle and dab is this. A number of stakes are driven into the ground and a species of [v]ine worked in between them so as to form a wall of basketwork exactly like a Gabion. This big basket is then pelted with soft clay until all the interstices are completely filled up both inside and out and then the ragged bits are carfully smothed [sic] off when dry it makes a tolerably good wall. *(Jun 5, 1840,Journal,226,1966)*  
> **HYDE** Swarming down from their lone-set mountain cabins,/ Tramping from cob and raupo, wattle and daub,/ *(1937,Selected Poems,44,1984)*  
> **BEATTIE** [The early runholder's home] ... might be of stone, clay, brick, earth, timber, canvas, ferntrees, or wattle-and-daub. *(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,20)*  
> **BEATTIE** [The early runholder's home] ... might be of stone, clay, brick, earth, timber, canvas, ferntrees, or wattle-and-daub. *(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,20)*  
> **SINCLAIR** ... many of the poorer people still loved in the tiny cottages built by the first settlers. These were constructed of wood, of cob (a mixture of clay and straw), or of `wattle and daub', that is, a frame of saplings filled in with puddled clay. *(1959,History of New Zealand,101)*  
> **SALMOND** <i>Wattle and daub</i>. walls made of wickerwork or woven <i>wattles </i>(thin branches) and plastered by hand with mud or clay thrown on both sides. *(1986,Glossary of Building Terminology,233)*  
> For poorer families the most common means of construction was known as "wattle and daub"; a framework of saplings coated with layers of puddle clay and grass cob, while the roof consisted of tussock grass or wooden shingles. *(Apr 1998,North & South,78)*  
> Large and handsome dwelling houses, surrounded in many instances by neat gardens, are re-placing the old "wattle and daub". *(Feb 8, 1872,Grey River Argus,2)*  
> He had built the walls of posts fi[x]ed in the ground with battens nailed on, and manuka scrub twisted in and out and clayed on the inside, known as wattle and daub. *(Nov 22, 1894,North Otago Times,1)*  
> The few houses erected were built of wattle and daub, manuka poles and fern scrub, and since every man had to be his own architect, the appearance of some of these houses, together with the wild surroundings, would no doubt have put misgivings into the minds of the immigrants if they had not been men and women of a sturdy character and not likely to be cast down at the thought of difficulties to be overcome. *(Nov 30, 1900,North Otago Times,5)*  
> Enthusiasts are solving the house-shortage problem by recommending mud houses ("pise"), "dobey," "adobe," "wattle," and "daub," or any other name you can think of for mud. *(Apr 3, 1920,Observer (Auckland),18)*  
> Some persons had brought out Manning's wooden houses, which were quickly erected;  other dwelt in tents, and others contracted with Maoris to run up houses of wattle and daub. *(Apr 9, 1940,Evening Post,8)*  
> The first houses were of wattle and dab;  the Maoris, paid at the rate of 3s a day, lending valuable aid. *(Mar 18, 1908,Otago Witness,93)*  
> Undaunted, Captain Ridley erected a tent on the bach made with the carpets he had brought from England, and here, he and his wife camped until he had built a house of wattle and daub (supplejacks and soft clay). *(Aug 2003,NZ Memories,No. 43/30)*

### wattlebird
  
noun. kokako or huia  
> ... the once silent woods now resound with the blows of the felling-axe ... the Wattle-bird is no longer to be found in its old haunts; it seeks shelter amongst the higher parts of the bushy gullies ... the condition of the forest is changing under the effects of ... constantly-recurring bush fires. ... The Kokako loving a moist temperature will probably soon entirely forsake its ancient places of resort. *(1874,TrNZI,6/146)* [NOTES: Potts]  
> Hastings Boys High School.. has taken on the challenge of restoring to life its school emblem, the huia wattlebird, extinct for nearly 70 years. *(May 20, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),26)*

### wattled crow
  
noun. [Environment] kokako  
> <i>wattle crow n </i>: either of two long-tailed slaty gray corvine birds (<i>Callaeas cinera</i> and <i>C. wilsoni</i>) of New Zealand having a brightly colored subcircular fleshy wattle on each side of the base of the lower mandible. *(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,2586)*  
> **FALLA et al.** KOKAKO <i>Callaeas cinerea Other names:</i> Wattled Crow, Organ-bird, Gill-bird, Bluegill (N.I.). *(1966,Birds of New Zealand,236)*

### watty
  
noun.

### watty
  
noun. [corruption of Ma. whata] a food store, raised from the ground on stilts.

noun.

noun.

### wawapaka
  
noun.

### wax
  
noun.  
> Sutton ..., who carried out the investigations, found it necessary first of all to devise a suitable method of measuring the amount [289] of suint (water extract) and wax (ether extract) produced by the sheep's skin month by month throughout the year under natural conditions in the paddock. *(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,288)*

### waxeye
  
noun. silver-eye  
[NOTES: aka wax-eye, wax eye, white eye, white-eye, silvereye, silver-eye, silver eye]  
> **FRAME** Kiddies, kiddies a waxeye had come to her to hide from the snow and find honey in her *(1960,Owls Do Cry,142)* [NOTES: US edition]  
> Three waxeyes quickly joined the bustle, followed by a fantail and a pair of yellowheads. *(Dec 9, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),47)*  
> Does Ginger give those waxeyes in you garden any bother? I remember you telling me about the waxeyes. *(Apr 3, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),13)*  
> **HUTTON** 14. ZOSTEROPS LATERALIS. Lath. White-eye. Wax-eye. Blight-bird. Tauhou. *(1871,Birds of New Zealand,6)*  
> ... [the] Wax-Eye (<i> Zosterops lateralis </i> ) being the usual foster-parents. *(1885,NZ Journal of Science,576)*  
> **BULLER** By the settlers it [sc <i> Zosterops </i> ] has been variously designated as Ring-eye, Wax-eye, White-eye, or Silver-eye, in allusion to the beautiful circlet of satiny-white feathers which surrounds the eyes; and quite as commonly as the `Blight-bird', or `Winter Migrant'. *(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/82)*  
> **MORRIS** <i> Wax-eye, n. </i> One of the many names for the bird called <i> Silver-Eye, White-Eye, Blight-Bird, </i> etc. See <i> Zosterops. </i> *(1898,Austral English,506)*  
> <i>Wax.-eye. </i>(w&abreve.ks.i.),<i> n. (Zoo.l.</i>) An Australian name for the <i> white-eye;</i>  - called also silver-eye, and, in New Zealand, <i> blightbird </i> . See WHITE-EYE, in the Dictionary. *(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2034)*  
> The honey-eaters are represented by the musical tui or parson-bird, the bell-bird, and the wax-eye - the last a comparatively recent immigrant from Australia. *(1900,Canterbury Old & New,195)*  
> Of all these creatures ... the grey duck and waxeye seem to be the only survivors, and that in greatly reduced numbers. *(1914,History of North Otago,43,1978)*  
> **WILLIAMS** <i> pihipihi </i> , n. 1.<i>  Zosterops caerulescens, wax-eye, blight-bird.</i> *(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,325)*  
> The tit (<i> Myiomoira </i> ), the fantail (<i> Rhipidura </i> ), the wax-eye (<i> Zosterops </i> ), ... also appear to be in no danger of extinction ... *(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/328)*  
> The wax-eye (<i> Zosterops lateralis </i> ), too, used to build in the bush on the Belt, and even in suburban gardens, and was decidedly more abundant than is the case now. *(1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/278)* [NOTES: (Bathgate, Otago)]  
> **GUTHRIE-SMITH** It was ideal country for birds, Bush Canaries, Tits (<i> Myiomoira macrocephala </i> ), Fantails (<i> Rhipidura fuliginosa </i> ), Grey Warblers <i> Gerygone igata </i> ), Waxeyes (<i> Zosterops lateralis </i> ), ... *(1936,NZ Naturalist,54)*  
> <i>tauhou </i> , a wandering bird (Zosterops halmaturina), blight-bird; twinkie; button-eye; wax-eye; ring-eye. Tauhou means stranger and this name was given by the Maori when the bird appeared, in 1856, in the central part of Newzealand, gradually spreading north and south till the islands were occupied. It is an Australian bird, and was blown across the Tasman sea in the year mentioned. *(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/`159)* [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]  
> wax-eye One of the names for the Australian bird also called `silver-eye' and `blight-bird'. (See <i> blight-bird.</i> ) *(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),440)*  
> **FALLA et al.** SILVEREYE <i> Zosterops lateralis Other names:</i>  Tauhou, Waxeye, White-eye, Blightbird, Blightie. *(1966,Birds of New Zealand,223)*  
> Mr CLIFFORD suggested the wax-eye as a bird which they were also desirous of procuring. *(Aug 2, 1867,Otago Daily Times,5)*  
> **NATUSCH** Family ZOSTEROPIDAE (silvereyes). <i> Zosterops lateralis </i>  ... , called ring-eye in the south, wax-eye, white-eye, silvereye, blightie, etc., elsewhere, is probably in actual fact a blown-away Australian. Not seen here before 1886, it is certainly plentiful ... *(1967,Animals of NZ, The,282)*  
> <i> waxeye n.</i>  a small New Zealand bird with a white circle around its eye. Also called <i> silver-eye, blighty. </i> *(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,1332)* [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 873; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 542]  
> **BRASCH** And rifled quickly by fantail, wax-eye, tui,/ In the shade of indifferent leaves./ *(1984,Collected Poems,53)*  
> **STUDHOL,ME** Waxeyes and the little grey warblers inhabit the garden, ... *(1985,Coldstream,xii)*  
> <i> Silvereye Zosterops lateralis lateralis </i> (LATHAM, 1801) ... OTHER NAMES: <i> White-eye, waxeye, blightbird, tauhou. </i> *(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,288)*  
> A fantail or a wax-eye probably tastes as good as one of the larger important fruit-eating birds, and all are equally easily caught at night when the owl is feeding. *(Jun 5, 1937,Evening Post,15)*

### waxweed
  
noun. <i>Hydrocotyle americana</i>  
> <i>waxweed [s] Hydrocotyle americana</i> L. *(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,85)*  
> **TAYLOR** WAXWEED (<i>Hydrocotyle americana</i>) ... Native to New Zealand and other countries, growing in lawns and fields, especially those mown or grazed short. *(1981,Weeds of Lawns,132)*

### way
  
noun. a log-way  
[NOTES: (sawmilling) usu. pl.]  
> **BAINES** Arrived at the mill, the logs were hauled up `ways' as required on to the mill-floor. Here they were rolled upon the carriage appertaining to the vertical saw. *(1874,Edward Crewe,178)*

### way the devil
  
phrase. expressing a strong sense of distance  
> **HUNT** And off we'd go, way the devil back into those hills *(1981,Speaking a Silence,18)*

### wayback
  
noun. a person from the wayback; a backblocker  
> She noticed a real "old way-back" eyeingher very interestingly. *(Oct 29, 1927,NZ Dairy Produce Exporter,40)*  
> We are certainly "waybacks" down here.but to sit listening at night toAddington market reports, etc., makes usfeel we are not so far out after all. *(Sep 30, 1927,NZ Dairy Produce Exporter,43)*

### wayback
  
noun. backblocks  
> When woolly whiskers of the Wayback goes into the township and is fined a quid for being drunk his boss never thinks of parting up for him. *(Sep 15, 1906,NZ Truth,65/3/6)*  
> [heading] `Wayback likes it' *(1910,Triad, The,18/5/47)*  
> **COOK** Some fifty miles in the `wayback'. *(1926,Far Flung,39)*  
> <i>wayback n.</i> the inland; one who comes from the back country. *(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),628)*

### wayback
  
adverb. in a place remote from the speaker's point of reference; ?an abbrev. of AWAY-BACK, and often used as a quasi-aj; occas has the form way out back: in NZ perhaps thought of as an Australianism, esp. <i>the wayback</i>, NZ idiom possibly preferring IN THE BACKBLOCKS, THE BOOHAI)  
[NOTES: aka way back, way-back]  
> **LANCASTER** He had saved his craven life at the undercut bluff when it parted the mob wayback in the dry creek. *(1904,Sons o' Men,30)*  
> **THORNTON** ... the condition of those living `wayback'. *(1916,Wowser, The,85)*  
> **BLAIR** I'm sure they're getting a storm wayback. *(1934,By Pacific Waters,12)*

### wayback
  
adverb.  
> **THOMPSON** Some stations way out back upon the hills. *(1909,Ballads about Business,43)*

### wayback
  
adjective. .  
[NOTES: includes away back, adj.  ?felt as an attrib use of n; ?or as an abbreviated for of an adverbial collocation AWAY BACK, spg<i> 'wayback</i>, with a frequent prefixed indefinite article helping to complete a morphological illusion (an away back > a wayback) ??]  
> Now, Sir, I hope they will show more judgment than we have apparently done, and not put their minister away back in the bush where some of his hearers does [sic] not know where to find him, and in winter time for mud can scarcely get to him. *(Jan 24, 1866,Southland Times,2)*  
> Away back in the ranges, after years of constant toil, we hear of the Alburnia, El Dorado, and other mines on Punga Flat, sending down large returns. *(Jun 23, 1871,Wanganui Herald,2)*  
> ... the `way-back' settler. *(1905,Red Funnel,2/491)*  
> At the wayback township of Mataroa ... *(Jan 6, 1906,NZ Truth,29/8/4)*  
> **BEATTIE** My great friend ... was `a white man'. His way back hotel was a home . *(1913,Trade Hunting,[16])*  
> There is a piquancy in the 'wayback race-meeting that one ... misses in the ... gatherings at Trentham ... *(Mar 10, 1920,Quick March,39)*  
> **HYDE** I liked the 'wayback articles signed `C.J.M.' every bit as much as the most highly-paid overseas `whimsicalities': ... *(1934,Journalese,26)*  
> **HISLOP** To use the expression of a wayback shed hand, it is real dinkum, especially if he saw me getting away with that awful of lambs' tails from the foot of the post in the yard. <i>Ibid.</i> 73 It was at one of these wayback stations one evening that generous old John ... said to me: `You can't go on the roads tonight, Jimmy.' *(1943,Pure Gold,35)*  
> **NEWTON** Lilybank is one of the real way-back stations. ... Fifty odd miles from Fairlie and often cut off by flooding rivers, it is one of Canterbury's most isolated stations... *(1952,High Country Journey,50)*  
> The other day a Southern paper gravely stated that the local 'cemetery' had been closed for the season.   This would lead people to infer at first that the inhabitants of that township were unusually healthy during the winter months, but it now appears that it was 'creamery' the wayback editor meant. *(May 18, 1895,Auckland  Star,10)*

### wayback
  
adverb. (in) the remote hinterland  
[NOTES: see also away back.   as a n and av, an infrequent variant (?elaboration) of WAY-BACK(S)]  
> It was away back where roads were unknown, ... and where a few hundred sheep annually evaded the shearing muster, and remained among the scrub and flax bushes to develop into `double-fleecers'. *(Apr 1904,NZ Illustrated Magazine,48)*  
> **THOMSON** Bristled faces, unshaven for weeks, and hair unkempt told they had come from a spell away back. *(1905,Bush Boys of NZ,183)*  
> **AYSON** My flock was the dry flock wethers. One day I got notice to ... put my flock of 3,000 sheep away back on the hill country of `Lochindorb', near the head of the Owaka river. It was very much infested with wild dogs and wild pigs, ... but it was very good sheep country. *(1937,Pioneering in Otago,94)*

### wayback
  
adjective. [of land, buildings, settlements etc] isolated.  
> NZ Farmer Jan 1. 132 An elderly lady I knew stayed one night in a tiny wayback hotel ... *(1929)*  
> WILSON & FRASER Book of the Pioneers 67 He had been for years mining on the way-back runs ... *(1936)*  
> NEWTON Big Country of the South Island 111 .. St James must surely be counted as the real way-back station of this island. *(1973)*  
> ibid. 230 One of the real way-back stations, it takes in some of the roughest sheep country in the island ... *(1973)*  
> ECCLESTON Irish Kiwi 96 The usual procedure, on the appointed Saturday morning, was for all those who wished to avail themselves of the services of this 'way-back' barber, to line up on the seat that ran outside the cookhouse and wait their turn. His visit came as a surprise, for he seldom came down from his isolated home in the 'wayback' district more frequently than twice a year. [way-back] *(1977)*

### waybacker
  
noun.  
[NOTES: aka way back; person from the wayback (way back, way-back, away back away-back)]  
> The Mens' [sic] Doubles, on heresay [sic], is a foregone conclusion for the "shorties," who have both done good balcony work, with a couple o waybacks" to upset things. *(Jun 19, 1902,Taranaki Herald,3)*  
> The waybackers had an eventful time at the Commonwealth. *(Feb 2, 1901,Observer (Auckland),15)*  
> When Jack Chase, who was custodian of the Opera House for many years, went forth in his caravans with the little Co., which includes some members of his family, to supply fun and frolic to the waybackers, there were not a few in the profesh. who predicted that Chase's tour would be a wild-goose chase, but, on the contrary, Jack chased and caught the goose we are all after - the goose that lays the golden googie. *(May 22, 1915,NZ Truth,2)*  
> [As Non-de-plumes] Mrs Waybacker... PollyWaybacker. *(Jun 30, 1926,NZ Dairy Produce Exporter,34)* [NOTES: (nb: date originally entered in the database as 31/6/1926)]

### waybackness
  
noun. the isolation of a farming area.  
> WATERS Mostly a Farmer 94 She was quite a girl and hated the waybackness of Tutaki but saw it out for some time. *(1976)*

### waybacks
  
noun. an isolated farming area, such as backblocks.  
> The delight of the children in the far 'waybacks" in the novelty of a motor-car invading their isolation is well shown.. *(Oct 30, 1926,NZ Dairy Exporter,1)*  
> MCKENZIE Wakatipu Pioneers 112 Roland Matheson will never know the heaven-sent boon dried milk was to the people working under difficulties in the way-backs. *(1951)*  
> TAIT Farms & Stations of NZ II 221 From a rough undeveloped area in the 'way backs' this property has proved to be abundantly fertile. *(1958)*  
> SINCLAIR Boot in the Stirrup 107 It could be of interest to the urban dweller that those of the waybacks have quite a large bundle of contempt for the town-dweller. *(1973)*  
> ECCLESTON Irish Kiwi 92 Everyone was happy, and the outcome of it all, had no effect at all on a friendship that was to last through the years, one that means so much to the teacher in the way-backs. *(1977)*  
> I can't claim to be a female pioneer from the waybacks, or a fern fighter from Te Kuiti. *(Oct 31, 1926,NZ Dairy Produce Exporter,41)*  
> My husband has had to be my family, my friend, my husband and everything a man can be to a woman in the waybacks. *(Feb 26, 1927,NZ Dairy Produce Exporter,35)*

### wayleggo
  
exclamation. [an alteration of 'come away here, let go'] a conventional command given to a working dog to heel or return to the master for further instructions.  
> NEWTON Wayleggo 11 'Wayleggo'! A strange word, its origin is somewhat obscure. *(1947)*  
> MCLEOD NZ High Country 20 Every now and then a man will shout 'get in behind, Dark,' 'come out o' that, Ben' or 'Wareleggo, Maid.' *(1951)*  
> STAPLES Cooks and Shepherds Come Away 25 It (Wayleggo) was a corruption of 'Come away here, let go' and was sometimes written as 'way'le'go'. *(1964)*  
> Southland Times June 14. 18 'Wayleggo' is easier to say and saves breath - something a shepherd walking the hills needs as he is mustering sheep. *(1980)*  
> LOUGHNAN Glossary 90 wayleggo: which really is a distortion of: 'come away, let go,' the final command to call a dog to heel at the end of his run. *(1981)*  
> J of Ag Jan/Feb 14 Wayleggo is the traditional call off, meaning 'let go and come away in behind me.' *(1984)*  
> GORDON Three Sheep and a Dog 22 Then there's the great New Zealand shepherd's cry, 'Wayleggo'. [wareleggo] *(1998)*  
> **NEWTON** `Wayleggo!' A strange word, its origin is somewhat obscure. It is a command a shepherd gives to call his dog off sheep, and is presumably an abbreviation of `come away and let them go'. *(1947,Wayleggo,11)*  
> **WOODHOUSE** I love the clean brown tussock/ And the hills where the cool winds blow./ It is my prayer/ I may still be there/ When the Lord calls 'Way're le' go,/ [fn] The shepherd command to a dog who has completed the job: `Away here. Let go.'/ Wareleggo is the musterers' call to a dog to come back and I believe it's supposed to be a sort of portmanteaux [sic] word made up of `Way-here-let-go,' meaning let go the sheep and come away here. Rather far-fetched perhaps but I know of no other explanation. The word is so characteristic that Peter Newton - the author of two books by a high-country musterer about high-country life - used it as a title to epitomise the spirit of his first book. *(1950,Farm & Station Verse,195)* [NOTES: 1950 Anon in 1950 Woodhouse Farm and Station Verse 195]  
> **McLEOD** Every now and then G man will shout `get in behind, Dark', `come out o' that, Ben' or `Wareleggo Maid'. *(1951,High Country,20)* [NOTES: 1951 McLeod High Country 431952 McLeod NZ High Country 20]  
> **MASTERS** ... on some distant day perhaps the lumberman's call of `timber,' will echo round the rugged valley, as once did the musterer's `wayleggo!' ... *(1960,Back-Country Tales,63)*  
> **DICK** Life had been tame for the dogs for many miles, so, heedless of their master's cries to `Wayleggo there, Tip', `Get in behind, Jock', `Siddown, Ben, siddown you brute', they rushed madly hither and thither, ... *(1964,High Country Family,64)*  
> <i> wayleggo exc.</i> command by shepherd to call dog to heel, particularly in S. Is. *(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),21)*  
> **CHARLES** There's my mate just down below,/ I can hear his `Where-a-go!'*/I can hear his kelpie bark/ ...[fn] *Musterer's command to his dog - `Away here, let go!' *(1981,Black Billy Tea,35)*  
> ) It is music in the chill mountain dawn to hear the musterer start his dogs on their long, hard day's work with a lusty cry that sounds `Awailago", with all the cadence on the second `a". One man told me this was Gaelic, but when I asked a Scot he said it was more likely to be a corruption of an original exhortation such as `Away and let her go." *(1945,NZ Geographer,38)* [NOTES: Pascoe `High Country"]  
>  *(Feb 20, 2010,Your Weekend)*  
>  *(Feb 20, 2010,Your Weekend)*  
> "This muster will be my last wayleggo after 58 years in the balley." says Brian. "Nothing lasts forever out here." *(Feb 20, 2010,Your Weekend,8)*

### wayside house
  
noun.  
> The location of this pioneer wayside house is still marked by a cluster of old trees near the Pareora River traffic bridge. *(May 1996,NZ Historic Places,58/10)*

### Wazza, the
  
noun.  
[NOTES: (various spellings) esp. battle of the Wazza April 3 1915]  
> Last month saw the fourth anniversary of the `Wassa' Riot ... Troops took charge of that port of Cairo known as the `Wassa' and the `Bull Ring' ... *(May 10, 1919,Quick March,23)*  
> **FRASER & GIBBONS** <i>WOZZER, THE: </i>The name among the Australians in Egypt for the street `Haret el Wazza' in Cairo, a low neighbourhood. Two encounters there in 1915 between police and native mobs were given the name of the `Battle of the Wozzer'. *(1925,Soldier and Sailor Words,310)*  
> **TREADWELL** At last the [VD] situation became so bad that the men took into their own hands the task of cleaning up the [Cairo] brothel area, which consisted of one principal street and some minor ones, comprehensively called the Wasser. ... [48] The two raids, popularly known as the `Battles of the Wasser', were conducted with the object of preventing the continued misuse of the premises attacked. *(1936,Recollections of an Amateur Soldier,47)*  
> **HYDE** When they struck Cairo, most of the men from Zitoun camp headed straight for the Wazza. <i>Ibid.</i> 102 Starkie stuck to Rada [an Egyptian prostitute] until after the battle of the Wazza. *(1936,Passport to Hell,99)*  
> **KING** Colonel Malone noted the incident cryptically in his diary: April3. Riot in Cairo last night. Some of the Australians and New Zealanders wrecked some brothels where they had been badly treated. If they had burnt the quarter down it would have ben a good thing. This was the so-called `Battle of the Wazza', named for the street in which the attack took place: the Haret el Wasser near Shepheards Hotel. In this quarter on Good Friday 1915, several hundred Australians and New Zealanders sacked and set fire to the brothels while thousands more watched. *(1981,New Zealanders at War,41)*  
> In 1915 the New Zealanders celebrated Good Friday here by wrecking brothels where they felt they had been treated badly, in what became known as `the battle of the Wazza.' *(Oct 4, 1982,Evening Post,41)*  
> **SHADBOLT** On the second of April [1915] there [at Cairo] the New Zealanders had their first battle, the riot in the brothel area called the Wazzir, with the English shooting down four New Zealanders and Australians. *(1988,Voices of Gallipoli,35)*  
> Again the Wazza - Cairo's underworld of depravity - was the scene of the disturbance, which, fortunately, was checked before any serious damage was done. *(Mar 2, 1916,Marlborough Express,5)*

### WD
  
abbreviation. a female police officer  
> There was a time when male police officers referred to women officers as 'WDs', from the time of the Women's Division in the 1970s. *(Jul 24, 2002,Capital Times,17)*

### we don't know how lucky we are
  
phrase.  
> Thirty years ago, entertainer John Clarke - breaking new ground in television satire as archetypal Kiwi joker Fred Dagg - insisted New Zealanders didn't know how lucky they were. *(Dec 20, 2003,Dominion Post,C1)*  
> We have wool in our country and so often we don't use it. We just don't know how lucky we are. *(May 6, 2001,Sunday Star Times,A4)*  
> As one thirty-something put it, quoting archetypal Kiwi bloke Fred Dagg: "We don't know how lucky we are, mate." *(Apr 28, 2001,Dominion,28)*

### we won, you lost, eat that
  
phrase.  
> To paraphrase our own Michael Cullen: "They won, you lost - get over it." *(Feb 7, 2006,Dominion Post,B4)*  
> Never mind "We won, you lost, eat that!" Now it's, "We only just won, you only just lost, and we've all lost our appetites" *(Aug 1, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),23)*  
> As another famous Pom, also our Minister of Finance, once said, "We won. You lost. Eat that." *(Jul 16, 2005,Internet - see Notes,July 16-22 2005 Vol 199 No 3401)* [NOTES: http://listener.co.nz/default,4317.sm]  
> Michael Cullen: [..Nine years ago, when Helen Clark was absent on overseas leave, I rose in this House on the second reading of the Employment Contracts Bill and committed the Labour Party to repealing the Employment Contracts Act. And we will repeal the Employments Contracts Act. Eat that! You lost, we won, it goes! It was as simple as that.] *(Aug 9, 2000,NZ Parliamentary Debates,586/4570)*

abbreviation. Workers' Educational Association

phrase.

### wean-by date
  
noun. the date by which milk-fed lambs are separated from their ewe mothers  
> When you're dealing with a hogget with a lamb at foot, you can't afford to worry about lamb weights and a wean-by date. *(Oct 2004,Country-Wide,26/3,10)*

### weaner
  
noun. [now used elsewhere, but first recorded in NZ.] a calf, piglet or lamb that has been weaned in the current year.  
> **MALCOLM** Gilbert once bought 26 head of black polled weaner calves for one shilling and six pence a head. *(1982,Where it all Began,177)*  
> Puketoi Station Diary May 2 He has been told .. that a small mob of weaners are just to the right of the dray spur on the rough ridge. *(1861)*  
> MURISON Puketoi Station Diary Sept 13 Sent Wilkinson to Drysdales to head weaners whilst Read searches for stragglers. *(1865)*  
> NZ Farmer Aug 16. 43 Slips and weaners have been in demand. *(1951)*  
> FARAM Hutchinson of Parihohonu 32 .. Dad promptly bought another 90 weaners at the fair. *(1987)*  
> Manawatu Evening Standard May 14. 3 Mr Orr says vendors are feeling positive about the sale, following on from early autumn weaner fair prices ... *(2002)*  
> SALISBURY I Was So Lucky 115 By the time I had broken in the horses the annual weaner fairs were fast approaching. *(1994)*  
> Quiet hand reared beef cross weaner Heifers and Bulls. *(Feb 17, 2001,Hawke's Bay Today,19)*  
> Several well-bred sows with pigs, weaners. *(Mar 7, 1860,Lyttelton Times,5)*  
> W. ROSE will sell by public auction .. 14 good weaner steers ... *(Apr 8, 1902,Bush Advocate,3)*  
> Fat ewes 27s, 30s, 31s .. mixed weaners 29s 6d, 35s, to aGBP2 5s 6d, weaner steers 33s, good weaner heifers aGBP2 6s ... *(May 27, 1916,Evening Post,9)*  
> Weaners sold at a slightly improved tone, but otherwise late rates prevailed. *(21/0/5,Hawera & Normanby Star,4)*  
> 30 Pigs, Stores, Slips and Weaners. *(Nov 28, 1945,Rodney and Otamatea Times,2)*  
> Pigs - 174 yarded, principally porkers, weaners and a few pens of heavyweight bacon pigs and slips. *(Dec 18, 1890,Otago Witness,18)*  
> looked for lambs but could find no tracks. ... he has been told ... that a small mob of weaners are just to the right of the dray spur on the rough ridge. *(May 2, 1861,Puketoi Station Diary)*  
> Drafted our Ewes and E. weaners ... turned about 1100 Ewe weaners back on the run. [AND sheep and calves : 1865] *(Mar 10, 1878,Chudleigh Diary,267,1950)*  
> **Mrs PRAED** cf weanling compare <i>yearling </i> *(1881,Boards, Blades & Barebellies)* [NOTES: 1881 Mrs PRAED 1890 Boldrewood]  
> Mustered lamb weaners. Found 26 drowned in a little creek and a large number nocked up. [check spg???] *(May 14, 1891,Chudleigh Diary,375,1950)*  
> **TURNER** (Dannevirke) Calves from weaning until about one year old were called weaners; after about a year they become yearlings. *(1931,Diary)*  
> <i>Weaner</i>.-Lambs, calves, and pigs are sometimes so called when they are lately weaned. *(Jan 27, 1934,Press, The,15)*  
> **LEVY** ... [pastures] kept well in hand by the use of sheep or weaner calves or of both, ... *(1951,Grasslands of NZ,231,1970)*  
> <i>weaner n.</i> young lamb weaned from its mother. *(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),628)*  
> When the calves are no longer fed ... they are called <i>weaners</i> till the next batch comes along, then <i>yearlings, springers, heifers</i>n they first calve at two years ... *(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,150)*  
> <i> weaner ... n.</i> young lamb weaned from its mother. *(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),21)*

### weaner
  
noun.

### weaning muster
  
noun. [Rural] a gathering together of mobs of ewes and lambs, in order to wean or separate some.

> **NEWTON** It so happens that his gang is out at camp on the weaning muster at present ... *(1972,Sheep Thief,110)*  
> **KERR** The next muster was the weaning muster. *(2000,High Times in the High Country,163)*

### weaning paddock
  
noun. a fenced area where newly-weaned lambs are grazed.

> Southland Times Nov 8. 10 Weaning paddocks should be prepared in advance for the lambs. *(2001)*  
> In the Paddock Nov 2 Weaning paddocks should be prepared. *(2001)*

phrase. be an All Black.

phrase. be or become an All Black (or other NZ representative sportsperson)

> **ROMANOS** 'In this country, rugby transcends sport and our All Blacks are young gods... You must wear the 'silver fern'. *(1997,Black,50)*

### wearable art
  
noun.  
> A definition as quoted by Susie Moncrief, creator of world of wearable art, is that wearable art is a piece of art work created not to be hung on a wall but to adorn the body. With wearable art the body becomes a hangar for a sculptured work of art this is not a static display but one that moves with the body to capture the characteristics of the creation. *(Oct 6, 2001,Evening Post,10)*  
> Nelson and the Montana New Zealand Wearable Art Awards have gained a prime spot in German magazine GEO special. *(Aug 25, 1999,Evening Post,18)*  
> Thirteen years ago, Suzie Moncrieff.. came up with  the idea of a wearable art show to promote a country art gallery. *(Sep 2000,North & South,105)*  
> I have this vision of a world wearable arts competition... Imagine all this mix of races and people on an international stage competing in wearable art in a huge, joyful, exciting, colourful event. *(Oct 9, 1999,Dominion,20)*  
> I was told by this grey little man in walk shorts that there wouldn't be any future in this wearable arts stuff. *(Oct 16, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30)*  
> This racy read takes in drug-dealing shenanigans in Queenstown and backstage doings at the Nelson Wearable Arts Show... *(May 31, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56)*  
> Wearable art is all about designing memorable adornment for the body. *(Oct 9, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),38)*  
> [Hdg] Nelson reels at loss of wearable arts. Nelson has been rocked by the loss of the $6.5 million World of Wearable Art Awards, but Wellington is welcoming the WOW factor. *(Jun 25, 2004,Dominion Post,A3)*  
> The wearable arts costumes designed and made by the pupils were part of a project which combined parts of both the technology and arts curriculum. The costumes were made completely from recycled materials. *(Jul 14, 2000,NZ Education Review,1)*  
> "Wearable Art is a catwalk show ... in effect a fashion show, and they don't prove to be huge hits with audiences."... Wearable Art has previously screened on networks around the world, including CNN, BBC and ABC in Australia, as well as gaining television coverage in Thailand, Singapore and Sweden. *(Aug 4, 2000,Evening Post,14)*  
> In Berlin, the judges.. were after models that stood out, were unique and original but not to the point of being wearable art. *(Aug 13, 2000,Sunday Star Times,D5)*

### weasel
  
noun. sly or devious person

> She said that she `cannot put it down to anything other than NZUSA's being full of weasels'. (Student politico slang for members of the Workers Communist League). *(Apr 22, 1985,Salient,7)*

### weasel piss
  
noun. weak tea, beer; cider  
> <i>weasel piss:</i> weak tea or beer *(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use,2)* [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]  
> [He] remembers the day he was able to waltz into the local working man's pub, order a cider and sit down for a drink without being teased. The "weasel piss" had come of age.... Picture this... [He] walks in, tried to flog his goods to the barman and gets told, "Why would we want to sell that weasel piss?" *(Dec 19, 2004,Sunday Star Times,D1)*

### weather bar
  
noun.  
> <i>Weather bar</i>- A strip of corrosion-resistant metal fitted into a groove in the underside of a wooden sill and fitting into a corresponding groove in a stone sill, or bedded into a concrete sill. Usually set in music. *(1979,SANZ Glossary Building Terminology,129)*

### weather bomb
  
noun. a complex deep depression giving rise to a violent storm with heavy rain and strong winds  
[NOTES: From www.logophilia.com/WordSpy/weatherbomb. noun. A massive and powerful storm that develops quickly and  without warning.  The Toronto Star, August 12, 2001 -"Suit Blames Deaths of Three Fishermen on Faulty Weather Forecast," The Associated Press, May 11, 1984 According to Augie Auer, NatRad, 26/6/2002, the term was probably invented by Norwegian meteorologists in the 1960s.]  
> All of us have a few weather terms we can bandy around. Weather bombs, El Nino, La Nina, lows and highs, cyclones, anti-cyclones, hurricanes (go the canes!) and now yo yo patterns. *(Feb 4, 2004,Capital Times,4)*  
> The cryptic world of weather forecasts has been congested further with: weather bomb.... MetService forecaster Shane Bidois said.. " I think Bob McDavitt used it in a press release... Mr McDavitt said it was when a low-pressure system lost one millibar of pressure every hour for 24 hours. "I was discussing with a colleague what to say and he suggested 'a particularly nasty low' ... but it was turning into a bomb and we wanted as many people as possible to brace themselves so we came up with the idea of calling it a 'bomb'," Mr McDavitt said. *(Jun 22, 2002,Dominion,3)*  
> Question of the week: Who thought up the great term "Weather bomb"" and why aren't they working for Saatchi and Saatchi... Rain and warmth erode a fine snow base, while a Friday opening at Turoa is put off by the "weather bomb" heading soputh. *(Jun 22, 2002,Evening Post,18)*  
> The rain and wind that hit Wellington early today is a false alarm, MetService says - the "weather bomb" is due this afternoon and evening... MetService's Bob McDavitt said a "weather bomb" occurred when a low pressure system lost one millibar of pressure every hour over 24 hours. *(Jun 21, 2002,Evening Post,3)*  
> [Niwa's] meteorologist Georgina Griffiths reported that "weather bombs" - a term for an explosive cyclogenesis, or rapidly deepening low - have been around for years. In 1984, a lawyer used the term in a fishing disaster suit... Quite where the term originated is not known... *(Jun 23, 2002,Sunday Star Times,A6)*  
> [Caption] Flooding in Manawatu in February was called a 100-year event, but so was the "weather bomb" in the Coromandel last year. *(Apr 15, 2004,Dominion Post,C2)*  
> Since a weather bomb two years ago, the Council have been working on a plan for such emergencies. *(Apr 19, 2004,Radio NZ / National Radio)*  
> I was last here with my family nearly 20 years ago and what a difference a "weather bomb" can make. *(Nov 20, 2005,Sunday Star Times,Escape 12)*  
> "Weather bombs that have hit New Zealand in the last 20 years should have provided a strategic plan to deal with weather events", he said. *(Jul 21, 2004,Dominion Post,A3)*  
> I could write about the weatherbomb - the new meteorological lingo for storm - that's bearing down upon Wellington. *(Jun 30, 2002,NZ Catholic,6)*  
> The term "weather bomb", used in June of this year by forecasters in relation to a rapidly intensifying storm that was approaching New Zealand, struck a chord with the media, which trumpeted the warnings so loudly that few people would have missed the message that there was serious trouble on the way. <i>Ibid. </i> 15 The use of the phrase "weather bomb" to describe the storm excited a certain amount of controversy... In fact, the term "bomb' has been used in North America for more than 20 years to describe rapidly intensifying lows, some of which have ben known to deepen by 60 hPa in 24 hours off the  east coast of the United States. One definition of a bomb is a low that deepens at a rate of 24 hPa in 24 hours at latitude 60a. When adjusted to the latitude of Auckland to allow for the effects caused by the curvature of the earth's surface, this is the equivalent of 17 hPa in 24 hours. *(Sep 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 59/14)*  
> A violent storm, described as a weather bomb, is causing havoc as it moves down the North island. *(Jun 21, 2002,Radio NZ / National Radio)* [NOTES: Midday news]

### weather groove
  
noun.  
> <i>Weather groove</i>- Groove in two adjacent surfaces of a sash or door and the frame to prevent the ingress of water. *(1979,SANZ Glossary Building Terminology,129)*

### weather side
  
noun. the outer side of a shorn sheep fleece.

### weatherboard
  
adjective.  
[NOTES: ?weatherboard to NZ as corrugated/galvanised iron is to Australia]  
> There is an excellent WeatherboardDwelling House. *(Mar 5, 1864,Otago Witness,24)*  
> The outward appearance of New Zealand's country pubs.. Is always the same - two storeys tall; oblong in shape; constructed of either kauri or rimu weatherboard, with a ridged roof of corrugated iron. *(Nov 1999,AA Directions,34)*  
> The Mount John homestead was a weatherboard cottage situated below the crest of the hill high above the river. *(Feb 2004,NZ Memories,No. 46/51)*  
> Mr. Taylor has already discarded the long low building by the creek for a fine weatherboard residence on the higher ground. *(Mar 8, 1873,Auckland Weekly News,4)*  
> The red tin roofs topping the weather board walls, the one straight street running between, and the few shops, did not seem to have acquired either roots or character. *(May 26, 1955,Dominion,9)*  
> **YATE** A two-story weather-board house *(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),173)* [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]  
> The chief took us to his cottage, a weather-board hut, and offered us rum ... *(Jun 1840,GBPP,107)* [NOTES: 1840 June 28 Thomas Bunbury's Report in GBPP House of Commons 1841 (No.311) 107]  
> **W.G.PUCKEY** `Our chief, Noble, has become quite a European in his habits. He has a neat little weatherboard cottage, which is furnished with tables and chairs like our own; and his food much the same. *(Jun 12, 1840,Letter)* [NOTES: 1840 June 12 W.G.Puckey letter in 1940 Matthews Matthews of Kaitaia 135]  
> There is a substantial weather-board house, stockyard, etc., ... *(Feb 2, 1861,Otago Witness,4)*  
> **COOPER** They have built a convenient weather-board house on their claim, ... *(Nov 3, 1867,Digger's Diary,9,1978)*  
> **MEREDITH** Being a weather-board house, the walls are not sound-proof. *(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,48,1935)*  
> He had seen weatherboard shanties and slab huts replace gumdiggers' tents and Maori whares *(Nov 1903,NZ Illustrated Magazine,108)*  
> **COWAN** Follow the stores-buying captain ... into one of the weatherboard trading-houses, ... thick with the tang of tarred rope. *(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/10)*  
> **BEATTIE** It was a weatherboard residence with two rooms below and two above. *(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,69)*  
> Horse and Weatherboard Nails, &c. *(Sep 13, 1851,Lyttelton Times,9)*  
> When the korero was going on the wife of the alleged aggressor threatened to burn down the house on the land in dispute - weatherboard house of four or five rooms - and had actually set fire to it, but Mr Mackay interfered and had it put out before any damage was done. *(Nov 29, 1882,Daily Telegraph,3)*  
> The house was a weatherboard structure of six or seven rooms, and it was owned by Mr M. J. Goodson, of Hawera, Taranaki. *(May 4, 1915,Northern Advocate,5)*  
> The bullet went through a paling fence, the wire front of a birdcase, the three-ply back of the cage, and then through the weatherboard side of the house. *(Sep 24, 1945,Evening Post,7)*  
> We need to know more about the machining skills of making early weatherboards and windows. *(Mar 17, 1993,Evening Post,7)*

### weatherboard
  
verb.  
> Some [houses] are merely covered with grass, others built with clay, others weather boarded, ... and for slates we have shingles, or a sort of lath one foot long, 4 inches broad, 1/4-inch thick; ... *(Apr 16, 1849,Letters from Otago,31,1978)*  
> ... the outside walls being weather-boarded and painted white *(1867,Rambles with a Philosopher,40)* [NOTES: Griffiths Collection]

### weatherboard
  
noun. a thin plank of wood used in overlapping rows to cover the outside of a house.  
[NOTES: [p.211 1971 Smithyman fn 11. Weatherboard. Although originally metropolitan, in the nineteenth century this seems to have become predominantly colonial, of boards laid horizontally, nailed with overlapping edges. Examples in the <i>Oxford English Dictionary</i> are almost wholly Australian.]  
> ...the doorway gives a clue to the almost-concealed entrance - a door cut out of the weatherboards. *(Aug 20, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20)*  
> **HOCKEN** We found them [the Deans family] living in an excellent weather-board verandah house, with large and substantial out-buildings, ... *(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,236)* [NOTES: 1844 Monro Notes of a Journey (<i>Nelson Examiner</i> July 20 et seq.) in 1898 Hocken Contributions 236]  
> <i>weatherboarded </i>appeared. In the form <i>weatherboard</i> (adjective and noun) it was destined to be much commoner in a land of wooden bungalows than in England. *(Feb 14, 1851,Lyttelton Times)* [NOTES: 1851 (?)Feb 14-15 Lyttelton Times in 1966 Turner 165]  
> **BELL** ... the slab whare [e-acute] will have given place to the weatherboard cottage, garden and [3] orchard will now be visible, and the gum-tree of Australia ... will be preparing to take the place of their dispossessed ... brethren of New Zealand ... *(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,2)*  
> **SATCHELL** The `Scarlet Man' stood alone in the solitude, a two-storeyed, weatherboard building, somewhat disreputable in appearance. *(1902,Land of the Lost,3,1971)*  
> **PFAFF** I used to get 2s.6d for carrying three weather-boards or three pieces of 3 by 2 from the saw pit to the township. *(1914,Diggers' Story, The,56)*  
> **SLATTER** They thumped on the weatherboards and made faces in the window. *(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,14)*  
> **WILLIAMS** `Well. You remind me of old Ma Palmer, mate, when I was replacing a few weatherboards on her dunny. Wasn't worth all the aggravation in the end.' *(1979,Skin Deep,30)*

### weather-boarded
  
adjective.  
> There are one and two storey weather-boarded houses, square and bell-shaped tents, T houses and V huts. *(Oct 21, 1934,Nelson Examiner,4)*  
> **A. WAKEFIELD** One private weather-boarded house and 60 warres. *(1841,Letter,41)* [NOTES: 1841 A. Wakefield to Edward Wakefield 41]  
> FOR SALE A WEATHER-BOARDED HOUSE, complete, with Windows and Door. *(Feb 15, 1851,Lyttelton Times,1)*  
> **SHORTLAND** On our arrival, the only quarters we could procure were two rooms of a weather-boarded house, ... The architect had probably been a ship's carpenter, for he had fitted up one of the rooms with tiers of [9] sleeping places of the form called by sailors bunks. <i>Ibid.</i> 128 Mr. Hughes had a very comfortable weatherboarded house, and a large barn with a thrashing-floor: ... *(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,8)*  
> **ROCHFORT** The town [of Christchurch] lay in a hollow ...; the streets were generally well laid out; and the snug little weather-boarded houses, (with chimneys made out of flour-barrels,) each standing in the midst of a neat little English garden, formed a delightful contrast to the wild hills around. *(1853,Adventures of a Surveyor,13)*

### wee
  
adjective. little (originally esp. in Otago and Southland, but now general)  
[NOTES: shared with and deriving from Scots English]  
> **DEVANNY** ... the fewer brown skins of the Maori race and here and there a wee half-caste. *(1929,Riven,127)*  
> **HISLOP** I took a ticket myself (2/6) in the name of a wee niece at West Taieri and to my surprise ... I was told she had won it. <i>Ibid.</i> 20 There are now several bonnie burns coming down the mountain sides to the river, the largest of which are the famous Roaring Meg and Gentle Annie; and a visitor ... may wash his colours of gold from almost anywhere while his wee wife boils the billy. *(1943,Pure Gold,16)*  
> **GASKELL** [Gaskell is from Invercargill] `It's a nice wee spot,' I said. `Very quiet, ... Isn't it crook about Keith and Gordon? ...' *(1978,All Part of the Game,43)* [NOTES: Gaskell `Tidings of Joy']  
> **GASKELL** It's a corker wee place,' I said. *(1947,Big Game,30)*  
> There were the wee jockeys leading in the horses, ... *(1984,Some Other Country,91)* [NOTES: 1950 Gaskell in 1984 Some Other Country 91]  
> **WHITWORTH** ... looked after me when I was wee. *(1950,Otago Interval,9)*  
> **SLATTER** Not that he was a hangman all the time. You should've seen him nursing the wee Ite babies and nattering to the pleased mommas. [check spg of Ite???] *(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,134)*  
> **HARVEY** `I'm just playing a wee game.' *(1964,Any Old Dollars Mister?,24)*  
> **McLINTOCK** Under the heading of vocabulary may be included the preferences shown for certain English words in ordinary use such as <i>village</i> for the shopping centre in suburbs and the very frequent use of the playful <i>wee</i> for `little' as in `a wee while' for `a little time' or `a short time'. *(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/679)* [NOTES: Wall NZ Speech]  
> **ARMFELT** ... they drove to Ray's wee home in Elgin. ... [242] Melva gave birth to a lovely wee daughter named Dawn. *(1971,Catching Up,238)*  
> **SARGESON** And in case of readers who might say that last word [`husbandry'] is archaic I would remind that I was now in a [Southland] countryside where there were wee boys, along the road, and two of them were sailing their wee boats on a wayside pond. Also I saw nothing so ordinary as a cowshed, no indeed, instead there was Romance: byres. *(1977,Never Enough!,76)*  
> **LELAND** <i>wee</i>: New Zealand has a Scots heritage and a wee anything is a SMALL one. *(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,111)*  
> **McGILL** ... Alan didn't have a bad life in Dunedin, probably because a good deal of it was out the point, at Puratanu. `I've got a wee crib there. Get over as often as I can. ... I've always liked the old shellfish, the gulls and herons. *(1985,G'day Country,171)*  
> <i>A cut lunch</i> (packed lunch) is handy to have with you, so if you feel <i>peckish</i> (hungry), you can pull over for a <i>wee</i> (small) picnic. *(Jan 14, 1987,NZ Woman's Weekly)* [NOTES: 1986 B.G. Ostmann U.S. food-writer's written remarks: p.c.Tui Flower NZ Woman's Weekly (14 Jan 1987)]  
> **SCANLAN** Perhaps it's a wee bit exaggerated. *(1933,Tides of Youth,91)*  
> **TURNER** In the South of New Zealand <i>a wee bit</i> would be added, a phrase extending north of the isoglosses for most Scotticisms into Canterbury. *(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,126)*  
> **McLINTOCK** Under the heading of vocabulary may be included the preferences shown for certain English words in ordinary use such as <i>village</i> for the shopping centre in suburbs and the very frequent use of the playful <i>wee</i> for `little' as in `a wee while' for `a little time' or `a short time'. *(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/679)* [NOTES: Wall NZ Speech]  
> **LUSH** ... I went back and found a very thin and very delicate but interesting looking young woman with a wee little baby in her arms - *(Jul 8, 1866,Waikato Journals,88,1982)*  
> This is what I call poverty that can absorb our "wee bit collection." *(Feb 17, 1880,Colonist (Nelson),3)*

### weedeater
  
noun. a line-trimmer

Jim Cameron writes: The formal word is <i>line-trimmer</i>, which I am told is also used in England, although not recorded in NSOD or Collins. <i>Weedeater</i> is proprietary but seems to be the usual informal generic name in NZ (cf customwood, gladwrap). The Australian equivalent is I believe <i>whipper-snipper</i>.]  
> **BURDON** It also held a few goats, God's early answer to the latter-day weedeater. *(1996,Hunting for DOC,19)* [NOTES: check quote]  
> The dog is licensing the tale to move at weedeater speed as often as it likes. *(Dec 16, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15)*

> **NATUSCH** [Caption] Fig. 72: BLENNIES OR COCKABULLIES. ... 3. Cockabully, <i>Forsterygion</i>. 4. Twister, <i>Helcogramma</i>. 5. Crested weedfish, <i>Notoclinus.</i> *(1967,Animals of NZ, The,228)*

The crested weedfish, Cristiceps aurantiacus, <i>is a blunt-headed, thin and tapering blenny-like fish, blotched brown, up to 25 cm long, which is probably quite common among weed beds. ### weeping tree
  
noun. <i>Rapanea montana </i>  
> Amongst smaller trees and shrubs the most prominent are the horoeka(<i>Panax crassifolia</i>), and its congeners <i>Panax simplex</i> and <i>P. edgerleyi</i>, both exhibiting such excessive luxuriance as frequently to require an examination of the fruit before their identification can be determined; the rautawhiri (<i>Pittosporum tenuifolium</i>, var. <i>colensoi</i>), the kotukutuku (<i>Fuchsia excorticata</i>), the mako (<i>Aristotelia racemosa</i>), the mapau (<i>Myrsine australis</i>), the pokako (<i>Eloeocarpus hookerianus</i>), also <i>Carpodetus serratus, Myrsine divaricata</i>, both of which are abundant. The remarkable `weeping tree', <i>Myrsine montana</i>, with its crowded recurved branches and small foliage, affords a picturesque effect *(1884,TrNZI,17/216)* [NOTES: (Kirk)]  
> **Allen** Myrsine montana [fam. Myrsinaceae]. Lowland to montane forest shrub up to 3m tall with dark red-brown bark; from lat. 39 S southwards; localised. *(1982,Flora NZ,I/542)*

noun. a breakfast cereal

It's just that I would feel more comfortable with steak-and-six eggs All Blacks or even liver-and-bacon-and-six-eggs All Blacks *(Aug 11, 1999,Evening Post,20)*  
> "She tried to integrate by eating Weetbix and refusing to talk Dutch." Hertoghs has dubbed it "the Weetbix mistake" - denying your own culture and habits for those of the country you have chosen to live in. He says it is exactly what themigrants of the 1950s did. *(Sep 12, 1998,Evening Post,8)*  
> Weetbix, produced since 1928, is the stalwart brand, commanding 45 percent of the ready-to-eat cereal market. *(Oct 14, 1999,Sunday Star Times,14)*

> To top it all, I haven't eaten Weet-bix in four years. *(Apr 25, 2004,Sunday Times (NZ),B12)*  
> ...Glen Colquhoun is the first New Zealand doctor-poet who was raised as a Seventh Day Adventist. So much so that his first job after leaving school was cooking Weet-bix in the church's Sanitarium factory... *(Feb 15, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),59)*  
> And there is simply no excuse, ever, for tiramisu made from Weet-bix. *(Jun 18, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),70)*

noun.

noun.

> Fairy prion, speckled skinks, yellow-crowned kakariki and flax weevils were relocated to Mana Island this year from the Marlborough Sounds as part of a plan to restore Mana Island's ecology. *(May 2004,Footnotes,No. 21/6)*

### weevil
  
noun.  
> Two biological weapons are about to be deployed against the destructive clover root weevil - a fungal disease and a parasitical wasp... The weevil was first discovered in Waikato in 1996 and is munching its way south at the rate of about 40 kilometres a year... Clover roof weevil is detectable by U-shaped notches on the leaves of the plant, made by the weevil adults feeding on them. *(Oct 28, 2004,Dominion Post,C7)*  
> Agricultural scientists battling the clover root weevil have imported a parasitic wasp they hope will help to control the damage the weevil is doing to pastures... Clover weevil was first noticed in 1996, and there is nothing to stop it from infesting the whole country as New Zealand's temperate climate and lack of predators offer ideal conditions for the weevil. *(May 27, 2004,Dominion Post,C5)*  
> The clover root weevil looms as the biggest threat to the future of Gwavas Station. *(Dec 2, 2004,Dominion Post,C7)*

### weita
  
noun. variant spelling of weta  
> The so-called mosquito was a "Weita" or "Ti-tree Sawyer" which is found at times in this district and is very common in Auckland and other provinces. It is called a scorpion by some~settlers, and is considered to be poisonous. *(Dec 23, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2)*

### weka
  
noun. a hen-sized, flightless bird. [Maori] <i>Gallirallus</i> spp., =woodhen; earlier =cassowary, =ground-pheasant, =maori hen, =water hen pl <i>wekas</i> early occas spg <i>waka, wika</i> early (SI) pron <i>[wi:ka] </i>Maori <i>weka</i>  
See: also woodhen  
> Mad as a weka should be part of the Kiwi lexicon. *(Feb 5, 2002,Evening Post,15)*  
> The more common term used on the island for the bird [sc. <i>Gallirallus australis</i>] was the one more often used in New Zealand : weka, which features in a poetic burst from a homesick islander in 1970 writing about indigenous treats on Macquarie: When you're tired of ancient eggs for brekker / How about toasting a tasty weka? *(Aug 2000,NZWords,No. 4 : 7)*  
> **BULLER** OCYDROMUS EARLI {GREYI}. (NORTH-ISLAND WOODHEN.) ... <i>Native name.</i>- Weka [in 1888 O earli is Brown woodhen] *(1873,Birds of New Zealand,165,1888 2nd edn 2/105)*  
> **HAAST** [The Maori judged] its value not by its extent but by the great number of Wekas (Wood hens) and Kakapos (Ground parrots) which ... had here enjoyed an undisturbed existence ... *(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,52)*  
> **BEATTIE** Weka - the familiar `woodhen' of Captain Cook, and `Maori hen' of the settlers. *(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,64)* [NOTES: c1880 South Island Maori Lists `Wahi Mahika Kai' in 1945 Beattie Maori Place-names of Canterbury 64]  
> **HAY** The Weka ... is found plentifully in the woods. Settlers call it the `bush-hen'. *(1882,Brighter Britain,2/219)*  
> **WALLACE** Another formidable enemy of this grub in New Zealand is the Wako [<i>sic.</i> ?= Weka], a dark-brown bird like a landrail, which is almost wingless and contains a large amount of oil, used by bushmen to make into candles. *(1891,Rural Economy of Aust. & NZ,52)*  
> **MORRIS** <i>Weka, n.</i> the Maori name for the <i>Wood-hen </i>(q.v.) of New Zealand, so called from its note. There are two species South Island Weka, or Wood-hen - <i>Ocydromus australis</i>, Strick. North-Island W. or W.-h - <i>Ocydromus brachypterus</i>, Buller. The specimens intergrade to such an extent that precise limitation of species is extremely difficult, but Sir W.L. Buller set them out as these two in 1878, regarding other specimens as varieties. The birds are sometimes called <i>Weka-Rails</i>, and the Maori name of <i>Weka-pango</i> is given to the<i> Black Wood-hen (O.fuscus, </i>Du Bus.). *(1898,Austral English,506)*  
> **PASCOE** The Weka or Maori Hen [<i>Gallirallus australis</i>]. *(1957,Mr Explorer Douglas,229)* [NOTES: c1899 Douglas Birds of South Westland in 1957 Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas 229]  
> **BAUGHAN** <i> Weka (wek-kah)</i>: The New Zealand wood-hen. *(1908,Shingle-Short,46)*  
> **BOOTH** We were much pestered here [near Wanaka] with weekas (woodhens)... *(1912,Five Years in NZ,90)*  
> **PFAFF** All night the More-pokes, Wekas (Maori hens), Kiwis, and, in summer, the Cuckoos, kept up their cries ... *(1914,Diggers' Story, The,11)*  
> The weka, or Maori-hen, as it was usually called (<i>Ocydromus australis</i>), another bird of the open country, which is now only met with occasionally in far-back regions, was plentiful and widely distributed throughout the province, ... *(1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,IV/277)* [NOTES: (Bathgate, Otago)]  
> **COWAN** The <i>weka</i> is a wingless bird, grey brown in colour, about the size of an ordinary fowl, and is often called the wood-hen, or `Maori-hen'. `As cunning as a Maori hen' is a familiar New Zealand bush simile. *(1926,Travel in NZ,Vol II South Island 63)*  
> <i>weka n</i>. the wood-hen; a wingless bird; the weka-rail. [Maori, imit. fr. its cry] *(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,12)*  
> **GRIEVE** On another occasion a ... bump-bump-bump in the vicinity of the cream-stand drew me as a  glittering object draws the curious <i>weka. </i> *(1939,Sketches from Maoriland,104,1961)*  
> **HOWARD** [Stewart Island birds include] the fern-bird (<i>Bowdleria fulva</i>), the bell-bird (<i>Anthornis melanura</i>), South Island Robin (<i>Miro Albifrons</i>), Tui (<i>Prosthemadera novae zealandiae</i>), Kingfisher (<i>Halcyon vagans</i>), Yellow-fronted parakeet (<i>Cyanorhampus auriceps</i>), Red-headed parrakeet (<i>Cyanorhampus novae zealandiae</i>), Kaka (<i>Nestor meridionalis</i>); and in the second class: Orange-wattled crow (<i>Glaucopis cinerea</i>), Blue Heron (<i>Demiegretta sacra</i>), Weka (<i>Ocydromus</i>) and Kiwi (<i>Apteryx</i>) *(1940,Rakiura,323)*  
> **HOWARD** [Stewart Island birds include] the fern-bird (<i>Bowdleria fulva</i>), the bell-bird (<i>Anthornis melanura</i>), South Island Robin (<i>Miro Albifrons</i>), Tui (<i>Prosthemadera novae zealandiae</i>), Kingfisher (<i>Halcyon vagans</i>), Yellow-fronted parakeet (<i>Cyanorhampus auriceps</i>), Red-headed parrakeet (<i>Cyanorhampus novae zealandiae</i>), Kaka (<i>Nestor meridionalis</i>); and in the second class: Orange-wattled crow (<i>Glaucopis cinerea</i>), Blue Heron (<i>Demiegretta sacra</i>), Weka (<i>Ocydromus</i>) and Kiwi (<i>Apteryx</i>) *(1940,Rakiura,323)*  
> <i>weka</i>, a ground-bird (Ocydromus sp.), wood-hen, Maori hen; it has wings, but too small for flight, but it can dodge and run well; and whilst its long toes are unwebbed it swims well, and voluntarily takes to the water to investigate distant isles in the great lakes: a most inquisitive and amusing bush-companion, and a courageous mother. *(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/147)* [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]  
> <i>weka</i>: woodhen *(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,176)*  
> <i>weka</i> The Maori name of a flightless bird, a kind of rail; also called `wood-hen'. -- *(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),442)*  
> **CURNOW** [`Pah-kay-hah Mah-o-ree'] I don't have much Trouble with Kiwi or Tui,/ But a Bird I call Weaker (or do I say Wecker?)/ Is as bad as that Place, Motchu-eeka (or -ecka?)./ Then I say Wy-t'marta, and not Wy-t'matter./ *(1959,Best of Whim-Wham, The,51)*  
> <i>weka n </i>-s [Maori] : any of several flightless New Zealand rails (genus <i>Gallirallus</i>, syn. <i>Ocydromus</i>) of thievish disposition having short wings each with a spur used in fighting - called also <i>Maori hen, wood hen </i> *(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,2594)*  
> <i>weka n </i>-s [Maori] : any of several flightless New Zealand rails (genus <i>Gallirallus</i>, syn. <i>Ocydromus</i>) of thievish disposition having short wings each with a spur used in fighting - called also <i>Maori hen, wood hen </i> *(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,2594)*  
> **FALLA et al.** WEKA <i>Gallirallus australis Other names</i>: Woodhen, Woodrail. *(1966,Birds of New Zealand,102)*  
> <i> weka n.</i> the New Zealand woodhen [Maori]. *(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),628)*  
> <i>weeka ... n.</i> N.Z. woodhen [Maori]. *(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),21)*  
> weka.. a nocturnal, flightless bird of New Zealand *(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,1336)*  
> **HULME** Weka = hensized bird with inordinate curiosity. Tastes good, too. *(1983,Bone People, The,469, 422)*  
> <i>Weka Gallirallus australis</i> (SPARRMAN, 1786) ... <i>North Island Weka G.a. greyi </i>BULLER, 1888). <i>Western Weka G.a. australis </i> (SPARRMAN, 1786). <i>  Weka G.a. hectori </i>(HUTTON, 1873). <i>Stewart Island Weka G.a. scotti </i>(OGILVIE-GRANT, 1905). OTHER NAMES: <i>Woodhen, woodrail. </i> *(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,168)*  
> We had the mortification of seeing three weka, or woodhens, stalking about, but unable to catch them, or we would have two days' provisions at least. *(Jun 8, 1860,Colonist, The,3)*  
> Commenting on the fact that the weka of Kapiti Island was not a pure-bred species, Mr. L. W. McCaskill pointed out during an address at the New Education Fellowship Conference at Christchurch that originally a weka was captured at Stewart Island. *(Oct 18, 1941,Evening Post,8)*  
> **MORELAND** Of wekas or Maori hens there were plenty. *(1911,Through South Westland,107)*  
> **MORELAND** Once a man had laid his set of teeth beside him on the grass, and a prowling weka snatched them up... They have curious eyes, red like rubies, and the wings are but weakly developed - they often remind me of a hen pheasant whose tail has been plucked out, and they can run quite as fast. *(1911,Through South Westland,108)*  
> **DAVIES** Wood-hens or waikas [sic], are a greatstand-by in the bush. Their cry can beimitated, and a man knowing theirlanguage and character can catch themeasily. They call each other by name,pronounced "weeka," the latter syllablebeing shrill and prolonged, an octavehigher than the first note. *(1884,Poems & Other Literary Remains,263)*  
> **SHORTLAND** We also heard the cry of the "weka". *(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,264)*  
> The Maori hen, or weka, is a most determined enemy of the bunny, and vigorously attacks and destroys both old and young. *(Jun 28, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,13)*  
> **LANCASTER** Finlay's pet weka was killing a rat all over the room. *(1904,Sons o' Men,174)*  
> **THOMSON** The plaintiff cry of the Maori-hen (Woka). *(1905,Bush Boys of NZ,170)*  
> Wekas are cute, aren't they?... A visiting British travel writer recently described them as stalking off "like an indignant Margaret Thatcher stepping through treacle". *(Jul 11, 2004,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),40)*  
> Weka, or Maori-hen, is undoubtedly a splendid enemy, but are  in most cases fast disappearing, being a ready prey to dogs, ferrets, &c. *(1896,Ann. Rep. Dept of Agriculture,IV/23)*  
> The meals at the pub are divine, if unusual - whole paua, satay crayfish with banana, boiled weka. *(Jun 26, 2004,Dominion Post,E2)*  
> The meals at the pub are divine, if unusual - whole paua, satay crayfish with banana, boiled weka. *(Jun 26, 2004,Dominion Post,E2)*  
> **LEE & KENDALL** W|eka, s. A large bird so called. *(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,227)*  
> ... the Chief of them, named Toowek (Woodhen) is feared by all; ...  <i>Ibid.</i> 109 woodhen - wek [weka] *(1835,Boultbee Journal,105,1986)* [NOTES: ? check proper name Toowek =? tu-weka Also 1826-28 Boultbee Vocabulary in 1979 Begg 282]  
> One [purchase] was a pair of Waka Wakas or Wood Hens. *(Oct 30, 1840,Journal of Ensign Best,257,1866)*  
> **SAM. STEPHENS** Weka, about the size of an English pheasant .. *(1842,Letters,60)*  
> **DIEFFENBACH** 55. <i>Ocydromus Australis.</i> Strickl. ... Weka or Weka-weka of the natives of Cook's Strait, Wood-hen of the Settlers. <i>Dieffenbach. </i> *(1843,Travels in NZ,2/197)* [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/197 55.]  
> **HEAPHY** The principal [newly-discovered species] is the <i>Weka</i>, an Apterix, termed by some of the settlers the `Ground Pheasant'. It is but litle larger than the partridge, which in some respects it resembles. *(1842,Narrative of a Residence in  Various Parts of NZ,47)*  
> **SELWYN** The natives added to our store by catching a parrot, five Tuis, and a Weka (a species of rail), living themselves upon fern root ... *(Nov 15, 1843,Journal)* [NOTES: 1843 Nov 15 Selwyn Journal in 1845 NZ Part II (Church in the Colonies VII) 49]  
> **DIEFFENBACH** We stopped for the night on a low island in the Waiwakaio, called Waiwiti, grown over with kahikatoa (Leptospermum), intermixed with a junceous plant, the Hamelinia veratroides of Achille Richard (Astelia Banksii), the seeds of which form the food of the kiwi and weka (Apterix Australis and Ral-[150]lus Australis). *(1843,Travels in NZ,1/149)*  
> **DIEFFENBACH** Wika, weka of the natives of Cook's Strait, wood-hen of the settlers ... *(1843,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/179)*  
> **DIEFFENBACH** Wika, weka of the natives of Cook's Strait, wood-hen of the settlers ... *(1843,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/179)*  
> **SHORTLAND** ... more or less convenient for catching eels or wekas; [fn] This bird is described by Captain Cook by the name of the water or woodhen. *(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,206)* [NOTES: 1844 Jan 13 in 1851 Shortland Southern Districts of NZ 206]  
> **WAKEFIELD**  *(1845,Adventure in NZ,ii. p. 95)*  
> **WAKEFIELD** <i>weka</i>, or wood-hen (<i>Ocydromus Australis</i>). This bird frequents the retired parts of the country, and especially the forests. <i>Ibid.</i> 247 <i>Quail</i> abound to a remarkable degree throughout the fern and grass districts. <i>Wild hogs</i>, also, and the <i>Wood-hen</i>, called <i>Weka</i> by the natives, ... are numerous ... <i>Ibid.<294> ... cassowary [fn] Woodhen or weka. *(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,163)*  
> ... one of the wekas brought on board ... Mr.Phillips' party bagged 20 wild duck - two wood hens and a small hawk ... *(May 1840,Acheron Journal)* [NOTES: 1850 May Acheron Journal in 1940 Howard Rakiura 391]  
> **HAAST** I soon learned ... that it was a weka (woodhen) which he had caught; ... *(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,13)*  
> I shot four blue duck, 4 pigeons and a caw-caw and a wecka. *(Dec 13, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,70,1950)*  
> **BARKER** Mr. L-- brought a large dog with him, a kangaroo-hound (not unlike a lurcher in appearance), to hunt the wekas. *(1865,Station Life in NZ,28,1870)*  
> **ANGAS** Another of the ... terrestrial birds of New Zealand ... [62] is the <i>Ocydromus Australis</i>, or `weka-weka' of the natives, a large species of rail, or wood-hen, ... *(1866,Polynesia,61)*  
> **PHILLIPS** ... shot 3 wekas and 13 rabbits and one quail hawk - *(Jul 10, 1867,Journal Kept at Rockwood and the Point,108)*  
> **HUTTON** 84. OCYDROMUS AUSTRALIS. Sparrm. Wood Hen. Weka. ... 85. OCYDROMUS EARLI. Gray. Wood Hen. Weka. *(1871,Birds of New Zealand,31)*

### weka
  
noun.

### weka
  
noun. a Chatham Islander  
> They [Chatham Islanders] call themselves Wekas, not Kiwis. *(Jun 26, 2004,Dominion Post,E1)*  
> **McGILL** <i>weka</i> a Chatham Islander; used by Islanders themselves, being proud of their weka population. *(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,121)*

### weka
  
noun. .  
[NOTES: call of the weka (from which it was named)]  
> **PYKE** `We-ka! we-ka! we-ka!' [a's and e's-acute] Three times the plaintive cry of the `wood-hen' was heard. *(1873,Wild Will Enderby,3/iii/82,(1889,1974))*  
> `Wait till I call him. We-ka-a!' *(1987,Happy Endings,104)* [NOTES: 1902 Lancaster Our Lady of the Plains in 1987 Happy Endings 104]  
> **DEMPSEY** We all heard the moreporks calling in the dark, `More <i> pork!</i>  More <i>  pork!'</i>  and once the shrill <i> `Wee </i> -ka, <i> wee </i> -ka,' of our camp followers. *(1964,Little World of Stewart island,74)*  
> "We-ka!   We-ka!" they would call to one another in the night, and so they were called Wekas. *(Feb 16, 1924,Evening Post,15)*

### Weka Pass stone
  
noun. Geol. definition  
> He had trained as a geologist, in Dunedin, and in the early part of his career wrote a number of papers, notably on the geology of Bluff and, with the late Professor R. Speight, on the stratigraphical relationship of the Weka Pass Stone and the Amuri Limestone. *(Dec 1973,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,36/14)*

### weka rail
  
noun. woodhen  
> **BULLER** The Weka Rail or Woodhen is one of the few New Zealand birds that already possess a literature. *(1873,Birds of New Zealand,166)*  
> **MORRIS** <i>Weka-rail, n.</i> See <i>Weka. </i> *(1898,Austral English,507)*  
> **HUNT** A lot of old weka-eaters would still like to cook up a few ... I could live on weka stew myself, just like it says in one of our old songs: <i>What would a Maori do/ For a good old weka stew. </i> *(1985,I'm Ninety-five,26)* [NOTES: (Wattie, Stewart I.)]

### weka stew
  
noun.  
> Another weka stew graced the festive board at dinner-time that day, but as the repast too frequently resolved itself into a vulgar struggle between the repasters and the defunct birds for the possession of a gutta-percha-like fragment of flesh tied on to a leg bone, the incidents of the orgie [sic] are subjects scarcely meet for publications among these disjointed memoirs. *(Mar 17, 1896,Mataura Ensign,2)*

### weka-berry
  
noun. <i>Gaultheria</i> spp., =??snowberry  
> **HUTCHINSON** [In the Ruahine range] shrubby Gaultherias (weka-berry and snow-berry). *(1947,At Omatua's Fireside,13)*

### weka-eater
  
noun.  
> **HUNT** A lot of old weka-eaters would still like to cook up a few ... I could live on weka stew myself, just like it says in one of our old songs: <i>What would a Maori do/ For a good old weka stew. </i> *(1985,I'm Ninety-five,261)*

### weka-hunting
  
noun.  
[NOTES: aka weka hunting]  
> proving, in the Native Land Court, the tribal title to this country, ... I was always glad to fall back upon evidence of Weka-hunting within the disputed boundaries, as affording proof of ancient title. *(1888,Birds of New Zealand,2/109)*  
> Then we had kiwi and weka hunting, pigeon and kaka snaring and spearing, eel spearing, and fish spearing by torch light on the sandy and muddy flats of our rivers. *(Dec 31, 1883,Wanganui Herald,2)*

### weka-oil
  
noun.  
[NOTES: aka weka oil]  
> I dressed my other scratches with weka oil. *(Oct 28, 1848,New Zealand Spectator & Cook's Strait Guardian,3)*  
> **BARKER** [I] equipped myself in a huge pair of ... riding-boots made of kangaroo-skin, well greased with weka-oil to keep the wet out. *(1867,Station Life in NZ,166,1870)*  
> **DEMPSEY** Could I obtain, from a muton-bird island, some weka oil for an old lady who was a great believer in this oil for rheumatism? *(1964,Little World of Stewart island,49)*  
> There was a theory, which was widely accepted, that "weka-oil" cured rheumatism. *(Jul 25, 1908,Evening Post,13)*

### weka-pango
  
noun.

noun.
